Manin Matrices for Quadratic Algebras
Alexey SILANTYEV ab

Abstract
We give a general definition of Manin matrices for arbitrary quadratic algebras in terms of idempotents. We establish their main properties and give their interpretation in terms of the category theory. The notion of minors is generalised for a general Manin matrix. We give some examples of Manin matrices, their relations with Lax operators and obtain the formulae for some minors. In particular, we consider Manin matrices of the types B B , C C and D D introduced by A. Molev and their relation with Brauer algebras. Infinite-dimensional Manin matrices and their connection with Lax operators are also considered.



keywords
Manin matrices; quantum groups; quadratic algebras

AMS
16T99; 15B99

1
Introduction

In the second half of the 80s Yuri Manin proposed to consider non-commutative quadratic algebras as a generalisation of vector spaces and called them “quantum linear spaces”
[
23
,
24
,
26
]
. A usual finite-dimensional vector space is presented by the algebra of polynomials. The linear maps correspond to the homomorphisms of these algebras in the category of graded algebras. One can consider homomorphisms of a quadratic algebra over a non-commutative ring (algebra). Such “non-commutative” homomorphisms of finitely generated quadratic algebras (“finite-dimensional” quantum linear spaces) are described by matrices with non-commutative entries satisfying some commutation relations. We call them Manin matrices.

The main example proposed by Manin is the “quantum plane” defined by the algebra with
2
generators
x
,
y
and the commutation relation
yx=qxy
. He established the connection of the quantum plane with some quantum group (Hopf algebra). This quantum group gives a “non-commutative” endomorphism of the quantum plane.

This picture was also generalised to the case of
n
generators with the commutation relations
x_{j}x_{i}=qx_{i}x_{j}
,
i<j
. The algebra describing all the “non-commutative” endomorphisms of the corresponding quantum linear space was called right quantum algebra in
[
10
]
, where the authors proved a
q
-analogue of MacMahon master theorem. The “non-commutative” endomorphisms and homomorphisms of these quadratic algebras are described by square and rectangular matrices respectively. We call them
q
-Manin matrices.

‘Non-commutative’ endomorphisms and homomorphisms of a usual finite-dimensional vector space generalise the matrices with commutative entries to the matrices with non-commutative entries satisfying certain commutation relations (these are the commutation relations of the right quantum algebra for
q=1
). This type of matrices were observed in the Talalaev’s formula
[
35
]
and called then Manin matrices
[
4
]
. These Manin matrices have a lot of applications to integrable systems, Sugawara operators, affine Lie algebras and quantum groups
[
4
,
7
,
34
]
. Since they are an immediate generalisation of the usual matrices to the non-commutative case, almost all the formulae of the matrix theory are valid for these Manin matrices
[
5
]
. Most of them are also generalised to the
q
-Manin matrices
[
6
]
.

The “non-commutative” homomorphisms between two quantum linear spaces are described by an algebra that can be constructed as internal hom
[
24
]
. These “non-commutative” homomorphisms give us a notion of a Manin matrix for a pair of finitely generated quadratic algebras
(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B})
. These are the matrices with non-commutative entries satisfying the commutation relations of the internal hom algebra. In previous works on Manin matrices the attention was concentrated on the case
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{B}
corresponding to the “non-commutative” endomorphisms. This general case allows us to consider more examples and answer some questions about
q
-Manin matrices, which was unclear before.

For instance, the column determinant is a natural generalisation of the usual determinant for the
q=1
Manin matrices, but some properties of this determinant are proved by using a part of commutation relations for the entries of these matrices. We show here that this part of commutation relations define Manin matrices for some pair of different quadratic algebras.

In the case
q\neq 1
the column determinant is generalised to (column)
q
-determinant. It is a natural generalisation for
q
-Manin matrices. Permutation of columns of
q
-Manin matrices gives a matrix, which is not a
q
-Manin matrix. However its natural analogue of the determinant is also
q
-determinant. In contrast, the
q
-determinant is not relevant operation for a
q
-Manin matrix with permuted rows. To consider the permuted
q
-Manin matrices as another type of Manin matrices and to define natural determinants for them one needs to consider some pairs of different quadratic algebras. It is convenient to do it in a more universal case when the role of quadratic algebras is played by multi-parametric deformations of the polynomial algebras.

A multi-parametric deformation of super-vector spaces was considered by Manin in the article
[
25
]
. The super-versions of Manin matrices and MacMahon master theorem for the non-deformed case were also considered in
[
30
]
. Here we do not consider the super-case, but we plan to do it in future works.

The notion of Manin matrices for two different quadratic algebras includes Manin matrices of types
B
,
C
and
D
introduced by Molev in
[
28
]
.

In this work we use tensor notations to describe quadratic algebras and Manin matrices by generalising the tensor approach given in
[
5
,
6
]
. In this frame a quadratic algebra is defined by an idempotent that gives commutation relations for this algebra. E.g., the commutative algebra of polynomials is defined by the anti-symmetrizer of the tensor product of two copies of a vector space. Two different idempotents may define the same quadratic algebra. This gives an equivalence relation between the idempotents.

The relations for Manin matrices can be written in tensor notations with the corresponding idempotents. In the case of two different quadratic algebras these commutation relations are given by a pair of idempotents
A
and
B
, so we call the matrices, satisfying these relations,
(A,B)
-Manin matrices. In the case
A=B
we call them
A
-Manin matrices.

The important notion in the matrix theory is the notion of minor, which is usually defined as a determinant of a square submatrix. The minors (defined via column determinant) play a role of decomposition coefficients in the expression for a “non-commutative” homomorphism of the anti-commutative polynomial algebras (Grassmann algebras). The dual notion is permanent of square submatrices (where some rows and columns may be repeated) that give coefficients in the case of commutative polynomial algebras.

This is directly generalised to some kinds of Manin matrices including the
q
-Manin matrices and the multi-parametric case. However in the general situation an analogue of minors is not defined by an operation (determinant or permanent) on submatrices. The minors are defined immediately without such operations. To do it we introduce some auxiliary “dual” quadratic algebras for a given idempotent and define a non-degenerate pairing between quadratic algebras (if it exists). In the tensor notation this pairing is written by using some higher idempotents, which we call pairing operators. These operators allow to define minors as entries of some operators with non-commutative entries, we call them minor operators. For the main examples the pairing operators is related with the representation theory of the symmetric groups, Hecke algebras and Brauer algebras.

The paper is organised as follows.

In Section
2
we consider quadratic algebras and related Manin matrices. In Section
2.1
we consider the quadratic algebras in terms of idempotents. Section
2.2
is devoted to the equivalence relations between idempotents. In Sections
2.3
and
2.4
we define
A
- and
(A,B)
-Manin matrices in terms of matrix commutation relations and in terms of quadratic algebras, we give the main properties of these matrices. In Section
2.5
we interpret Manin matrices in terms of comma categories and define a general right quantum algebra via adjoint functors. Section
2.6
is devoted to multiplication of Manin matrices and to bialgebra structure on the right quantum algebra. A generalisation of Manin matrices to the infinite-dimensional case was done in Section
2.7
.

In Section
3
we consider the following particular cases: the Manin matrices of
[
4
,
5
]
,
q
-Manin matrices
[
6
]
and multi-parametric case
[
25
]
(the Sections
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
respectively). We recall basic properties of these Manin matrices and their determinants. In Section
3.4
we consider a generalisation of the
n=3
case from Section
3.3
.

Section
4
is devoted to relationship between Manin matrices and Lax operators. In Section
4.1
we explain the known connection of
q
-Manin matrices with some Lax operators (without spectral parameter) via a decomposition of an
R
-matrix into idempotents. This gives an important example of the idempotent equivalence. In Section
4.2
we interpret Lax operators (with a spectral parameter) for the rational
R
-matrix as infinite-dimensional Manin matrices.

In Section
5
we generalise the notion of minors for Manin matrices. Section
5.1
has a motivating role, where we consider minors for
q
-Manin matrices (defined via determinant and permanent) as some decomposition coefficients. In Sections
5.2
and
5.3
we define “dual” quadratic algebras and the corresponding parings via pairing operators. In Sections
5.4
and
5.5
we define minors as entries of minor operators and give some properties of these operators. In Section
5.6
we investigate the relation of pairing and minor operators for equivalent idempotents. Section
5.7
devoted to an algebraic approach to construction of pairing operators.

We consider examples of pairing and minor operators in Section
6
. Section
6.1
is devoted to the multi-parametric case (which includes the case of
q
-Manin matrices). In Section
6.2
we again consider the case of
q
-Manin matrices, but we construct the pairing operators by using another idempotent (equivalent to the idempotent used in Section
6.1
), which was found in Section
4.1
. We show how they give related minor operators. Section
6.3
is devoted to the case defined in Section
3.4
.

Section
7
is devoted to the Manin matrices of types
B
,
C
and
D
. In Section
7.1
we recall the Molev’s definition of these matrices and interpret them as Manin matrices for pairs of idempotents. We consider the corresponding quadratic algebras. In Section
7.2
we construct the pairing operators by means of Brauer algebra.

In Appendix
A
we give a formulae for a set of inversions in the case of arbitrary Weyl group. In Appendix
B
the universal enveloping algebras of Lie algebras are represented as quadratic algebras.

2
Quadratic algebras and Manin matrices

As a basic field we choose the field of complex numbers
{\mathbb{C}}
, however we note that all the statements are valid for any field of characteristic zero, unless the contrary is explicitly indicated. All the vector spaces are supposed to be defined over
{\mathbb{C}}
. By an
algebra
we understand an associative unital algebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
(not necessary commutative). By a
graded algebra
we mean an
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
-graded associative unital algebra over
{\mathbb{C}}
, where
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
is the set of non-negative integers: the grading of such algebra has the form
{\mathfrak{A}}=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathfrak{A}}_{k}
and the condition
{\mathfrak{A}}_{k}{\mathfrak{A}}_{l}\subset{\mathfrak{A}}_{k+l}
is implied for all
k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. By a
quadratic algebra
1
1

1

There are more general quadratic algebras over
{\mathfrak{R}}
, but we do not consider them in this work.

we mean a graded algebra of the form
{\mathfrak{A}}={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes(TV/I)
, where
{\mathfrak{R}}
is an arbitrary algebra,
TV=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}V^{\otimes k}={\mathbb{C}}\oplus V\oplus(V\otimes V)\oplus\cdots
is the tensor algebra of a finite-dimensional vector space
V
and
I\subset TV
is its (two-sided) ideal generated by a subspace of
V\otimes V
; in particular,
{\mathfrak{A}}_{0}={\mathfrak{R}}
,
{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
.

Let
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be the space of complex column vectors of the size
n
. Its dual
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n},{\mathbb{C}})
is the space of complex row vectors. Their standard bases
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and
(e^{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are dual to each other:
e^{i}e_{j}=\delta^{i}_{j}
. Let
E_{i}{}^{j}
be the
n\times m
matrices with entries
\big(E_{i}{}^{j}\big)_{kl}=\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{j}_{l}
. It acts on
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
from the left and on
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}
from the right as
E_{i}{}^{j}e_{k}=\delta^{j}_{k}e_{i}
,
e^{i}E_{j}{}^{k}=\delta^{i}_{j}e^{k}
. We have
E_{i}{}^{j}=e_{i}e^{j}
.

We use the following tensor notations. Let
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be an
n\times m
matrix over an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
. It can be considered as an operator from
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
with entries
M_{ij}=M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
, that is
M=\sum_{i,j}M^{i}_{j}E_{i}{}^{j}
. Introduce the notation

\displaystyle M^{(a)}=\sum_{i,j}M^{i}_{j}(1\otimes\cdots\otimes 1\otimes E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes 1\otimes\cdots\otimes 1),

where
E_{i}{}^{j}
is placed to the
a
-th site and
1
are identity matrices (of different sizes in general). This is an operator from
{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{r}}
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{r}}
, where
m_{a}=m
,
n_{a}=n
and
m_{b}=n_{b}
for all
b\neq a
. Analogously, for a matrix
T=\sum_{i,j,k,l}T^{ij}_{kl}E_{i}{}^{k}\otimes E_{j}{}^{l}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m^{\prime}},{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n^{\prime}}\big)
with entries
T^{ij}_{kl}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
we denote

\displaystyle T^{(ab)}=\sum_{i,j,k,l}T^{ij}_{kl}\big(1\otimes\cdots\otimes 1\otimes E_{i}{}^{k}\otimes 1\otimes\cdots\otimes 1\otimes E_{j}{}^{l}\otimes 1\otimes\cdots\otimes 1\big),

with
E_{i}{}^{k}
in the
a
-th site and
E_{j}{}^{l}
in the
b
-th site. The numbers
a
and
b
should be different but the both cases
a<b
and
a>b
are possible. In particular,

\displaystyle T^{(21)}=\sum_{i,j,k,l}T^{ij}_{kl}\big(E_{j}{}^{l}\otimes E_{i}{}^{k}\big).

In the same way the notations
M^{(a)}
and
T^{(ab)}
can be defined for any vector spaces
V
,
W
,
V^{\prime}
,
W^{\prime}
and any operators
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
,
T\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime})
(in the infinite-dimensional case the tensor product with
{\mathfrak{R}}
may be completed in some way).

2.1
Quadratic algebras

Consider a quadratic algebra with
n
generators, that is an algebra generated by the elements
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
with some quadratic commutation relations. Since the number of elements
x^{i}x^{j}
is equal to
n^{2}
the number of independent quadratic relations is less or equal to
n^{2}
. It means that these relations can be presented in the form

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}A^{kl}_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}=0,\qquad k,l=1,\dots,n,

(2.1)

where
A^{kl}_{ij}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. The quadratic algebra with these relations is the quotient
TV/I
, where
V=({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}
and
I
is the ideal generated by the elements
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}A^{kl}_{ij}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}
. An element
x^{i}\in TV/I
is the class of
e^{i}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}\subset T({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}
.

The coefficients
A^{kl}_{ij}
can be considered as entries of a matrix
A
acting on
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. In terms of basis this action looks as follows:
A(e_{i}\otimes e_{j})=\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}A^{kl}_{ij}(e_{k}\otimes e_{l})
. Note that for any invertible
n^{2}\times n^{2}
matrix
G
, the product
GA
defines the same quadratic algebra, since the relations
\sum_{ij=1}^{n}(GA)^{kl}_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}=0
is equivalent to (
2.1
).

Proposition 2.1
.

For each quadratic algebra the matrix
A
defining its relations can be chosen to be an idempotent:

\displaystyle A^{2}=A.

(2.2)

Proof.

Let the given quadratic algebra be defined by the relations with a matrix
A
. If one has proved that there exists an invertible
G\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
such that
(GA)^{2}=GA
then we can choose
GA
as an idempotent matrix defining the quadratic relations for this algebra. Since
G
should be invertible the equation
GAGA=GA
is equivalent to
AGA=A
. Let us reduce the matrix
A
to a Jordan form. In the corresponding basis of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
it takes the form

\displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\alpha_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\alpha_{r}\end{pmatrix}\!,

where
\alpha_{k}
are Jordan cells. Let us find the solution in the form (in the same basis)

\displaystyle G=\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\beta_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\beta_{r}\end{pmatrix}\!,

where each
\beta_{k}
is an invertible square submatrix, which has the same dimension as the Jordan cell
\alpha_{k}
. Then, the equation
AGA=A
is equivalent to system of
r
equations
\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k}
. If the Jordan cell
\alpha_{k}
corresponds to the non-zero eigenvalue of the matrix
A
, then the matrix
\alpha
is invertible and we can choose
\beta_{k}=\alpha_{k}^{-1}
. Otherwise,
\alpha_{k}
has the form

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&1&\cdots&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}

and
\beta_{k}
can be chosen as

\displaystyle\beta_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\cdots&0&1\\
1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
0&1&\cdots&0&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
0&0&\cdots&1&0\end{pmatrix}\!.

For
\alpha_{k}=0
we choose
\beta_{k}=1
. In all the cases such chosen matrices
\beta_{k}
are invertible. ∎

Remark 2.2
.

This construction of an idempotent
A
is appropriate for algebraically closed fields only. In general case one needs to consider decomposition of the vector space
({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}
into a direct sum
R\oplus R_{c}
such that
R
is the subspace spanned by the quadratic relations. Then the matrix
A
is the transposed to the idempotent projecting
({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}
onto
R
in parallel to the complementary subspace
R_{c}
.

Henceforth we suppose that the matrix
A
is an idempotent unless otherwise specified. Denote the corresponding quadratic algebra by
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
.

More generally, for an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
define the quadratic algebra
{{\mathfrak{X}}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{X}}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. This is a graded algebra generated by the elements
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
over
{\mathfrak{R}}
with the quadratic commutation relations (
2.1
) and
rx^{i}=x^{i}r
,
r\in{\mathfrak{R}}
,
i=1,\dots,n
. The formulae
\deg x^{i}=1
,
\deg r=0

\;\forall\,r\in{\mathfrak{R}}
give the grading on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
. An idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
defines a functor
{{\mathfrak{X}}}_{A}
from the category of algebras to the category of graded algebras:
{{\mathfrak{X}}}_{A}(f)(rx^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}})=f(r)x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}}
,
\forall f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{R}},{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
,
\forall r\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. Due to Proposition
2.1
any quadratic algebra is isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
for some
A
and
{\mathfrak{R}}
.

The relations (
2.1
) can be rewritten in matrix notations as follows. Consider the column vector
X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{i}e_{i}
. Then the relations (
2.1
) takes the form

\displaystyle A(X\otimes X)=0,

(2.3)

where
X\otimes X=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}x^{i}x^{j}(e_{i}\otimes e_{j})
.

The fact that there is no another independent relations for
x^{i}
besides (
2.1
) can be reformulated as follows: if
T_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}=0
for some
T_{ij}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
then
T_{ij}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}G_{kl}A^{kl}_{ij}
for some
G_{kl}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The following equations for a matrix
T\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
are equivalent:

\displaystyle T(X\otimes X)=0,

(2.4)

\displaystyle T(1-A)=0.

(2.5)

Proof.

The equation (
2.4
) is derived from (
2.5
) by multiplying by
(X\otimes X)
from the right. Conversely, if the equation (
2.4
) holds then we have
n^{2}
relations
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}T^{ab}_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}=0
enumerated by
a,b=1,\dots,n
, where
T^{ab}_{ij}
is entries of the matrix
T
. This implies
T^{ab}_{ij}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}G^{ab}_{kl}A^{kl}_{ij}
for some matrix
G=(G^{ab}_{kl})
that is
T=GA
. By multiplying the last equality by
(1-A)
on the right and by taking into account (
2.2
) one yields (
2.5
).
∎

Let us consider the “change of variables”
y^{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}M^{i}_{k}x^{k}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, where
x^{1},\dots,x^{m}
are the generators of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. In general, the transition matrix
M
is an
n\times m
matrix with non-commutative entries
M^{i}_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
, so that
y^{i}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
. In terms of
Y=\sum_{i=1}^{n}y^{i}e_{i}
we have

\displaystyle Y=MX.

(2.6)

Lemma 2.4
.

The equation

\displaystyle A(Y\otimes Y)=0

(2.7)

is equivalent to

\displaystyle AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}(1-A)=0.

(2.8)

Proof.

Note that the generators
x^{i}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
commute with
M^{k}_{l}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
as elements of algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. Then by substituting (
2.6
) to (
2.7
) we obtain
AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}(X\otimes X)=0
, which in turn is equivalent to (
2.8
) by Lemma
2.3
. ∎

Consider the quadratic algebra
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
generated by the elements
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
over
{\mathfrak{R}}
with the commutation relations

\displaystyle\psi_{i}\psi_{j}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}A^{kl}_{ij}\psi_{k}\psi_{l}

(2.9)

and
r\psi_{i}=\psi_{i}r
,
r\in{\mathfrak{R}}
.
Each idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
defines a functor
\Xi_{A}
from the category of algebras to the category of graded algebras:
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. Consider the row vector
\Psi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}e^{i}=(\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n})
. Then the relations (
2.9
) take the form

\displaystyle(\Psi\otimes\Psi)(1-A)=0.

(2.10)

Remark 2.5
.

If
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
is a Koszul algebra, then the algebra
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
is its Koszul dual algebra.

We will use the following conventions. We write
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
iff there exists an algebra isomorphism
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
identical on generators, that is
x^{i}\mapsto x^{i}

\forall i
and
r\mapsto r

\forall r\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. We write
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})=\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
iff there exists an isomorphism
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
identical on generators
\psi^{i}
and
r\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. We also write
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
iff there exists an isomorphism
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
such that
\psi_{i}\mapsto x^{i}

\forall i
and
r\mapsto r

\forall r\in{\mathfrak{R}}
.

Remark 2.6
.

We have
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
,
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})=\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
or
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
iff
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
,
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
or
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
respectively. The latter equalities are exactly the isomorphisms of quadratic algebras
TV/I
as
TV
-modules (left, right of two-sided), where in the case
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
we identify
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
with
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}
via
e_{i}\mapsto e^{i}
.

Note that
S=1-A
is an idempotent iff
A
is idempotent. The transformation
S\to SG
by an invertible matrix
G\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
does not change the relation (
2.10
). By transposing the relation (
2.10
) we see that it is equivalent to the relation (
2.3
) with
X
replaced with
\Psi^{\top}
and
A
replaced with
S^{\top}=1-A^{\top}
, where
(-)^{\top}
means the matrix transposition. Hence
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{S^{\top}}({\mathfrak{R}})
the functor
\Xi_{A}
can be identified with the functor
{{\mathfrak{X}}}_{S^{\top}}={{\mathfrak{X}}}_{1-A^{\top}}
.

2.2
Left and right equivalence of idempotents

A fixed quadratic algebra can be defined by different idempotents. Here we give a condition when it happens. To do it we introduce two equivalence relations for idempotent endomorphisms acting on a vector space. We prove some useful properties of these idempotents and their equivalence relations.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional vector space (here we use that the field
{\mathbb{C}}
is algebraically closed). Note that the Jordan form of an idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
in an appropriate basis of
V
is a diagonal matrix
\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\nolimits(1,\dots,1,0,\dots,0)
. It can be written in the block form as

\displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

(2.11)

with the blocks of the sizes
r\times r
,
r\times d
,
d\times r
,
d\times d
, where
r
is the rank of
A
and
d=\dim V-r
. We obtain the first property of the idempotents.

Proposition 2.7
.

The rank of an idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
equals to its trace:
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits A
.

Let us say that two idempotents
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
are
left-equivalent
(to each other) if there exists an operator
G\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(V)
such that
A^{\prime}=GA
, and we call them
right-equivalent
if there exists
G\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(V)
such that
A^{\prime}=AG
. The both relations are true equivalence relations.

Proposition 2.8
.

Two idempotents
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
are left-equivalent iff the dual idempotents
S=1-A
and
S^{\prime}=1-A^{\prime}
are right-equivalent.

Proof.

Let us fix a basis of
V
such that the idempotent
A
has the form (
2.11
). Suppose that
A
is left-equivalent to
A^{\prime}
, then there exists an invertible matrix
G
such that
A^{\prime}=GA
. The matrices
G
and
A^{\prime}
have the form

\displaystyle G=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}\!,\qquad A^{\prime}=GA=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\
\gamma&0\end{pmatrix}\!,

where
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
and
\delta
are
d\times d
,
d\times\ell
,
\ell\times d
and
\ell\times\ell
matrices respectively. The idempotentness of
A^{\prime}
implies
AA^{\prime}=A
, that is
\alpha=1
. Hence
A^{\prime}=A_{\gamma}
, where
A_{\gamma}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
\gamma&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Since
S=1-A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
S^{\prime}=1-A^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
-\gamma&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
we have
S^{\prime}=SG_{-\gamma}
, where
G_{\gamma}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
\gamma&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Further, note that two idempotents
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are right-equivalent iff the idempotents
S_{1}^{\top}
and
S_{2}^{\top}
are left-equivalent. Thus the right equivalence of
S
and
S^{\prime}
implies the left equivalence of
A=1-S
and
A^{\prime}=1-S^{\prime}
. ∎

Remark 2.9
.

We see from the proof that any matrix left-equivalent to (
2.11
) has the form
A_{\gamma}=G_{\gamma}A
. Analogously, any matrix right-equivalent to
S=1-A
has the form
S_{\gamma}=1-A_{\gamma}=SG_{-\gamma}
.

Proposition 2.10
.

If two idempotents
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
are right-equivalent then
SV=S^{\prime}V
.
If two idempotents
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
are left-equivalent then
V^{*}A=V^{*}A^{\prime}
,
where
V^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,{\mathbb{C}})
.

Proof.

If
G\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(V)
then
GV=V
, so from
S^{\prime}=SG
one yields
S^{\prime}V=SGV=SV
. The second statement follows from the first one for
S=A^{\top}
,
S^{\prime}=(A^{\prime})^{\top}
. ∎

Lemma 2.11
.

If two idempotents
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
satisfy the relations
A^{\prime}(1-A)=0
and
A(1-A^{\prime})=0
then they are left-equivalent.

Proof.

By substituting (
2.11
) and
A^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
to these relations we obtain
\beta=0
,
\delta=0
and
\alpha=1
, so that
A^{\prime}=A_{\gamma}=G_{\gamma}A
(see the proof of Proposition
2.8
). ∎

Consider the case
V={\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Recall that any idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
defines quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be idempotents. Then the following conditions are equivalent.

(a)

A
is left-equivalent to
A^{\prime}
.

(b)

S=1-A
is right-equivalent to
S^{\prime}=1-A^{\prime}
.

(c)

{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
.

(d)

\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
.

Proof.

The conditions
(a)
and
(b)
are equivalent due to Proposition
2.8
. Two left-equivalent idempotents
A
and
A^{\prime}
define the same commutation relations (
2.3
). This means that
(a)
implies
(c)
. Let us prove the converse implication. If
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
then
A^{\prime}(X\otimes X)=0
and
A(X\otimes X)=0
. By Lemma
2.3
we obtain the relations
A^{\prime}(1-A)=0
and
A(1-A^{\prime})=0
. Due to Lemma
2.11
these relations imply the left equivalence of
A
and
A^{\prime}
. Since
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{S^{\top}}({\mathbb{C}})
the equivalence of
(b)
and
(d)
is derived by considering the transposed matrices. ∎

Remark 2.13
.

Note that the vanishing of
A^{\prime}(1-A)
and
A(1-A^{\prime})
is equivalent to
V^{*}A=V^{*}A^{\prime}
. Due to Proposition
2.8
and Lemma
2.11
can be reformulated in the following way: idempotents
A
and
A^{\prime}
are left equivalent iff
V^{*}A=V^{*}A^{\prime}
.
Analogously, idempotents
S
and
S^{\prime}
are right equivalent iff
SV=S^{\prime}V
.
For the case
V={\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
we can add two more equivalent conditions to the list of Proposition
2.12
:

(e)

({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}A=({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}A^{\prime}
and

(f)

S({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})=S^{\prime}({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
.

Thus, the choice of an idempotent
A
from a class of the left equivalence corresponds to the choice of complementary subspace
R_{c}
in the decomposition
V^{*}=R\oplus R_{c}
, where
R
is the fixed subspace defining this class.
Note also that all the statements of this subsection are valid for any field
{\mathbb{C}}
.

2.3
\boldsymbol{A}
-Manin matrices

Definition 2.14
.

An
n\times n
matrix
M
over some algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
satisfying the equation

\displaystyle AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}(1-A)=0

(2.12)

is called
Manin matrix corresponding to the idempotent
A
or simply
A
-Manin matrix
.

The definition (
2.12
) can be rewritten in the following form

\displaystyle AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}

\displaystyle=AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}A.

(2.13)

This relation means that the expression
AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}
is invariant with respect to multiplication by
A
from the right.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
M
be an
n\times n
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Let
x^{i}
and
\psi_{i}
be the generators of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
respectively. Consider the elements
y^{i}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
and
\phi_{i}\in\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}y^{1}\\
\cdots\\
y^{n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}M^{1}_{1}&\cdots&M^{1}_{n}\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
M^{n}_{1}&\cdots&M^{n}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x^{1}\\
\cdots\\
x^{n}\end{pmatrix}\!,

(2.14)

\displaystyle(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n})=(\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n})\begin{pmatrix}M^{1}_{1}&\cdots&M^{1}_{n}\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
M^{n}_{1}&\cdots&M^{n}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\!.

(2.15)

Then the following three conditions are equivalent:

•

The matrix
M
is an
A
-Manin matrix.

•

The elements
y^{i}
satisfy the relations (
2.1
), i.e.,
A(Y\otimes Y)=0
, where
Y=\sum_{i=1}^{n}y^{i}e_{i}
.

•

The elements
\phi_{i}
satisfy the relations (
2.9
), i.e.,
(\Phi\otimes\Phi)(1-A)=0
, where
\Phi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}e^{i}
.

Proof.

Note that the formulae (
2.14
) and (
2.15
) have the form
Y=MX
and
\Phi=\Psi M
. The second condition is equivalent to the first one due to Lemma
2.4
. The third condition can be written as
\big(1-A^{\top}\big)\big(\Phi^{\top}\otimes\Phi^{\top}\big)=0
. Since the operator
1-A^{\top}
is also idempotent we can again apply Lemma
2.4
, so the third condition is equivalent to the equation

\displaystyle\big(1-A^{\top}\big)\big(M^{\top}\big)^{(1)}\big(M^{\top}\big)^{(2)}A^{\top}=0.

Transposition of this equation and the formula
\big(\big(M^{\top}\big)^{(1)}\big(M^{\top}\big)^{(2)}\big)^{\top}=M^{(1)}M^{(2)}
gives exactly (
2.12
), which is the first condition. ∎

Note that the relation (
2.12
) has the form
AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}S=0
, where
S=1-A
. It is equivalent to

\displaystyle M^{(1)}M^{(2)}S=SM^{(1)}M^{(2)}S.

(2.16)

Let
P=1-2A
. Then
P^{2}=1
. We have
A=\frac{1-P}{2}
and
S=\frac{1+P}{2}
. The idempotents
A
and
S
are often given by the matrix
P
satisfying
P^{2}=1
. The relation (
2.12
) in terms of
P
takes the form

\displaystyle M^{(1)}M^{(2)}-PM^{(1)}M^{(2)}+M^{(1)}M^{(2)}P-PM^{(1)}M^{(2)}P=0.

(2.17)

Remark 2.16
.

The notations
A
,
S
,
P
and the sign of
P
is chosen according to the basic case: the algebra of commutative polynomials. In this case the roles of
A
,
S
and
P
are played by antisymmetrizer, symmetrizer and permutation matrix respectively. See Section
3.1
for details.

Consider some trivial examples. For
A=0
we have
P=S=1
. The algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathbb{C}}\langle x^{1},\dots,x^{n}\rangle
is the algebra of all the non-commutative polynomials of the variables
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
(without any relations). The algebra
\Xi_{0}({\mathbb{C}})
is defined by the relations
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}=0
,
i,j=1,\dots,n
. As a linear space it has the from
\Xi_{0}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathbb{C}}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\psi_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\psi_{n}
. For
A=1
we have
P=-1
,
S=0
,
{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathbb{C}}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}x^{1}\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathbb{C}}x^{n}\cong\Xi_{0}({\mathbb{C}})
,
\Xi_{1}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathbb{C}}\langle\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}\rangle\cong{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}({\mathbb{C}})
. In the both cases the relation defining Manin matrices has the form
0=0
, so that any
n\times n
matrix
M
is a Manin matrix for the idempotent
A=0
as well as for the idempotent
A=1
.

Due to Propositions
2.12
and
2.15
the notion of
A
-Manin matrix does not change if we substitute
A
by a left-equivalent idempotent
A^{\prime}
. This means that
M
is an
A
-Manin matrix iff it is an
A^{\prime}
-Manin matrix. This implies that
A
-Manin matrices are associated to the quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
(with fixed generators).

More generally, the definition of
A
-Manin matrix can be written in the form

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}(1-A^{\prime})=0,

where
\widetilde{A}=\widetilde{G}A
,
A^{\prime}=GA
and
\widetilde{G},G\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
are such that
A^{\prime}
is an idempotent (i.e.,
AGA=A
). The most general form is

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}\widetilde{S}=0,

where
\widetilde{A}=\widetilde{G}A
and
\widetilde{S}=SG
for any
\widetilde{G},G\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
.

2.4
\boldsymbol{(A,B)}
-Manin matrices

Let
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
be two idempotents and
{\mathfrak{R}}
be an algebra. Let
x^{1},\dots,x^{m}
be generators of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
be generators of
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. They satisfy the relations
B(X\otimes X)=0
and
(\Psi\otimes\Psi)(1-A)=0
, where
X=\sum_{j=1}^{m}x^{j}e_{j}
and
\Psi=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\psi_{k}e^{k}
. Let
M
be an
n\times m
matrix with entries
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. Consider a “change of variables”

\displaystyle y^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{i}_{j}x^{j}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}}),\qquad i=1,\dots,n,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}M^{i}_{j}\in\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}}),\qquad j=1,\dots,m.

(2.19)

Consider
n\times 1
and
1\times m
matrices
Y=\sum_{i=1}^{n}y^{i}e_{i}
and
\Phi=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\phi_{j}e^{j}
. Then one can rewrite (
2.18
), (
2.19
) in the matrix form:
Y=MX
and
\Phi=\Psi M
. Now let us generalise Proposition
2.15
to this case.

Proposition 2.17
.

The following three conditions are equivalent:

\displaystyle AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}(1-B)=0,

(2.20)

\displaystyle A(Y\otimes Y)=0,

\displaystyle(\Phi\otimes\Phi)(1-B)=0.

The proof of this proposition repeats the proof of Proposition
2.15
with
B
instead of
A
somewhere.

Definition 2.18
.

An
n\times m
matrix
M
over an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
satisfying the equation (
2.20
) is called
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
or
Manin matrix corresponding to the pair of idempotents
(A,B)
.

The defining relation for the
(A,B)
-Manin matrices can be also written in terms of dual idempotents
S
and matrices
P
. For instance, the relation (
2.20
) is equivalent to (
2.17
), where we should understand the matrices
P
placing to the left from the matrices
M
as
1-2A
and
P
placing to the right as
1-2B
. In the similar way we can generalise the relation (
2.16
).

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
{\mathfrak{A}}
be an algebra and
M
be an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix with entries
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{A}}
. Let
x^{1},\dots,x^{m}\in{\mathfrak{A}}
be elements commuting with all
M^{i}_{j}
and satisfying
\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}B^{kl}_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}=0
,
k,l=1,\dots,m
,
(
in particular, it is valid for the algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
, the generators
x^{i}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
and an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M
over
{\mathfrak{R}})
. Then the elements
y^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{i}_{j}x^{j}
satisfy
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}A^{kl}_{ij}y^{i}y^{j}=0
,
k,l=1,\dots,n
.

Proof.

We use the matrix notations introduced below. From
Y=MX
and (
2.20
) we obtain

\displaystyle A(Y\otimes Y)=AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}(X\otimes X)=AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}B(X\otimes X).

The right hand side vanishes since
B(X\otimes X)=0
. ∎

Note if
M
is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix then
M^{\top}
is
\big(1-B^{\top},1-A^{\top}\big)
-Manin matrix. By means of the identification of the functors
\Xi_{A}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{1-A^{\top}}
we can write Proposition
2.19
in the following form.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
{\mathfrak{A}}
be a algebra and
M
be an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix with entries
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{A}}
. Let
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}\in{\mathfrak{A}}
be elements commuting with all
M^{i}_{j}
and satisfying
\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}A^{ij}_{kl}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}=\psi_{k}\psi_{l}
,
k,l=1,\dots,n
,
(
in particular, it is valid for the algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}=\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
, the generators
\psi^{i}\in\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M
over
{\mathfrak{R}})
. Then the elements
\phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M^{i}_{j}\psi_{i}
satisfy
\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}B^{ij}_{kl}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}=\phi_{k}\phi_{l}
,
k,l=1,\dots,m
.

For arbitrary operators
A^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
S^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
(not necessarily idempotents) the relation
A^{\prime}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}S^{\prime}=0
is equivalent to
(G_{1}A^{\prime})M^{(1)}M^{(2)}(S^{\prime}G_{2})=0
, where
G_{1}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
G_{2}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
. Hence it is equivalent to (
2.20
) for some idempotents
A
and
B
, which have the forms
A=G_{1}A^{\prime}
and
B=1-S^{\prime}G_{2}
. In particular, if
A^{\prime}
is an idempotent it is left-equivalent to the idempotent
A
; if
S^{\prime}
is an idempotent it is right-equivalent to
1-B
, which means that
1-S
is left-equivalent to
B
(see Proposition
2.8
). Thus we obtain the following statement.

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
M
be an
n\times m
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Let
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
B,B^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
be idempotents. Suppose
A
is left-equivalent to
A^{\prime}
and
B
is left-equivalent to
B^{\prime}

(
equivalently,
1-A
is right-equivalent to
1-A^{\prime}
and
1-B
is right-equivalent to
1-B^{\prime})
. Then
M
is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix iff it is an
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
-Manin matrix.

We see from this proposition that the property of the matrix
M
to be an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix effectively depends on the algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
(with fixed generators). So the notion of
(A,B)
-Manin matrix can be associated with the pair of
{\mathfrak{X}}
-quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
. Alternatively it can be associated with the pair of
\Xi
-quadratic algebras
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
. We will see this in Section
2.5
more explicitly.

Consider the question of permutation of rows and columns of a Manin matrix. Let
{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
be the
n
-th symmetric group. For a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
let us permute the rows of an
n\times m
matrix
M
in the following way: we put the
i
-th row to the place of the
\sigma(i)
-th row. We denote the obtained matrix by
{}^{\sigma}M
. By permuting columns of
M
with a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{m}
in the same way we obtain a matrix denoted by
{\vphantom{M}}{}_{\sigma}M
. More explicitly we have
(^{\sigma}M)^{\sigma(i)}_{j}=M^{i}_{j}
and
(_{\sigma}M)^{i}_{\sigma(j)}=M^{i}_{j}
. We write the permutation-index from the left since
{}_{\tau}(_{\sigma}M)={\vphantom{M}}_{\tau\sigma}M
and
{}^{\tau}(^{\sigma}M)={\vphantom{M}}^{\tau\sigma}M
for any
\tau
,
\sigma
from
{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
and
{\mathbf{S}}_{m}
respectively. Note that the space
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
has a structure of
{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
-module: a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
acts by the formula
\sigma e_{i}=e_{\sigma(i)}
, and we denote the corresponding operator
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
by the same letter
\sigma
. In this notation we have
{}^{\sigma}M=\sigma M
and
{}_{\sigma}M=M\sigma^{-1}
.

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
M
be an
n\times m
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Let
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
and
\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{m}
or, more generally,
\sigma\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
,
\tau\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{C}})
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

•

M
is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix.

•

{}^{\sigma}M=\sigma M
is a
\big((\sigma\otimes\sigma)A\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big),B\big)
-Manin matrix.

•

{}_{\tau}M=M\tau^{-1}
is a
\big(A,(\tau\otimes\tau)B\big(\tau^{-1}\otimes\tau^{-1}\big)\big)
-Manin matrix.

Proof.

Multiplication of the condition (
2.20
) by the operator
\sigma\otimes\sigma
from the left gives the relation
(\sigma\otimes\sigma)A\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big)(\sigma M)^{(1)}(\sigma M)^{(2)}(1-B)=0
. By multiplying (
2.20
) by
\tau\otimes\tau
from the right we obtain
A\big(M\tau^{-1}\big)^{(1)}\big(M\tau^{-1}\big)^{(2)}(\tau\otimes\tau)(1-B)\big(\tau^{-1}\otimes\tau^{-1}\big)=0
.
∎

Proposition
2.22
can be interpreted in terms of linear change of generators of the corresponding quadratic algebras. Indeed, consider another generators
\tilde{\psi}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{j}_{i}\psi_{j}
of the algebra
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
, where
\alpha^{j}_{i}
are entries of an invertible matrix
\alpha\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
. Let
\sigma\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
be the inverse matrix:
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha^{j}_{i}\sigma^{i}_{k}=\delta^{j}_{k}
. By substituting
\psi_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma^{i}_{k}\tilde{\psi}_{i}
to (
2.19
) we obtain
\phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\psi}_{i}(\sigma M)^{i}_{j}
. The quadratic commutation relation for the generators
\tilde{\psi}_{i}
are
\tilde{A}^{ij}_{kl}\tilde{\psi}_{i}\tilde{\psi}_{j}=\tilde{\psi}_{k}\tilde{\psi}_{l}
, where
\tilde{A}^{ij}_{kl}
are entries of the idempotent matrix
\tilde{A}=(\sigma\otimes\sigma)A\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big)
. The formulae
\tilde{\psi}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{j}_{i}\psi_{j}
,
\psi_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma^{i}_{k}\tilde{\psi}_{i}
describe the isomorphism
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\cong\Xi_{\tilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})
. It corresponds to the change of basis in
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}
, namely
\Psi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}e^{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{\psi}_{k}\tilde{e}^{k}
, where
\tilde{e}^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma^{k}_{i}e^{i}
. Since
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{1-A^{\top}}({\mathbb{C}})
we have an isomorphism
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\cong{\mathfrak{X}}_{\tilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})
, where
A
is an arbitrary idempotent and
\tilde{A}=(\sigma\otimes\sigma)A\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big)
.

Analogously, the matrix
M\tau^{-1}
corresponds to the new generators
\tilde{x}^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau^{i}_{j}x^{j}
of the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
, where
\tau^{j}_{i}
are entries of
\tau\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{C}})
. The transition to these generators corresponds to the change of basis in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. The new basis elements are
\tilde{e}_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta^{i}_{j}e_{i}
, where
\beta^{i}_{j}
are such that
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta^{i}_{j}\tau^{j}_{k}=\delta^{i}_{k}
. The formulae
\tilde{x}^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau^{i}_{j}x^{j}
,
x^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta^{k}_{i}\tilde{x}^{i}
define an isomorphism
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\cong{\mathfrak{X}}_{\tilde{B}}({\mathbb{C}})
, where
\tilde{B}=(\tau\otimes\tau)B\big(\tau^{-1}\otimes\tau^{-1}\big)
.

Thus the notion of
(A,B)
-Manin matrix is not associated with a pair of isomorphism classes of quadratic algebras. However, isomorphisms of quadratic algebras gives a transformation of the Manin matrix in the same way as the change of bases of vector spaces
V
and
W
transform a matrix of a linear operator
V\to W
. The notion associated with isomorphism classes of quadratic algebras is a notion of Manin operator, which we will introduce in Section
2.7
.

Finally, consider the case when
A=0
or
B=1
in the both cases the defining relation (
2.20
) is
0=0
. This means that any
n\times m
matrix is a
(0,B)
-Manin matrix as well as an
(A,1)
-Manin matrix, where
0\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
1\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
. Further, the definition (
2.20
) for
B=0\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
has the from
AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}=0
. By multiplying if by
(1-B)
from the right with an arbitrary idempotent
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
we obtain (
2.20
). Thus any
(A,0)
-Manin matrix is in particular an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix. Analogously, any
(1,B)
-Manin matrix is also an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix, where
1\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
.

2.5
Comma categories and Manin matrices

Consider first the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
with
A
and
B
as above. It consists of algebra homomorphisms
f\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
preserving the grading. Let
x^{1}_{A},\dots,x^{n}_{A}
and
x^{1}_{B},\dots,x^{m}_{B}
be the generators of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
respectively, they satisfy
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}A^{kl}_{ij}x^{i}_{A}x^{j}_{A}=0
and
\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}B^{kl}_{ij}x^{i}_{B}x^{j}_{B}=0
. To give a homomorphism
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
it is enough to give its value on the generators
x^{i}_{A}
; since
f(x^{i}_{A})
has degree
1
in
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
it has the form

\displaystyle f(x^{i}_{A})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{i}_{j}x^{j}_{B},

(2.21)

where
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. This means that each
f
is given by a matrix
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Proposition
2.15
implies that the formula (
2.21
) defines a homomorphism
f
iff the matrix
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix. Denote this homomorphism
f\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
by
f_{M}
. Thus we obtain a bijection
f_{M}\leftrightarrow M
between
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
and the set of all
(A,B)
-Manin matrices over the algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Note that this bijection depends on the choice of generators of the algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
.

More generally, consider the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
for two algebras
{\mathfrak{S}}
and
{\mathfrak{R}}
. It can be identified with a subset of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{S}},{\mathfrak{R}})\times\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
, since each graded homomorphism
f\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
is given on the zero-degree elements
s\in{\mathfrak{S}}
and the generators
x^{i}_{A}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. Let
\alpha\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
be an algebra homomorphism and
f_{M}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
be a graded algebra homomorphism defined by an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
, then the formulae

\displaystyle f(s)=\alpha(s),\qquad s\in{\mathfrak{S}},\qquad f(x^{i}_{A})=f_{M}(x^{i}_{A})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{i}_{j}x^{j}_{B},\qquad i=1,\dots,n,

(2.22)

define a homomorphism
f\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
iff
\alpha(s)M^{i}_{j}=M^{i}_{j}\alpha(s)
for all
s\in{\mathfrak{S}}
,
i=1,\dots,n
and
j=1,\dots,m
. We write
f=(\alpha,f_{M})
in this case. We have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\Big\{(\alpha,f_{M})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{S}},{\mathfrak{R}})\times\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)\mid\big[\alpha(s),M^{i}_{j}\big]=0\;\forall\,s,i,j\Big\}.

(2.23)

Analogously, we obtain that the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}}),\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
consists of the algebra homomorphisms
f^{M}\colon\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
defined on generators as
f^{M}\big(\psi^{B}_{j}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M^{i}_{j}\psi^{A}_{i}
, where
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
are entries of an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
. More generally,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathfrak{S}}),\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\Big\{\big(\alpha,f^{M}\big)\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{S}},{\mathfrak{R}})\times\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}}),\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)\mid\big[\alpha(s),M^{i}_{j}\big]=0\;\forall\,s,i,j\Big\}.

Definition 2.23

(
[
22
]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
be categories,
c
be an object of
{\mathcal{C}}
and
G\colon{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
be a functor. The
comma category

(c\downarrow G)
consists of the pairs
(d,f)
, where
d
is an object of
{\mathcal{D}}
and
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathcal{C}}}(c,Gd)
is a morphism in
{\mathcal{C}}
. A morphism in
(c\downarrow G)
from
(d,f)
to
(d^{\prime},f^{\prime})
is a morphism
h\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathcal{D}}}(d,d^{\prime})
such that the diagram

\textstyle{c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Gd\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Gh}
\textstyle{Gd^{\prime}}

is commutative.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be the category of associative unital algebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
be the category of associative unital
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
-graded algebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
. By setting
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{G}}
,
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{A}}
,
c={\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
G={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}
in Definition
2.23
we obtain the comma category
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big)
. It consists of the pairs
({\mathfrak{R}},f_{M})
, where
{\mathfrak{R}}
is an algebra and
f_{M}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
is a homomorphism corresponding to some
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M
over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Thus we can interpret the
(A,B)
-Manin matrices as objects of the comma category
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big)
.

The morphisms in
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big)
from
({\mathfrak{R}},f_{M})
to
({\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime},f_{M^{\prime}})
are all the homomorphisms
h\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
such that
h\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)=(M^{\prime})^{i}_{j}
, where
M^{i}_{j}
and
(M^{\prime})^{i}_{j}
are entries of the corresponding
(A,B)
-Manin matrices
M
and
M^{\prime}
. Two
(A,B)
-Manin matrices
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
M^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
are isomorphic to each other iff there exists an isomorphism
h\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
such that
h\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)=(M^{\prime})^{i}_{j}
(note that
(A,B)
-Manin matrices which have the same entries but are formally considered over different algebras may be non-isomorphic).

In the same way the
(A,B)
-Manin matrices can be interpreted as objects of the comma category
\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow\Xi_{A}\big)
. So that the last one is equivalent to
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big)
.

One of the application of the comma categories in the category theory is the universal morphism (universal arrow).

Definition 2.24

(
[
22
]
)
.

Let
{{\mathcal{C}}}
and
{{\mathcal{D}}}
be categories,
c
be an object of
{{\mathcal{C}}}
and
G\colon{{\mathcal{D}}}\to{{\mathcal{C}}}
be a functor. The
universal morphism
from
c
to
G
is the initial object in the comma category
(c\downarrow G)
, that is a pair
(r,u)
of an object
r\in{{\mathcal{D}}}
and a morphism
u\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathcal{C}}}(c,Gr)
such that for any object
d\in{{\mathcal{D}}}
and any morphism
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathcal{C}}}(c,Gd)
there is a unique morphism
h\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathcal{D}}}(r,d)
making the diagram

\textstyle{c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Gr\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Gh}
\textstyle{Gd}

commutative.

As an initial object the universal morphism is unique up to an isomorphism. Let us describe the universal morphism
({\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B},u_{A,B})
from the object
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
to the functor
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}
. It consists of the algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
generated by the elements
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
,
i=1,\dots,n
,
j=1,\dots,m
, with the commutation relation
A{\mathcal{M}}^{(1)}{\mathcal{M}}^{(2)}(1-B)=0
, where
{\mathcal{M}}
is the
n\times m
matrix with entries
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
. Due to Definition
2.18
it is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix. It defines a homomorphism
u_{A,B}=f_{\mathcal{M}}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B})
. On can check that
({\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B},u_{A,B})
is the universal morphism from
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
to
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}
: for any morphism
f=f_{M}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
corresponding to the
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M
with entries
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
there is a unique homomorphism
h\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
such that
f={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}(h)u_{A,B}
, namely,
h(\mathcal{M}^{i}_{j})=M^{i}_{j}
. Via the equivalence of comma categories
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big)
and
\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow\Xi_{A}\big)
we obtain the universal morphism from the object
\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
to the functor
\Xi_{A}
, this is the pair
({\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B},\widetilde{u}_{A,B})
, where
\widetilde{u}_{A,B}=f^{\mathcal{M}}\colon\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B})
.

Let us call the matrix
{\mathcal{M}}

the universal
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
.
The algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
is a generalisation of the right quantum algebra
[
10
]
, so we call it
the right quantum algebra for the pair of idempotents
(A,B)
.

Consider more general comma category
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big)
. It consists of pairs
({\mathfrak{R}},f)
of an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
and a homomorphism
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
. Such a homomorphism
f
can be identified with a pair of homomorphisms in the sense of the formulae (
2.22
), (
2.23
). If
f=(\alpha,f_{M})
we write also
({\mathfrak{R}},f)=({\mathfrak{R}},\alpha,f_{M})
. A morphism
h\colon({\mathfrak{R}},\alpha,f_{M})\to({\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},f_{M^{\prime}})
is a homomorphism
h\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
such that
h\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
and
h\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)=(M^{\prime})^{i}_{j}
.

The initial object in
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big)
is the universal morphism
({\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B},\iota,u_{A,B})
, where
\iota\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
is the inclusion. A homomorphism
f=(\alpha,f_{M})\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
gives the unique homomorphism
h\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
defined by the formulae
h(s)=\alpha(s)
and
h(\mathcal{M}^{i}_{j})=M^{i}_{j}
and making the diagram

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.67365pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.67365pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.67365pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 102.75114pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}(h)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.75114pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 40.67365pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 84.99019pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(2.24)

commutative.

Now let us calculate a composition

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\xrightarrow{(\alpha,f_{M})}{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\xrightarrow{(\beta,f_{N})}{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}(\mathfrak{T}),

where
C\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{k}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{k})
is an idempotent,
\mathfrak{T}
is an algebra,
\beta\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to\mathfrak{T}
is a homomorphism and
f_{N}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}(\mathfrak{T})
is defined by
(B,C)
-Manin matrix
N=(N^{j}_{l})
. On the elements
s\in{\mathfrak{S}}
we have
(\beta,f_{N})(\alpha,f_{M})(s)=\beta(\alpha(s))
. On the generators
x^{i}_{A}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
we obtain
(\beta,f_{N})(\alpha,f_{M})(x^{i}_{A})=(\beta,f_{N})(\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{i}_{j}x^{j}_{B})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\beta\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)N^{j}_{l}x^{l}_{C}
. Thus,

\displaystyle(\beta,f_{N})(\alpha,f_{M})=\big(\beta\alpha,f_{K}\big),

(2.25)

where
K=\beta(M)N
is the
n\times k
matrix with entries
K^{i}_{l}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)N^{j}_{l}\in\mathfrak{T}
. Since (
2.25
) is a morphism the matrix
K
is an
(A,C)
-Manin matrix.

Recall also (see
[
22
]
) that the functor
F\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
is called
left adjoint
to the functor
G\colon{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
(while the functor
G\colon{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is called
right adjoint
to
F\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
), iff there is an isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}(c,Gd)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{D}}(Fc,d)
natural in
c
and
d
. To construct a left adjoint functor to a functor
G\colon{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
it is enough to construct a universal morphism
(r_{c},u_{c})
from each
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
to
G
. Then the left adjoint functor on objects is defined as
Fc=r_{c}
. For a morphism
\alpha\colon c\to c^{\prime}
the morphism
F\alpha\colon Fc\to Fc^{\prime}
is the unique morphism
h\colon Fc\to Fc^{\prime}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{GFc\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Gh}
\textstyle{c^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{c^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{GFc^{\prime}}

is commutative (as consequence, we obtain a natural transformation
u\colon\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}\to GF
with components
u_{c}\colon c\to GFc
).

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}
be the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{G}}
consisting of the graded algebras of the form
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})
. This is the category of quadratic algebras. We can consider the functors
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}
and
\Xi_{A}
as functors from
{\mathcal{A}}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
. This means that we can substitute
{\mathcal{G}}
by
{\mathcal{Q}}
in the previous considerations.

From the diagram (
2.24
) we see that the functor
G={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}
has a left adjoint functor
F=F_{B}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
. It is defined on objects as
F_{B}\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\big)={\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
. In particular, on the quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\in{\mathcal{Q}}
the functor
F_{B}
gives the right quantum algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
.

Let us calculate the functor
F_{B}
on a morphism
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\xrightarrow{(\alpha,f_{N})}{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
, where
\alpha\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\to{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is a homomorphism and
f_{N}
is defined by an
(A,A^{\prime})
-Manin matrix
N=(N^{i}_{j})
. We need to construct a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\iota,u_{A,B})}
\scriptstyle{(\alpha,f_{N})}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}(h)}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\iota^{\prime},u_{A^{\prime},B})}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A^{\prime},B}).}

Remind that
u_{A,B}=f_{\mathcal{M}}
and
u_{A^{\prime},B}=f_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}}
, where
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
is the universal
(A^{\prime},B)
-Manin matrix. By the formula (
2.25
) we have
f=(\iota\alpha,f_{K})
, where
K=N\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. Thus we obtain the homomorphism
F_{B}(\alpha,f_{N})=h\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\to{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A^{\prime},B}
defined by the formulae

\displaystyle h(s)=\alpha(s),\qquad s\in{\mathfrak{S}},\qquad h(\mathcal{M}^{i}_{j})=\sum_{l}N^{i}_{l}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{l}_{j}.

The left adjoint to the functor
\Xi_{A}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}
is the functor
\widetilde{F}_{A}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
defined on objects as
\widetilde{F}_{A}\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathfrak{S}})\big)={\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
. The value of
\widetilde{F}_{A}
on
(\alpha,f^{N})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathfrak{S}}),\Xi_{B^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})\big)
is the homomorphism
h\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\to{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B^{\prime}}
such that
h(s)=\alpha(s)
and
h({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j})=\sum_{l}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})^{i}_{l}N^{l}_{j}
, where
{\mathcal{M}}=({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j})
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\big(({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})^{i}_{l}\big)
are the universal
(A,B)
- and
(A,B^{\prime})
-Manin matrices.

In particular, for
{\mathfrak{S}}={\mathbb{C}}
we have the natural bijections

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}}),\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B},{\mathfrak{R}}).

(2.26)

This means that the set of
(A,B)
-Manin matrices over
{\mathfrak{R}}
is identified with the set of algebra homomorphisms
f\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
. Namely, each
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
has the form
M^{i}_{j}=f({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j})
for some homomorphism
f\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
and any homomorphism
f\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
gives an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M
. In terms of non-commutative geometry
[
26
]
this means that an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
is an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-point of the algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
.

Proposition 2.25
.

Let
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
A^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
be idempotents. Then the following three statements are equivalent:

•

{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\cong{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
as graded algebras.

•

\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\cong\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
as graded algebras.

•

n=m
and there exists
\sigma\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
such that
A
is left-equivalent to the idempotent
(\sigma\otimes\sigma)A^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big)

(
that is
A^{\prime}=GA(\sigma\otimes\sigma)
for some operators
G\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
\sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}))
.

Proof.

If
A
is left-equivalent to
\widetilde{A}=(\sigma\otimes\sigma)A^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big)
then
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\cong{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\cong\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
(see Section
2.4
). Conversely let
f\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
be an isomorphism and
f^{-1}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
be its inverse. They correspond to
(A,A^{\prime})
-Manin matrices
\sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
\sigma^{-1}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
respectively. Due to invertibility of these matrices we must have
n=m
. The relations
A(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(1-A^{\prime})=0
and
A^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big)(1-A)=0
imply
A(1-\widetilde{A})=0
and
\widetilde{A}(1-A)=0
. By virtue of Lemma
2.11
the idempotents
A
and
\widetilde{A}
are left-equivalent. One can similarly prove that the second statement implies the third one. ∎

Remark 2.26
.

In the same way one can prove that
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
are isomorphic as graded algebras iff
n=m
,
{\mathfrak{S}}\cong{\mathfrak{R}}
and there exist invertible matrices
M\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathfrak{R}})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
,
G\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
such that
A^{\prime}=GA(M\otimes M)
, where
\mathcal{Z}({\mathfrak{R}})=\{z\in{\mathfrak{R}}\mid[z,r]=0\;\forall\,r\in{\mathfrak{R}}\}
is the centre of
{\mathfrak{R}}
. The isomorphism and its inverse have the form
(\alpha,f_{M})\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})
and
\big(\alpha^{-1},f_{N}\big)\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})
, where
\alpha\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
is an isomorphism in
{\mathcal{A}}
and
N=\alpha^{-1}\big(M^{-1}\big)\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathfrak{S}})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
.

2.6
Products of Manin matrices and co-algebra structure

The main property of Manin matrices is that their product is also a Manin matrix under some condition.

Proposition 2.27
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be an algebra. Let
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
,
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
and
C\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big({\mathbb{C}}^{k}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{k}\big)
be idempotents.
Let
M
and
N
be
n\times m
and
m\times k
matrices over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Suppose that
M
and
N
are
(A,B)
- and
(B,C)
-Manin matrices respectively and that

\displaystyle\big[M^{i}_{j},N^{j^{\prime}}_{l}\big]=0\qquad\forall i=1,\dots,n,\quad j,j^{\prime}=1,\dots,m,\quad l=1,\dots,k.

(2.27)

Then the
n\times k
matrix
K=MN
is an
(A,C)
-Manin matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
.

Proof.

Since
M
is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix we have
AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}=AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}B
. The commutativity condition (
2.27
) implies that
N^{(1)}M^{(2)}=M^{(2)}N^{(1)}
, hence

\displaystyle AK^{(1)}K^{(2)}(1-C)

\displaystyle=AM^{(1)}N^{(1)}M^{(2)}N^{(2)}(1-C)=AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}(1-C)

\displaystyle=AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}BN^{(1)}N^{(2)}(1-C).

The right hand side vanishes since
N
is a
(B,C)
-Manin matrix. ∎

Remark 2.28
.

Proposition
2.27
can be proved by using the functors
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}
or
\Xi_{A}
. In the case of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}
one needs to consider the elements
y^{j}=\sum_{l}N^{j}_{l}x^{l}
and
z^{i}=\sum_{j}M^{i}_{j}y^{j}
of the algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathfrak{R}})
, where
x^{l}=x^{l}_{C}
. By applying Proposition
2.19
twice we obtain the relations
\sum_{k,l}A^{ij}_{kl}z^{k}z^{l}=0
. Since
z^{i}=\sum_{l}K^{i}_{l}x^{l}
the matrix
K
is an
(A,C)
-Manin matrix by virtue of Proposition
2.17
. Analogously, one can apply Propositions
2.20
and
2.17
to the elements
\phi_{j}=\sum_{i}M^{i}_{j}\psi_{i}
and
\chi_{l}=\sum_{j}N^{j}_{l}\phi_{j}
of the algebra
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
.

In some particular case the property claimed in Proposition
2.27
was deduced in Section
2.5
(see the formula (
2.25
) and the text after it). The role of
{\mathfrak{R}}
is played by the algebra
\mathfrak{T}
. The homomorphism
\beta\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to\mathfrak{T}
gives a
{\mathfrak{R}}
-algebra structure on
\mathfrak{T}
and maps entry-wise the
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
over
{\mathfrak{R}}
to a
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
\beta(M)=\big(\beta\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)\big)
over
\mathfrak{T}
. The entries of this matrix commute with the entries of
N
since
[\beta(r),N^{i}_{j}]=0
for any
r\in{\mathfrak{R}}
.

The typical case of Proposition
2.27
is
{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
,
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{S}}
,
N^{j}_{l}\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
. In this case it can be formulated in terms of comma categories as follows. Let
({\mathfrak{S}},f_{M})
and
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},f_{N})
be objects of the categories
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big)
and
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}\big)
corresponding to
(A,B)
- and
(B,C)
-Manin matrices
M
and
N
. Then
({\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},f_{K})
is an object of
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}\big)
, where
K=MN
. In particular, for
A=B=C
we obtain a structure of tensor category on the comma category
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}\big)
with the unit object
\big({\mathbb{C}},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})}\big)
corresponding to the unit matrix.

Proposition
2.27
can be formulated in terms of right quantum algebras. This formulation was described in the works
[
23
,
24
,
26
]
.

Proposition 2.29
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
,
{\mathcal{N}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}
be universal
(A,B)
-,
(B,C)
- and
(A,C)
-Manin matrices respectively. They have entries
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
,
{\mathcal{N}}^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{B,C}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,C}
. Then the formula

\displaystyle\Delta_{A,B,C}({\mathcal{K}}^{i}_{l})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}^{j}_{l}

defines a homomorphism
\Delta_{A,B,C}\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,C}\to{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{B,C}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{B,C}
. The matrix
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
with entries
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{i}_{l}=\Delta_{A,B,C}\big({\mathcal{K}}^{i}_{l}\big)\in{\mathfrak{R}}
is the product of the matrices
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
. We need to check that
A\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(2)}(1-C)=0
. The last equality means that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
is an
(A,C)
-Manin matrix, but this follows from Proposition
2.27
. ∎

Note that in terms of functors
F_{A},\widetilde{F}_{A}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
constructed in Section
2.5
(as left adjoint functors to the functors
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A},\Xi_{A}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}
) we have
\Delta_{A,B,C}=F_{C}(u_{A,B})=\widetilde{F}_{A}(\widetilde{u}_{B,C})
, where
u_{A,B}=f_{\mathcal{M}}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B})
and
\widetilde{u}_{B,C}=f^{{\mathcal{N}}}\colon\Xi_{C}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{B}({\mathfrak{U}}_{B,C})
are the corresponding universal homomorphisms.

Consider the algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}={\mathfrak{U}}_{A,A}
. Proposition
2.29
implies that the map
\Delta_{A}=\Delta_{A,A,A}
is a homomorphism
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}\to{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
. Moreover, it is easy to check that
\Delta_{A}
is a comultiplication, that is
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\Delta_{A})\Delta_{A}=(\Delta_{A}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\Delta_{A}
. More generally, we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{A,B,D}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{A,C,D}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{B,D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\Delta_{B,C,D}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,C}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{C,D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{A,B,C}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{B,C}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{C,D},}

which reflects the associativity of the matrix multiplication.

Proposition 2.30
.

The algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
has a bialgebra structure defined by the following comultiplication
\Delta_{A}\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}\to{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
and counit
\varepsilon_{A}\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}\to{\mathbb{C}}
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{A}({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}^{j}_{k},\qquad\varepsilon_{A}\big({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}\big)=\delta^{i}_{j}.

(2.28)

The formula (
2.21
) for the universal
A
-Manin matrix
{\mathcal{M}}
gives a coaction of the bialgebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
on the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. This is a homomorphism
\delta=f_{\mathcal{M}}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}\otimes{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
defined as

\displaystyle\delta(x^{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}\otimes x^{j}.

It satisfies the coaction axiom
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\delta)\delta=(\Delta\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\delta
. In terms of non-commutative geometry the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
is interpreted as an algebra of functions on a non-commutative space and the coaction
\delta
as an action on this space. Thus the bialgebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
(or its dual) plays the role of algebra of endomorphisms of a non-commutative space corresponding to the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
.

More generally, for arbitrary
A,B,C
we have
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes u_{B,C})u_{A,B}=(\Delta_{A,B,C}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)u_{A,C}
and
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\widetilde{u}_{A,B})\widetilde{u}_{B,C}=\big(\Delta^{\rm op}_{A,B,C}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\big)\widetilde{u}_{A,C}
, where
\Delta^{\rm op}_{A,B,C}\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,C}\to{\mathfrak{U}}_{B,C}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
is an algebra homomorphism defined as
\Delta^{\rm op}_{A,B,C}\big({\mathcal{K}}^{i}_{l}\big)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\mathcal{N}}^{j}_{l}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
. In particular, the homomorphism
\widetilde{u}_{A,A}
:
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}\otimes\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
is a coaction on the algebra
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
with respect to the comultiplication
\Delta^{\rm op}_{A}=\Delta^{\rm op}_{A,A,A}\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}\to{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
.

Remark 2.31
.

The bialgebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
is not a Hopf algebra: an antipode
S
for the bialgebra structure (
2.28
) should have the form
