S({\mathcal{M}})={\mathcal{M}}^{-1}
, but the matrix
{\mathcal{M}}
is not invertible over
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
. One can extend the algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}_{A}
by adding new generators
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
being elements of the formal inverse matrix
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}^{-1}
. Then the matrix
S({\mathcal{M}})^{\top}
should be inverse to
\widetilde{M}^{\top}
, however its invertibility is not guaranteed in this extended algebra. The universal construction of a Hopf algebra extending the bialgebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
is
Hopf envelope

[
26
]
. This is the algebra
\mathcal{H}_{A}
generated by the entries of the infinite series of matrices
{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, with the relations

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k}^{\top}{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}={\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}{\mathcal{M}}_{k}^{\top}=1,\qquad A{\mathcal{M}}_{2k}^{(1)}{\mathcal{M}}_{2k}^{(2)}(1-A)=0,

\displaystyle\big(1-A^{\top}\big){\mathcal{M}}_{2k+1}^{(2)}{\mathcal{M}}_{2k+1}^{(1)}A^{\top}=0,

k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Its Hopf structure is given by the formulae

\displaystyle\Delta_{A}\big(({\mathcal{M}}_{k})^{i}_{l}\big)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}({\mathcal{M}}_{k})^{i}_{j}\otimes({\mathcal{M}}_{k})^{j}_{l},\qquad\varepsilon_{A}({\mathcal{M}}_{k})=1,\qquad S({\mathcal{M}}_{k})={\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}^{\top}.

The algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
is mapped to
\mathcal{H}_{A}
by the formula
{\mathcal{M}}\mapsto{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
. Note also that the matrix
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{-1}={\mathcal{M}}_{1}
satisfies the relation

\displaystyle A\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{(2)}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{(1)}(1-A)=0.

The complex square and rectangular matrices are often interpreted as homomorphisms in a category with objects
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and homomorphisms
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(m,n)=\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits_{n\times m}({\mathbb{C}})
(it is equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces). One can interpret the Manin matrices in a similar way. Let
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
be a small full tensor subcategory, this means that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is a full subcategory of
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
{\mathbb{C}}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
,
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}

\;\forall\,{\mathfrak{R}},{\mathfrak{S}}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Ob}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
is a small set (in practice one needs only a small set of algebras, so we can take the full tensor subcategory generated by this set as
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
). Define the following category
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
. The objects of
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
are all the idempotents
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. The homomorphisms
A\to B
are all the
(A,B)
-Manin matrices
M
over algebras
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
.
In other words, we define
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(A,B)=\bigsqcup_{{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
, these sets are small since
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is small. The composition of homomorphisms
(M,{\mathfrak{R}})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
and
(N,{\mathfrak{S}})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
we define as
(NM,{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}})
. Due to Proposition
2.27
the product
NM
is an
(A,C)
-Manin matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}
.

If two idempotents are left-equivalent, then they are isomorphic as objects of
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
, so it is enough to take the quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
as objects of
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
instead of the idempotents. Moreover, one can prove that
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
are isomorphic as objects of
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
iff they are isomorphic as objects of
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Indeed, if
({\mathfrak{R}},f_{M})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
is an isomorphism
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
and
({\mathfrak{S}},f_{N})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\big)
is its inverse then
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}={\mathbb{C}}
, hence
{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathfrak{S}}={\mathbb{C}}
and
f_{M}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
,
f_{N}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{C}})
are isomorphisms in
{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Remark 2.32
.

Due to Proposition
2.12
the formula
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\mapsto\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
correctly define an operation on the quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{A}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
. It was denoted in the works of Manin
[
23
,
24
,
26
]
by
{\mathfrak{A}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{A}}^{!}
. The first equality (
2.26
) gives a contravariant fully faithful functor
(-)^{!}\colon{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
. Since any object of
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
isomorphic to
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
for some idempotent
A
this functor is an anti-autoequivalence of the category
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
. The (quasi-)inverse of
(-)^{!}
is
(-)^{!}
itself.

Remark 2.33
.

One can extend the category
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
up to a category
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
by taking all the algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})\in{\mathcal{Q}}
as objects of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
. The sets of homomorphisms in
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
can be defined as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}))={}

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}\Big\{(\alpha,M)\mid\alpha\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{S}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(M,{\mathfrak{R}})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}})\big),\;\big[\alpha(s),M^{i}_{j}\big]=0\;\forall\,s\in{\mathfrak{S}}\Big\}

with some natural composition rule. In these settings
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{S}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
are isomorphic as objects of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
iff they are isomorphic as objects of
{\mathcal{Q}}
(see Remark
2.26
).

2.7
Infinite-dimensional case: Manin operators

Let
V
and
W
be two vector spaces (may be infinite-dimensional). Let
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V\otimes V)
and
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(W\otimes W)
be idempotents and
{\mathfrak{R}}
be an algebra. Instead of a matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
we need to take an element
M
of the space
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
or of some completion of this space. We consider
M
as an operator with entries in the algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
: this means that
\lambda Mw\in{\mathfrak{R}}
, where
\lambda\in V^{*}
and
w\in W
(in the case of completion the covector
\lambda
runs over a subset of the dual space
V^{*}
such that
\lambda Mw
is well defined).

Definition 2.34
.

The operator
M
(from the space
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
or its completion) is called an
(A,B)
-Manin operator
if it satisfies the relation
AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}(1-B)=0
, where
M^{(1)}=M\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}
and
M^{(2)}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W}\otimes M
. In the case
V=W
,
A=B
it is called
A
-Manin operator
.

Let
(v_{i})
be a basis of the space
V
, so that any vector
v\in V
has the from
v=\sum_{i}\alpha^{i}v_{i}
for unique coefficients
\alpha^{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}
and the sum is finite. Then the action of the operator
A
can be written as
A(v_{k}\otimes v_{l})=\sum_{i,j}A^{ij}_{kl}v_{j}\otimes v_{j}
for unique coefficients
A^{ij}_{kl}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Since the sum
\sum_{i,j}
should be finite there are only finitely many non-zero coefficients
A^{ij}_{kl}
for any fixed
k
,
l
, we call it
column finiteness condition
for the matrix
(A^{ij}_{kl})
. This condition allows as to define the quadratic algebra
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
generated by
\psi_{i}
with commutation relations
\psi_{k}\psi_{l}=\sum_{i,j}A^{ij}_{kl}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}
. Formally this is the quotient
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})=\mathfrak{T}/\mathfrak{I}
, where
\mathfrak{T}
is the algebra of all the non-commutative polynomials of the formal variables
\psi_{i}
while
\mathfrak{I}
is the ideal of
\mathfrak{T}
generated by the elements
\psi_{k}\psi_{l}-\sum_{i,j}A^{ij}_{kl}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\in\mathfrak{T}
. Another choice of the basis
(v_{i})
leads to an isomorphic quadratic algebra, so it essentially depends on the operator
A
only.

To interpret an
(A,B)
-Manin operator
M
as an object of a comma category as in Section
2.5
we need to define also an algebra
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
. Let
(v_{i})
and
(w_{i})
be bases of
V
and
W
. The formula
Mw_{j}=\sum_{i}M^{i}_{j}v_{i}
defines the entries
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
of an
(A,B)
-Manin operator
M
in these bases. If
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
(without completion), then there are only finitely many non-zero entries
M^{i}_{j}
for any fixed
j

\big(
column finiteness condition for the matrix
\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)\big)
and hence the sum
\sum_{i}M^{i}_{j}\psi_{i}
is finite.
Define
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
(non-completed). Then the set of
(A,B)
-Manin operators
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
bijectively corresponds to the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}}),\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
.

To consider the case of arbitrary infinite matrix
\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
without any finiteness condition we define a completion of the space
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
as the set of all the infinite formal sums
\sum_{i,j}M^{i}_{j}E_{i}{}^{j}
, where
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
and
E_{i}{}^{j}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
are operators acting as
E_{i}{}^{j}w_{k}=\delta^{j}_{k}v_{i}
. Denote this completion by
{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
. It can be identified with
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(W,{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V\big)
, where
{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V
is the completion of
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
consisting of all the infinite formal sums
\sum_{i}r_{i}v_{i}
,
r_{i}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. The operator
M=\sum_{i,j}M^{i}_{j}E_{i}{}^{j}
acts by the formula
Mw_{j}=\sum_{i}M^{i}_{j}v_{i}
.

Note that the completion
{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V

(
and hence
{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(W,{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V\big)
)
does not depend on the choice of the basis
(w_{i})
, but it does depend on the choice of the basis
(v_{i})
. Namely, a basis
(v_{i})
defines the following topology in the
{\mathfrak{R}}
-module
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
: neighbourhoods of
0
are the
{\mathfrak{R}}
-submodules generated by all
v_{i}
except finitely many of them. The module
{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V
is the completion of
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
with respect to this topology. Any two bases
(v_{i})
and
(v^{\prime}_{i})
are related by
v_{i}=\sum_{k}\alpha^{k}_{i}v^{\prime}_{k}
,
v^{\prime}_{j}=\sum_{k}\beta^{k}_{j}v_{k}
, they define the same topology of
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
iff for any
k
there are only finitely many non-zero
\alpha^{k}_{i}
and
\beta^{k}_{j}
.

Suppose
A
satisfy
row finiteness condition
: there are only finitely many non-zero
A^{ij}_{kl}
for fixed
i
,
j
. This condition means exactly that the operator
A\colon V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
is continuous with respect to the topology corresponding to the basis
(v_{i}\otimes v_{j})
. Define
\widehat{\Xi}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})=\widehat{\mathfrak{T}}/\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}
, where
\widehat{\mathfrak{T}}=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\widehat{\mathfrak{T}}_{k}
is the graded algebra with grading component
\widehat{\mathfrak{T}}_{k}
consisting of the infinite formal sums

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}r_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\psi_{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}},\qquad r_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\in{\mathfrak{R}},

and
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}=\Big\{\sum_{k,l}t_{kl}(\psi_{k}\psi_{l}-\sum_{i,j}A^{ij}_{kl}\psi_{i}\psi_{j})t^{\prime}_{kl}\mid t_{kl},t^{\prime}_{kl}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{T}}\Big\}
(due to the row finiteness condition the sum over
k
and
l
is correctly defined). The algebra
\widehat{\Xi}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
is a completion of
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
, it depends on the choice of the basis
(v_{i})
due to the identification
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{1}=V
via
\psi_{i}\leftrightarrow v_{i}
.

The proof of Lemma
2.3
holds for the completed algebra
\widehat{\Xi}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
, so that the system of equations
\sum_{i,j}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}T^{ij}_{ab}=0
for
T^{ij}_{ab}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
is equivalent to the system
\sum_{k,l}A^{ij}_{kl}T^{kl}_{ab}=0
. Hence we have a bijection between the set of
(A,B)
-operators
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
and the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}}),\widehat{\Xi}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
.

Let
V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V
be the completion of
V\otimes V
with respect to the topology corresponding to the basis
(v_{i}\otimes v_{j})
. It consists of all the infinite formal sums
\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{ij}v_{i}\otimes v_{j}
,
\alpha_{ij}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Any matrix
\big(A^{ij}_{kl}\big)
satisfying row finiteness condition define a continuous operator
A\colon V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V\to V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V
by the formula
Av_{k}\otimes v_{l}=\sum_{i,j}A^{ij}_{kl}v_{i}\otimes v_{j}
. Denote by
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V\big)
the space of all the continuous operators
V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V\to V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V
. Note that we do not need the column finiteness condition to define the algebra
\widehat{\Xi}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
. Hence the
(A,B)
-Manin operators
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
for arbitrary idempotents
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V\big)
and
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(W\otimes W)
can be identified with the elements of the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}}),\widehat{\Xi}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
. Explicitly, the relations
\sum_{k,l,a,b}A^{ij}_{kl}M^{k}_{a}M^{l}_{b}\big(\delta^{a}_{r}\delta^{b}_{s}-B^{ab}_{rs}\big)=0
are correctly defined in this case since all the sums in these relations are finite.

Thus the
(A,B)
-Manin operators in the non-completed or completed case are objects of the comma category
\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow\Xi_{A}\big)
or
\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow\widehat{\Xi}_{A}\big)
for the functor
\Xi_{A}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{G}}
or
\widehat{\Xi}_{A}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{G}}
respectively.

One can also generalise the graded algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
to the case of infinite-dimensional matrices
(A^{ij}_{kl})
. To do it one needs to require the row finiteness condition, which means that this matrix defines a continuous operator
A\colon V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V\to V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V
. For any idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(V\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V\big)
define the quadratic algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
as the algebra generated by
x^{i}
with the commutation relations
\sum_{k,l}A^{ij}_{kl}x^{k}x^{l}=0
. That is
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-A^{\top}}({\mathbb{C}})
, where
A^{\top}
is the operator
V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
defined as
A^{\top}v_{i}\otimes v_{j}=\sum_{k,l}A^{ij}_{kl}v_{k}\otimes v_{l}
. Let
W\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}W
be the completion of
W\otimes W
with respect to the basis
(w_{i}\otimes w_{j})
and
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(W\otimes W)
be an idempotent. The corresponding matrix
(B^{ij}_{kl})
satisfy the column finiteness condition. Define
\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})=\widehat{\Xi}_{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-B^{\top}}({\mathfrak{R}})
, where
B^{\top}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(W\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}W\big)
is the continuous operator
W\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}W\to W\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}W
acting as
B^{\top}w_{i}\otimes w_{j}=\sum_{k,l}B^{ij}_{kl}w_{k}\otimes w_{l}
. Then we have the comma category
({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}_{B})
equivalent to
\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\downarrow\widehat{\Xi}_{A}\big)
.

In particular, the completions allow us to consider the
universal
(A,B)
-Manin operator
. It has the form
{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i,j}{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}E_{i}{}^{j}
, where
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
are generators of the algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
with the commutation relations
\sum_{k,l,a,b}A^{ij}_{kl}{\mathcal{M}}^{k}_{a}{\mathcal{M}}^{l}_{b}\big(\delta^{a}_{r}\delta^{b}_{s}-B^{ab}_{rs}\big)=0
. These relations are correctly defined iff the matrices
\big(A^{ij}_{kl}\big)
and
\big(B^{ij}_{kl}\big)
satisfy the row and column finiteness conditions respectively.

Proposition
2.27
can be generalised if we additionally suppose that the sums
\sum_{j}M^{i}_{j}N^{j}_{l}
are well defined: if nether
M
nor
N
satisfies a needed finiteness condition, then we need to complete
{\mathfrak{R}}
in order to include all these sums. In particular, the map
\Delta_{A,B,C}
is a homomorphism from
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,C}
to a completion of
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\otimes{\mathfrak{U}}_{B,C}
which contains the sums
\sum_{j}{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}{\mathcal{N}}^{j}_{l}
(we need to suppose that
A
,
B
and
C
satisfy the row, both and column finiteness conditions respectively). In the case
A=B=C
the map
\Delta_{A}
is a “completed” comultiplication for
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}={\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
. In terms of representations this means that tensor product of
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A}
-modules are not always defined: we need to impose some finiteness condition on the modules to guarantee the existence of their tensor product.

Finally, we consider some examples of completions used below. The simplest example of the infinite-dimensional space is the space of polynomials
V={\mathbb{C}}[u]
. It has the basis
\big(v_{i}=u^{i-1}\big)_{i\geqslant 1}
. The completion of
{\mathfrak{R}}[u]={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}[u]
is the algebra of formal series
{\mathfrak{R}}[[u]]
. It consists of all the formal infinite sums
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}r_{k}u^{k}
,
r_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. The algebra of finite Laurent polynomials
{\mathfrak{R}}\big[u,u^{-1}\big]={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[u,u^{-1}\big]
can be completed to the algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}((u))
consisting of the series
\sum_{k=N}^{\infty}r_{k}u^{k}
,
N\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
r_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. It has another completion – the algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}((u^{-1}))
consisting of
\sum_{k=-\infty}^{N}r_{k}u^{k}
,
N\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
r_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. Note that the space
{\mathfrak{R}}[[u,u^{-1}]]
consisting of
\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}r_{k}u^{k}
is also a completion of
{\mathfrak{R}}\big[u,u^{-1}\big]
, but it is not an algebra in the usual sense.

3
Particular cases

Here we consider the main examples corresponding to the polynomial and Grassmann algebras and their deformations. We also consider a generalisation of a deformed polynomial algebra with three variables. More examples will appear in Sections
4
,
6
,
7
and Appendix
B
.

3.1
Manin matrices for the polynomial algebras

Let
P_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be the permutation operator acting as

\displaystyle P_{n}(v\otimes w)=w\otimes v,\qquad v,w\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}.

(3.1)

Substituting basis elements
v=e_{k}
and
w=e_{l}
we obtain the entries
(P_{n})^{ij}_{kl}=\delta^{i}_{l}\delta^{j}_{k}
. Since
P_{n}^{2}=1
the operators

\displaystyle A_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1-P_{n}\big),\qquad S_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1+P_{n}\big)=1-A_{n}

(3.2)

are idempotents:
A_{n}^{2}=A_{n}
,
S_{n}^{2}=S_{n}
. These are anti-symmetrizer and symmetrizer for two tensor factors respectively. Note that the permutation operator satisfies the braid relation

\displaystyle P_{n}^{(23)}P_{n}^{(12)}P_{n}^{(23)}=P_{n}^{(12)}P_{n}^{(23)}P_{n}^{(12)}

(3.3)

(this is an equality of operators acting on the space
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
).

The commutation relations (
2.1
) and (
2.9
) for
A=A_{n}
have the form
x^{i}x^{j}-x^{j}x^{i}=0
and
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+\psi_{j}\psi_{i}=0
. Hence the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
is the polynomial algebra
{\mathbb{C}}[x^{1},\dots,x^{n}]
and
\Xi_{A_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
is the Grassmann algebra with Grassmann variables
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
.

The
(A_{n},A_{m})
-Manin matrices are
n\times m
matrices
M
over an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
satisfying the relation

\displaystyle A_{n}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}S_{m}=0.

(3.4)

These matrices were called
Manin matrices
in
[
4
]
. In this subsection we call them just Manin matrices if there is no confusion with the general notion of Manin matrix.

To write the matrix relation (
3.4
) in terms of entries one can substitute
P^{ij}_{kl}=\delta^{i}_{l}\delta^{j}_{k}
to the formula (
2.17
) written in entries. We obtain the following system of commutation relations:

\displaystyle\big[M^{i}_{k},M^{j}_{k}\big]=0,\qquad i<j,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\big[M^{i}_{k},M^{j}_{l}\big]+\big[M^{i}_{l},M^{j}_{k}\big]=0,\qquad i<j,\quad k<l,

(3.6)

(the relations (
3.5
) is in fact the relations (
3.6
) for
k=l
).
The commutation relations (
3.5
) mean that any two entries of the same column commute. The formula (
3.6
) is so-called
cross-relation
for the
2\times 2
submatrix with rows
i
,
j
and columns
k
,
l
.

For example, consider a
2\times 2
matrix

\displaystyle M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\!.

(3.7)

It is a Manin matrix iff its entries
a,b,c,d\in{\mathfrak{R}}
satisfy

\displaystyle[a,c]=0,\qquad[b,d]=0,\qquad[a,d]+[b,c]=0.

(3.8)

In the case
n\geqslant 2
an
n\times m
matrix is a Manin matrix iff any
2\times 2
submatrix of this matrix is a Manin matrix. In particular, any matrix over a commutative algebra is a Manin matrix.

The notion of
(A_{n},A_{m})
-Manin matrix is such a non-commutative generalisation of matrix that the most of the properties of the usual matrices are inherited (with some generalisation of the notion of determinant). These properties are described in the works
[
4
]
and
[
5
]
in details.

It is clear, that a Manin matrix keeps to be a Manin matrix after the following operations: taking a submatrix, permutation of rows or columns, doubling of a row or a column. In other words, if
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
is an
n\times m
Manin matrix and
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
j_{1},\dots,j_{l}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
then the new
k\times l
matrix
N
with the entries
N^{s}_{t}=M^{i_{s}}_{j_{t}}
is also a Manin matrix. Note that in the case of a permutation this fact follows from Proposition
2.22
and
(\sigma\otimes\sigma)A_{n}\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big)=A_{n}

\forall\,\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
.

Let us recall one more important fact on the Manin matrices
[
5
]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

A matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
has pairwise commuting entries
(
i.e.,
[M^{i}_{j},M^{k}_{l}]=0
for any
i
,
j
,
k
,
l)
iff
M
and its transposed
M^{\top}
are both Manin matrices.

Proof.

If all entries of
M
commute with each other then
M
as well as
M^{\top}
is a Manin matrix. In the converse direction it is enough to prove the statement for the case of
2\times 2
matrix, since any two entries are contained in some
2\times 2
submatrix (if
n=1
or
m=1
there are no
2\times 2
submatrices – in this case the commutativity of entries follows from the relations (
3.5
) for
M^{\top}
or
M
respectively).

The condition that the matrix
M^{\top}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\
b&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is a Manin matrix is equivalent to the relations
[a,b]=0
,
[c,d]=0
,
[a,d]-[b,c]=0
. Together with the relations (
3.8
) they imply that all the entries
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
pairwise commute. ∎

For a general Manin matrix the entries from different columns do not commute, so the notion of determinant should be generalised in a special way. It turns out that the natural generalisation is so-called
column determinant
. For a
n\times n
matrix
M
over an algebra (or a ring)
{\mathfrak{R}}
it is defined as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits M=\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits^{\rm col}M=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}M^{\sigma(1)}_{1}M^{\sigma(2)}_{2}\cdots M^{\sigma(n)}_{n},

where
(-1)^{\sigma}
is the sign of the permutation
\sigma
. This is the usual expression for the determinant, but with the specified order of entries in each term: they are ordered in accordance with the order of columns. If
M
is a Manin matrix then the order of columns can be chosen in a different way and this leads to the same result (see
[
4
,
5
]
):

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}M^{\sigma(i_{1})}_{i_{1}}M^{\sigma(i_{2})}_{i_{2}}\cdots M^{\sigma(i_{n})}_{i_{n}}=\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits^{\rm col}M,\qquad\begin{pmatrix}1&2&\cdots&n\\
i_{1}&i_{2}&\cdots&i_{n}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}.

(3.9)

However, we can not take different order for different terms. For example, the determinant of the
2\times 2
matrix (
3.7
) is
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(M)=ad-cb
. If it is a Manin matrix then due to the last relation (
3.8
) we have
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(M)=da-bc
, but in general
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(M)
does not equal to
ad-bc
or to
da-cb
even for a Manin matrix.

An important property of the column determinant of Manin matrices is its behaviour
under
the permutation of rows and columns. The determinant of an
n\times n
Manin matrix
M
changes the sign under the transposition of two columns or rows. In the notations of Section
2.4
we have
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(^{\tau}M)=(-1)^{\tau}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits M
and
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(_{\tau}M)=(-1)^{\tau}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits M
for any
\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
. The first formula is deduced as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(_{\tau}M)=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}M^{\tau^{-1}\sigma(1)}_{1}\cdots M^{\tau^{-1}\sigma(n)}_{n}=(-1)^{\tau}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits M,

where we made the substitution
\sigma\to\tau\sigma
and used
(-1)^{\tau\sigma}=(-1)^{\tau}(-1)^{\sigma}
. The second formula follows from (
3.9
) in a similar way.

Since any submatrix of a Manin matrix
M
is also a Manin matrix, it is natural to define
k\times k
minors of
M
to be the column determinants of
k\times k
submatrices. We say that a Manin matrix has
rank

r
if there is non-zero
r\times r
minor and all the
k\times k
minors vanish for all
k>r
. In fact, it is enough to check it for
k=r+1
(see
[
4
,
5
]
). Many important properties of the Manin matrices are formulated in terms of column determinants and minors. In particular, one can construct “spectral invariants” of square Manin matrices
[
4
,
5
]
.

The theory of Manin matrices are applies to the Yangians
Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
, affine Lie algebras
\widehat{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
, Heisenberg
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}

XXX
-chain, Gaudin
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
model
[
4
]
, to elliptic versions of these models
[
34
]
etc.

Let us, for example, present the connection of the notion of
A_{n}
-Manin matrix with the Yangian
Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. Consider the
rational
R
-matrix

R(u)=u-P_{n}
. The Yangian
Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
is defined as the algebra generated by
t_{ij}^{(r)}
,
i,j=1,\dots,n
,
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geqslant 1}
, with the commutation relation

\displaystyle R(u-v)T^{(1)}(u)T^{(2)}(v)=T^{(2)}(v)T^{(1)}(u)R(u-v),

(3.10)

where
u
and
v
are formal variables and

T(u)=1+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\!\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t_{11}^{(r)}\cdots t_{1n}^{(r)}\\
\vdots\ddots\vdots\\
t_{n1}^{(r)}\cdots t_{nn}^{(r)}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\!u^{-r}
. Note that for
u=1
we have
R(u)=2A_{n}
. Substituting
v=u-1
to (
3.10
) and multiplying by
S_{n}=1-A_{n}
from the right we obtain
A_{n}T^{(1)}(u)T^{(2)}(u-1)S_{n}=0
(we understand
(u-1)^{-r}
as a series in
u^{-1}
). This relation means that the matrix
M=T(u)e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}}
is a Manin matrix over the algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}=Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\big[\big[u^{-1}\big]\big]\big[e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}}\big]
. See details in
[
4
]
.

The column determinant of
M=T(u)e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}}
is related with the notion of quantum determinant important in the theory of Yangians. Namely, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits M=\left(\mathop{\mathrm{qdet}}\nolimits T(u)\right)e^{-n\frac{\partial}{\partial u}}
, where

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{qdet}}\nolimits T(u)=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}T^{\sigma(1)}_{1}(u)T^{\sigma(2)}_{2}(u-1)\cdots T^{\sigma(n)}_{n}(u-n+1)

is the
quantum determinant
. The coefficients of the series
\mathop{\mathrm{qdet}}\nolimits T(u)\in Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\big[\big[u^{-1}\big]\big]
generate the centre of the Yangian
Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
(see
[
29
]
).

Now let us consider
(A_{n},0)
-Manin matrices
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
, where
0\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
. They are defined by the relation
M^{i}_{k}M^{j}_{l}=M^{j}_{k}M^{i}_{l}
, where
i,j=1,\dots,n
,
k,l=1\dots,m
. The
2\times 2
matrix of the form (
3.7
) is an
(A_{2},0)
-Manin matrix iff
ad=cb
,
bc=da
,
ac=ca
,
bd=db
. These are the relations (
3.8
) plus the condition
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits M=0
. Again one can see that an
n\times m
matrix is an
(A_{n},0)
-Manin matrix iff any
2\times 2
submatrix of this matrix is an
(A_{2},0)
-Manin matrix. Thus the set of an
(A_{n},0)
-Manin matrices over
{\mathfrak{R}}
coincides with the set of rank
\leqslant 1
Manin matrices over
{\mathfrak{R}}
of the size
n\times m
.

Recall that the formula (
3.9
) is valid for all matrices satisfying the cross-relations (
3.6
). This is more general case than the case of Manin matrices. To describe it we first consider a
2\times 2
matrix of the form (
3.7
) and the “transformation” (
2.19
), that is

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=a\psi_{1}+c\psi_{2},\qquad\phi_{2}=b\psi_{1}+d\psi_{2}.

Then we have
\phi_{1}\phi_{2}+\phi_{2}\phi_{1}=(ab+ba)\psi_{1}^{2}+(ad+bc)\psi_{1}\psi_{2}+(da+cb)\psi_{2}\psi_{1}+(cd+dc)\psi_{2}^{2}
. The relation
[a,d]+[b,c]=0
follows from the relations
\psi_{1}\psi_{2}+\psi_{2}\psi_{1}=0
and
\phi_{1}\phi_{2}+\phi_{2}\phi_{1}=0
only. Hence by refusing the relations
\phi_{i}^{2}=0
we have only the cross-relations (
3.6
) without commutation in columns (
3.5
). If we refuse from
\psi_{i}^{2}=0
, then we obtain the anti-commutation in rows:

\displaystyle M^{i}_{k}M^{i}_{l}+M^{i}_{l}M^{i}_{k}=0,\qquad k<l.

(3.11)

Let
\widetilde{P}_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be the matrix with the entries
\big(\widetilde{P}_{n}\big)^{ij}_{kl}=(-1)^{\delta_{ij}}\delta^{i}_{l}\delta^{j}_{k}=(-1)^{\delta_{kl}}\delta^{i}_{l}\delta^{j}_{k}
. Then the commutation relations
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+\psi_{j}\psi_{i}=0
,
i<j
(without
\psi_{i}^{2}=0
) can be written as
(\Psi\otimes\Psi)\big(1+\widetilde{P}_{n}\big)=0
, so these commutation relations define the quadratic algebra
\Xi_{\widetilde{A}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
, where
\widetilde{A}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1-\widetilde{P}_{n}\big)
. Let us conclude.

•

The matrix
M
is an
\big(A_{n},\widetilde{A}_{n}\big)
-Manin matrix iff it satisfies (
3.6
).

•

The matrix
M
is an
\big(\widetilde{A}_{n},\widetilde{A}_{n}\big)
-Manin matrix iff it satisfies (
3.6
) and (
3.11
).

•

The matrix
M
is an
\big(\widetilde{A}_{n},A_{n}\big)
-Manin matrix iff it satisfies (
3.5
), (
3.6
) and (
3.11
).

The notion of Manin matrix can be generalized to the infinite-dimensional case. Denote for any vector space
V
the operator
P_{V}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V\otimes V)
by

\displaystyle P_{V}(v\otimes v^{\prime})=v^{\prime}\otimes v,\qquad v,v^{\prime}\in V.

Let
A_{V}=\frac{1-P_{V}}{2}
and
S_{V}=1-A_{V}=\frac{1+P_{V}}{2}
be corresponding anti-symmetrizer and symmetrizer. Let us call an
(A_{V},A_{W})
-Manin operator
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
just Manin operator (over
{\mathfrak{R}}
, from
V
to
W
).

Consider the case of the space of polynomials:
V=W={\mathbb{C}}[u]
. Its tensor square can be identified with the space of polynomials of two variables:
V\otimes V={\mathbb{C}}[u_{1},u_{2}]
. In terms of this identification the operator
P_{{\mathbb{C}}[u]}
can be interpreted as the operator permuting the variables
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
, we denote it by
P_{u_{1},u_{2}}
. Let
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a basis of the space
{\mathbb{C}}[u]
. For example, one can take
v_{i}=u^{i-1}
. Acting by the operator
M
to the basis we obtain
Mv_{j}=\sum_{i}M^{i}_{j}v_{i}
(the sum is finite), so we have infinite-dimensional matrix
\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
with the column finiteness condition. The relation
A_{V}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}S_{V}=0
in the basis
(v_{i})
takes the form (
3.5
), (
3.6
), where
i,j,k,l=1,\dots,\infty
. This means that
M
is a Manin operator iff any
2\times 2
submatrix of
\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
is a Manin matrix. The set of Manin operators includes all the
n\times m
Manin matrices as well as
(A_{n},A_{{\mathbb{C}}[u]})
- and
(A_{{\mathbb{C}}[u]},A_{m})
-Manin operators (all over a fixed
{\mathfrak{R}}
).

To generalise the consideration to the case of any infinite matrix
\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
without any finiteness condition we should suppose that the operator
M
belongs to the completion of
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big({\mathbb{C}}[u]\big)
consisting of the formal infinite sums
\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}M^{i}_{j}E_{i}{}^{j}
, where
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
and
E_{i}{}^{j}v_{k}=\delta^{j}_{k}v_{i}
. This completion is the space
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathbb{C}}[u],{\mathfrak{R}}[[u]]\big)
.

3.2
\boldsymbol{q}
-Manin matrices

Let
q
be a non-zero complex number. Consider the
q
-commuting variables
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
, that is
x^{j}x^{i}=qx^{i}x^{j}
for
i<j
. By means of the notation

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(k)=\begin{cases}+1,&\text{if}\quad k>0,\\
\phantom{-}0,&\text{if}\quad k=0,\\
-1,&\text{if}\quad k<0,\end{cases}

one can write these relations as

\displaystyle x^{i}x^{j}=q^{\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(i-j)}x^{j}x^{i},\qquad i,j=1,\dots,n.

(3.12)

In the matrix form they have the form
(X\otimes X)=P^{q}_{n}(X\otimes X)
, where
X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{i}e_{i}
and
P^{q}_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
is the
q
-permutation operator acting as
P^{q}_{n}(e_{i}\otimes e_{j})=q^{-\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(i-j)}e_{j}\otimes e_{i}
, that is

\displaystyle P^{q}_{n}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}q^{\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(i-j)}E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{i}.

Its entries are
\big(P^{q}_{n}\big)^{ij}_{kl}=q^{\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(i-j)}\delta^{i}_{l}\delta^{j}_{k}=q^{\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(l-k)}\delta^{i}_{l}\delta^{j}_{k}
. It also satisfies the braid relation

\displaystyle\big(P^{q}_{n}\big)^{(23)}\big(P^{q}_{n}\big)^{(12)}\big(P^{q}_{n}\big)^{(23)}=\big(P^{q}_{n}\big)^{(12)}\big(P^{q}_{n}\big)^{(23)}\big(P^{q}_{n}\big)^{(12)}.

Since
(P^{q}_{n})^{2}=1
the matrices

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1-P^{q}_{n}\big),\qquad S_{n}^{q}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1+P^{q}_{n}\big)=1-A_{n}^{q}

are idempotents. The corresponding algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{n}^{q}}({\mathbb{C}})
is generated by
x^{i}
with the relations (
3.12
). It can be interpreted as an “algebra of functions” on the
n
-dimensional quantum space
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{q}
. The algebra
\Xi_{A_{n}^{q}}({\mathbb{C}})
is the
q
-Grassmann algebra generated by
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
with the relations

\displaystyle\psi_{i}\psi_{j}=-q^{-\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(i-j)}\psi_{j}\psi_{i},\qquad i,j=1,\dots,n.

The matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
is an
\big(A_{n}^{q},A_{m}^{q}\big)
-Manin matrix iff the following equivalent relations hold

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}S_{m}^{q}=0,

\displaystyle\big(1-P^{q}_{n}\big)M^{(1)}M^{(2)}\big(1+P_{m}^{q}\big)=0,

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}=A_{n}^{q}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}A_{m}^{q},

\displaystyle M^{(1)}M^{(2)}S_{m}^{q}=S_{n}^{q}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}S_{m}^{q}.

(3.13)

In terms of entries these relations have the form

\displaystyle M^{i}_{k}M^{j}_{k}=q^{-1}M^{j}_{k}M^{i}_{k},\qquad i<j,

\displaystyle\big[M^{i}_{k},M^{j}_{l}\big]+qM^{i}_{l}M^{j}_{k}-q^{-1}M^{j}_{k}M^{i}_{l}=0,\qquad i<j,\quad k<l.

(3.14)

The
(A_{n}^{q},A_{m}^{q})
-Manin matrices are called
q
-Manin matrices
. The Manin matrices considered in Section
3.1
are
q
-Manin matrices for
q=1
. The properties of the Manin matrices described in
[
4
,
5
]
were generalised to the
q
-case in the work
[
6
]
.

A natural generalisation of the column determinant to the case of
q
-Manin matrices is
q
-determinant
defined for a matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M)=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}(-q)^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)}M^{\sigma(1)}_{1}M^{\sigma(2)}_{2}\cdots M^{\sigma(n)}_{n},

(3.15)

where
\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)
is the number of inversions: it is equal to the number of pairs
(i,j)
such that
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
and
\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)
. It coincides with the length of
\sigma
defined as the minimal
l
such that
\sigma
can be presented as a product of
l
elementary transpositions
\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{i,i+1}
(see Appendix
A
for details).

For the
2\times 2
matrix
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)
the
q
-determinant has the from
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}M=ad-q^{-1}cb
. This matrix
M
is a
q
-Manin matrix iff

\displaystyle ca=qac,\qquad db=qbd,\qquad ad-da+qbc-q^{-1}cb=0.

In this case the
q
-determinant can be rewritten as
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}M=da-qbc
.

A general
n\times m
matrix is a
q
-Manin matrix iff any
2\times 2
submatrix of this matrix is a
q
-Manin matrix (
n\geqslant 2
). For an
n\times n

q
-Manin matrix we can change the order of columns in the expression of
q
-determinant in the following way
[
6
]
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}M^{\sigma(1)}_{\tau(1)}M^{\sigma(2)}_{\tau(2)}\cdots M^{\sigma(n)}_{\tau(n)}=(-q)^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\tau)}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}M,\qquad\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}.

(3.16)

By changing
\tau
by
\tau^{-1}
in (
3.16
) and by taking into account
\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\tau^{-1})=\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\tau)
one can write this formula in the form
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(_{\tau}M)=(-q)^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\tau)}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}M
. In contrast with the case of Section
3.1
the
q
-determinant of the matrix
{}^{\tau}M
obtained from an
n\times n

q
-Manin matrix by a permutation of rows does not related with
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}M
(the proof done for the
q=1
case does not work since
q^{\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\tau\sigma)}\neq q^{\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\tau)}q^{\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)}
). Moreover, neither
{}^{\tau}M
nor
{}_{\tau}M
are
q
-Manin matrices in general. However they are Manin matrices for another idempotents. Namely, by virtue of Proposition
2.22
they are
\big((\tau\otimes\tau)A_{n}^{q}\big(\tau^{-1}\otimes\tau^{-1}\big),A_{m}^{q}\big)
- and
\big(A_{n}^{q},(\tau\otimes\tau)A_{m}^{q}\big(\tau^{-1}\otimes\tau^{-1}\big)\big)
-Manin matrices respectively (see Section
3.3
). As we will see in Section
6.1
the
q
-determinant is a natural operation for
\big(A_{n}^{q},B\big)
-Manin matrices for any
B
, but not for
\big(B,A_{m}^{q}\big)
-Manin matrices (the symmetry of the
q
-determinant of an
\big(A_{n}^{q},B\big)
-Matrix with respect to permutation of columns depends on the choice of the idempotent
B
).

Analogously to the case
q=1
, the formula (
3.16
) is valid for any
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
satisfying the cross-relations (
3.14
), that is for any
\big(A_{n}^{q},\widetilde{A}_{n}^{q}\big)
-Manin matrix
M
, where
\widetilde{A}_{n}^{q}=\frac{1-\widetilde{P}_{n}^{q}}{2}
,
\big(\widetilde{P}_{n}^{q}\big)^{ij}_{kl}=(-1)^{\delta_{ij}}q^{\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(i-j)}\delta^{i}_{l}\delta^{j}_{k}
,
i,j,k,l=1,\dots,n
.

3.3
Multi-parametric case:
\boldsymbol{(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})}
-Manin matrices

In Section
3.2
we introduced a
q
-deformation of the polynomial algebra
{\mathbb{C}}[x^{1},\dots,x^{n}]
. The
q
-commutation of variables was defined by a unique parameter
q
. However we can consider multi-parameter deformation
[
25
]
. One has
n(n-1)/2
deformation parameters: one for each pair of variables.

We will say that an
n\times n
matrix
\widehat{q}=(q_{ij})
is
parameter matrix
iff it has entries
q_{ij}\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0\}
satisfying the conditions

\displaystyle q_{ij}=q_{ji}^{-1},\qquad q_{ii}=1.

A parameter matrix
\widehat{q}
defines the commutation relations

\displaystyle x^{j}x^{i}=q_{ij}x^{i}x^{j},

where
i,j=1,\dots,n
are arbitrary or subjected to
i<j
. It gives the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
, where

\displaystyle A_{\widehat{q}}=\frac{1-P_{\widehat{q}}}{2},\qquad S_{\widehat{q}}=\frac{1+P_{\widehat{q}}}{2},\qquad(P_{\widehat{q}})^{kl}_{ij}=q_{ij}\delta^{k}_{j}\delta^{l}_{i}.

It is immediately checked that
(P_{\widehat{q}})^{2}=1
and that
P_{\widehat{q}}
satisfies the braid relation

\displaystyle P_{\widehat{q}}^{(23)}P_{\widehat{q}}^{(12)}P_{\widehat{q}}^{(23)}=P_{\widehat{q}}^{(12)}P_{\widehat{q}}^{(23)}P_{\widehat{q}}^{(12)}.

The corresponding algebra
\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
is defined by the relations

\displaystyle\psi_{j}\psi_{i}=-q_{ij}^{-1}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}.

(3.17)

The independent relations are (
3.17
) for
i<j
and
\psi_{i}^{2}=0
.

Let
\widehat{p}=(p_{ij})
be an
m\times m
parameter matrix. An
\big(A_{\widehat{q}},A_{\widehat{p}}\big)
-Manin matrix
M
is an
n\times m
matrix over an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
satisfying

\displaystyle A_{\widehat{p}}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}S_{\widehat{p}}=0.

In terms of entries this relation can be written as

\displaystyle M^{i}_{k}M^{j}_{k}=q_{ji}M^{j}_{k}M^{i}_{k},

(3.18)

\displaystyle M^{i}_{k}M^{j}_{l}-q_{ji}p_{kl}M^{j}_{l}M^{i}_{k}+p_{kl}M^{i}_{l}M^{j}_{k}-q_{ji}M^{j}_{k}M^{i}_{l}=0.

(3.19)

These conditions are empty for
i=j
and they do not change under
i\leftrightarrow j
or
k\leftrightarrow l
, hence it is enough to check (
3.18
) for
i<j
and (
3.19
) for
i<j
,
k<l
(the relation (
3.18
) is the relation (
3.19
) for
k=l
).

Definition 3.2
.

A matrix
M
is called a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix
satisfying the relations (
3.18
), (
3.19
). A square matrix
M
satisfying these relations is called
\widehat{q}
-Manin matrix
if
\widehat{q}=\widehat{p}
.

Now let us consider the permutation of rows and columns of such matrices.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
M
be an
n\times m
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
,
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
and
\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{m}
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

•

M
is an
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix.

•

{}^{\sigma}M=\sigma M
is a
\big(\sigma\widehat{q}\sigma^{-1},\widehat{p}\big)
-Manin matrix.

•

{}_{\tau}M=M\tau^{-1}
is a
\big(\widehat{q},\tau\widehat{p}\tau^{-1}\big)
-Manin matrix.

The matrix
\sigma\widehat{q}\sigma^{-1}
has entries

\displaystyle\big(\sigma\widehat{q}\sigma^{-1}\big)_{ij}=q_{\sigma^{-1}(i),\sigma^{-1}(j)}.

(3.20)

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
2.22
and the formula

\displaystyle(\sigma\otimes\sigma)P_{\widehat{q}}\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big)=P_{\sigma\widehat{q}\sigma^{-1}},

(3.21)

which in turn is deduced by direct calculation. ∎

Remark 3.4
.

Proposition does not work for any operators
\sigma\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
and
\tau\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{C}})
since the formula (
3.21
) is not valid for general
\sigma\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
.

Let us consider more general situation: one can apply the following operations on a matrix
M
: taking a submatrix, permutation of rows or columns, doubling of a row or a column. The result of a sequence of such operations is a new matrix
N=M_{IJ}
considered below.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{l})
, where
1\leqslant i_{s}\leqslant n
and
1\leqslant j_{t}\leqslant m
for any
s=1,\dots,k
and
t=1,\dots,l
. Let
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
be an
n\times m
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
M_{IJ}
be
k\times l
matrix with entries
(M_{IJ})^{s}_{t}=M^{i_{s}}_{j_{t}}
. Let
\widehat{q}
and
\widehat{p}
be
n\times n
and
m\times m
parameter matrices and let
\widehat{p}_{II}
and
\widehat{q}_{JJ}
be
k\times k
and
l\times l
matrices with entries
(\widehat{q}_{II})_{su}=q_{i_{s}i_{u}}
,
s,u=1,\dots,k
,
(\widehat{p}_{JJ})_{tv}=q_{j_{t}j_{v}}
,
t,v=1,\dots,l
. They are also parameter matrices. If
M
is a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix then
M_{IJ}
is a
(\widehat{q}_{II},\widehat{p}_{JJ})
-Manin matrix.

Proof.

By substituting
i\to i_{s}
,
j\to i_{u}
,
k\to j_{t}
,
l\to j_{v}
to (
3.19
) we obtain the relations (
3.19
) for the matrix
M_{IJ}
with coefficients defined by the parameter matrices
\widehat{q}_{II}
and
\widehat{p}_{JJ}
. ∎

For a non-zero complex number
q
let us denote by
q^{[n]}
the
n\times n
parameter matrix with entries
\big(q^{[n]}\big)_{ij}=q^{\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(j-i)}
. Then
P_{q^{[n]}}=P_{n}^{q}
and the
\big(q^{[n]},q^{[m]}\big)
-Manin matrices are exactly the
n\times m

q
-Manin matrices. A permutation of rows or columns of a such matrix
M
gives a
\big(\sigma q^{[n]}\sigma^{-1},q^{[m]}\big)
- or
\big(q^{[n]},\tau q^{[m]}\tau^{-1}\big)
-Manin matrix respectively. In general these are not
q
-Manin matrices any more (see Section
3.2
), but they are related with the quadratic algebras isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{n}^{q}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{m}^{q}}({\mathbb{C}})
, so that they have the same properties permuted in some sense. For instance, properties of the
q
-determinant of
{}_{\sigma}M
are similar to ones of the
q
-determinant of a
q
-Manin matrix.

Let
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
be the generators of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{n}^{q}}({\mathbb{C}})
. Then the
n\times n
diagonal matrix

\displaystyle M=\begin{pmatrix}x^{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&x^{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&x^{n}\end{pmatrix}

(3.22)

is an
(A_{n}^{q},A_{n})
-Manin matrix, i.e., a
\big(q^{[n]},1^{[n]}\big)
-Manin matrix. More generally, for any
p\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0\}
the matrix (
3.22
) is a
\big((pq)^{[n]},p^{[n]}\big)
-Manin matrix.

An analogue of the
q
-determinant for a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix depends on
\widehat{q}
, but not on
\widehat{p}
. We call it
\widehat{q}
-determinant
, it is defined for an
n\times n
matrix
M
as follows
[
25
]
:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)\end{subarray}}q_{ij}^{-1}\cdot M^{\sigma^{-1}(1)}_{1}M^{\sigma^{-1}(2)}_{2}\cdots M^{\sigma^{-1}(n)}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\sigma^{-1}(i)>\sigma^{-1}(j)\end{subarray}}q_{ij}^{-1}\cdot M^{\sigma(1)}_{1}M^{\sigma(2)}_{2}\cdots M^{\sigma(n)}_{n}.

(3.23)

The products in this formula runs over all inversions of the permutations
\sigma
and
\sigma^{-1}
respectively. Hence for
\widehat{q}=q^{[n]}
the formula (
3.23
) gives the
q
-determinant (
3.15
). The
\widehat{q}
-determinant is a “generalised” determinant for the square
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrices. In particular,
q
-determinant is a “generalised” determinant for the square
\big(q^{[n]},\widehat{p}\big)
-Manin matrices.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
satisfy (
3.17
). Then

\displaystyle\psi_{i_{1}}\psi_{i_{2}}\cdots\psi_{i_{n}}=0,\qquad\text{if}\quad i_{k}=i_{l}\quad\text{for some}\quad k\neq l;

(3.24)

\displaystyle\psi_{\sigma(1)}\psi_{\sigma(2)}\cdots\psi_{\sigma(n)}=(-1)^{\sigma}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\cdots\psi_{n}\!\!\!\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\sigma^{-1}(i)>\sigma^{-1}(j)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!q_{ij}^{-1},\qquad\text{for any}\quad\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}.

(3.25)

Proof.

The relations (
3.17
) allows us to permute the factors in the left hand side of (
3.24
). In particular, one can place the factors
\psi_{i_{k}}
and
\psi_{i_{l}}
to neighbour sites. It
i_{k}=i_{l}
then
\psi_{i_{k}}\psi_{i_{l}}=0
.

Let us rewrite the formula (
3.25
) in terms of Appendix
A
. Consider the root system for the reflection group
{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
. Denote
q_{\alpha}=q_{ij}
for the root
\alpha=e_{i}-e_{j}
. Then due to (
A.2
) the formula (
3.25
) takes the form

\displaystyle\psi_{\sigma(1)}\cdots\psi_{\sigma(n)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sigma}\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{n}\prod_{\alpha\in R^{+}_{\sigma^{-1}}}q_{\alpha}^{-1}.

(3.26)

We prove the formula (
3.26
) by using induction on the length
\ell=\ell(\sigma)
. Let
\sigma=\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{\ell-1}}\sigma_{i_{\ell}}
be a reduced expression and
\tau=\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{\ell-1}}
. Then
\psi_{\sigma(1)}\cdots\psi_{\sigma(n)}=\psi_{\tau\sigma_{i_{\ell}}(1)}\cdots\psi_{\tau\sigma_{i_{\ell}}(n)}=-q^{-1}_{\tau(\alpha_{i_{\ell}})}\psi_{\tau(1)}\cdots\psi_{\tau(n)}
. Note that
\ell(\tau)=\ell-1
. From (
A.4
) we obtain
R^{+}_{\sigma^{-1}}=R^{+}_{\tau^{-1}}\sqcup\{\tau(\alpha_{i_{\ell}})\}
. Together with the induction assumption this implies the formula (
3.26
). ∎

Lemma
3.6
implies that for any
n\times n
matrix
M
such that
[M^{i}_{j},\psi_{k}]=0
we have

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\cdots\phi_{n}=\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M)\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\cdots\psi_{n},

(3.27)

where
\phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}M^{i}_{j}
. A permutation of rows or columns corresponds to a permutation of
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
or
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}
respectively:

\displaystyle\phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(^{\tau}M)^{i}_{j}\psi_{\tau^{-1}(i)},\qquad\phi_{\tau^{-1}(j)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(_{\tau}M)^{i}_{j}\psi_{i}.

(3.28)

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
\widehat{q}=(q_{ij})
and
\widehat{p}=(p_{ij})
be
n\times n
parameter matrices and
\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
. Let
M
be a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Then the “generalised” determinants of the
(\tau\widehat{q}\tau^{-1},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix
{}^{\tau}M
and of the
(\widehat{q},\tau\widehat{p}\tau^{-1})
-Manin matrix
{}_{\tau}M
have the form

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\tau\widehat{q}\tau^{-1}}(^{\tau}M)=(-1)^{\tau}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\tau(i)>\tau(j)\end{subarray}}q_{ij},

(3.29)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(_{\tau}M)=(-1)^{\tau}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\tau(i)>\tau(j)\end{subarray}}p_{ij}^{-1}.

(3.30)

More generally, the formula (
3.29
) is valid for any
n\times n
matrix
M
.

Proof.

Let
\psi_{i}
be generators of the algebra
\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
. Then Proposition
2.17
implies that the elements
\phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M^{i}_{j}\psi_{i}\in\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathfrak{R}})
satisfy the commutation relations (
3.17
) with the parameter matrix
\widehat{p}
. Due to the formula (
3.20
) the elements
\psi^{\prime}_{i}=\psi_{\tau^{-1}(i)}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{j}=\phi_{\tau^{-1}(j)}
satisfy the commutation relations (
3.17
) with the parameter matrices
\sigma\widehat{q}\sigma^{-1}
and
\sigma\widehat{p}\sigma^{-1}
respectively. From the formulae (
3.27
) and (
3.28
) we obtain

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{n}=\psi^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\psi^{\prime}_{n}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\tau\widehat{q}\tau^{-1}}(^{\tau}M),\qquad\phi^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\phi^{\prime}_{n}=\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{n}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(_{\tau}M).

(3.31)

The formula (
3.25
) for
\phi_{j}
and
\psi_{i}
with
\sigma=\tau^{-1}
takes the form

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\psi^{\prime}_{n}=(-1)^{\tau}\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{n}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\tau(i)>\tau(j)\end{subarray}}q_{ij}^{-1},\qquad\phi^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\phi^{\prime}_{n}=(-1)^{\tau}\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{n}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\tau(i)>\tau(j)\end{subarray}}p_{ij}^{-1}.

Substitution of these formulae and the formula (
3.27
) to (
3.31
) gives (
3.29
) and (
3.30
). We did not use the commutation relations of
\phi_{j}
for the proof of the formula (
3.29
), so it is valid for any matrix
M
. ∎

Let us consider the case when two rows or two columns coincide. We write some conditions leading to vanishing of the
\widehat{q}
-determinant.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
M
be a
k\times k
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Let
\widehat{q}
and
\widehat{p}
be
k\times k
parameter matrices.

•

Let
i\neq j
. If
M^{i}_{l}=M^{j}_{l}
and
q_{il}=q_{jl}

\forall\,l

(
in particular,
q_{ij}=1)
then
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M)=0
.

•

If two columns of a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix
M
coincide
(
that is
M^{l}_{i}=M^{l}_{j}

\;\forall\,l
for some
i\neq j)
then
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M)=0
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma_{ij}\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
be the transposition of
i
and
j
. Suppose
i<j
(without loss of generality). The conditions
q_{il}=q_{jl}
imply
\sigma_{ij}\widehat{q}\sigma_{ij}=\widehat{q}
and

\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\sigma_{ij}(s)>\sigma_{ij}(t)\end{subarray}}q_{\rm st}=q_{ij}\prod\limits_{s=i+1}^{j-1}(q_{sj}q_{is})=1,

so the substitution
\tau=\sigma_{ij}
to (
3.29
) gives
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M)=-\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M)
. For generic
\widehat{p}
one can similarly prove the second statement by using the formula (
3.30
) with
\tau=\sigma_{ij}
. For arbitrary
\widehat{p}
it follows from the relations (
3.27
) and
\phi_{i}\phi_{j}=\phi_{i}^{2}=0
. ∎

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
M
be an
n\times m
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Let
\widehat{q}
and
\widehat{p}
be
n\times n
and
m\times m
parameter matrices. Let
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
, where
1\leqslant i_{s}\leqslant n
and
1\leqslant j_{s}\leqslant m
for all
s=1,\dots,k
. Let
\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
,
K=(i_{\tau(1)},\dots,i_{\tau(k)})
and
L=(j_{\tau(1)},\dots,j_{\tau(k)})
.

•

We have

\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{KK}}(M_{KJ})=(-1)^{\tau}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IJ})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\tau^{-1}(s)>\tau^{-1}(t)\end{subarray}}q_{i_{s}i_{t}}.

•

If
i_{s}=i_{t}
for some
s\neq t
then
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IJ})=0
.

•

If
M
is a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix then

\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IL})=(-1)^{\tau}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IJ})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\tau^{-1}(s)>\tau^{-1}(t)\end{subarray}}p_{j_{s}j_{t}}^{-1}.

•

If
M
is a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix and
j_{s}=j_{t}
for some
s\neq t
then
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IJ})=0
.

Proof.

Note that
\tau^{-1}\widehat{q}_{II}\tau=\widehat{q}_{KK}
,
M_{KJ}={\vphantom{(}}^{\tau^{-1}}(M_{IJ})
and
M_{IL}={\vphantom{(}}_{\tau^{-1}}(M_{IJ})
, so the statements follow from Theorems
3.5
,
3.7
and Corollary
3.8
. ∎

Remark 3.10
.

The formula (
3.25
) follows from
\psi_{j}\psi_{i}=-q_{ij}^{-1}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}
,
i<j
. As consequence, we did not need the relations
\phi_{i}^{2}=0
to prove the formula (
3.30
). The relations
\psi_{j}\psi_{i}=-p_{ij}^{-1}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}
,
i<j
, define the algebra
\Xi_{\widetilde{A}_{\widehat{p}}}({\mathbb{C}})
, where
\widetilde{A}_{\widehat{p}}=\frac{1-\widetilde{P}_{\widehat{p}}}{2}
and
\big(\widetilde{P}_{\widehat{p}}\big)^{kl}_{ij}=(-1)^{\delta_{ij}}p_{ij}\delta^{k}_{j}\delta^{l}_{i}
. Hence the formula (
3.30
) is valid for any
\big(A_{\widehat{q}},\widetilde{A}_{\widehat{p}}\big)
-Manin matrix
M
. As consequence, the second statement of Corollary
3.8
is valid for these matrices if
\widehat{p}
is generic. However they are not valid for some
\widehat{p}
, so it is necessary to require
M
to be a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix. Moreover the third and forth statements of Corollary
3.9
are not valid for
\big(A_{\widehat{q}},\widetilde{A}_{\widehat{p}}\big)
-Manin matrices even if
\widehat{p}
is generic since we used Theorem
3.5
. For example, let
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
I=(1,2)
,
J=(1,1)
. Then the
q
-determinant of the matrix
M_{IJ}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&a\\
c&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M_{IJ})=ac-q^{-1}ca
. It vanishes if
M
is a
\big(q^{[2]},p^{[2]}\big)
-Manin matrix, but the cross relation
ad-q^{-1}pda+pbc-q^{-1}cb=0
is not enough for
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M_{IJ})=0
. The cross relation for the matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&a\\
c&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
itself gives
ac-qp^{-1}ca+pac-q^{-1}ca=0
. This imply
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M)=0
unless
p=-1
.

Remark 3.11
.

Let
n=k+l
,
q_{ij}=-1
for
i,j=k+1,\dots,n
,
i\neq j
, and
q_{ij}=1
for other
i
,
j
. By factorizing the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
over the relations
x_{i}^{2}=0
,
i=k+1,\dots,n
, and introducing a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-grading we obtain the free super-commutative quadratic algebra with
k
even and
l
odd generators. However the approach of Section
2
applied to this algebra does not give super-Manin matrices considered in
[
25
,
30
]
. The reason is that we suppose commutativity of
x^{i}
with entries of
M
, which should be replaced by super-commutativity in the super-case. We will consider Manin matrices for quadratic super-algebras in future works.

3.4
A 4-parametric quadratic algebra

Consider the algebra with generators
x
,
y
,
z
and relations

\displaystyle axy-a^{-1}yx=\kappa z^{2},\qquad byz-b^{-1}zy=\kappa x^{2},\qquad czx-c^{-1}xz=\kappa y^{2},

(3.32)

where
a,b,c\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0\}
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

Let
x^{1}=x
,
x^{2}=y
,
x^{3}=z
,
a_{12}=a
,
a_{23}=b
,
a_{31}=c
,
a_{ij}=a_{ji}^{-1}
,
a_{ii}=1
. Let
\varepsilon_{ijk}
be the totally antisymmetric tensor such that
\varepsilon_{123}=1
. Then (
3.32
) is equivalent to the system

\displaystyle a_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}-a_{ji}x^{j}x^{i}=\kappa\sum_{k=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{ijk}x^{k}x^{k},\qquad i,j=1,2,3.

(3.33)

These relations can be written as
x^{i}x^{j}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{3}P^{ij}_{kl}x^{k}x^{l}
, where

\displaystyle P^{ij}_{kl}=a_{ji}^{2}\delta^{j}_{k}\delta^{i}_{l}+\kappa a_{ji}\delta_{kl}\varepsilon_{ijk}.

(3.34)

The operator
P\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big({\mathbb{C}}^{3}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{3}\big)
with the entries (
3.34
) satisfies
P^{2}=1
. Hence the operator
A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}:=\frac{1-P}{2}
is an idempotent and the relations (
3.32
) define the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})
. By setting
\kappa=0
we obtain the quadratic algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
with the parameters
q_{ij}=a_{ij}^{2}
, so the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})
is a generalisation of the
3
-dimensional case of the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
considered in Section
3.3
(this is not a
\kappa
-deformation in general, see Remark
6.5
).

Let us consider some examples of Manin matrices by taking
A=A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}
as one of the idempotents. A
2\times 3
matrix

M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{3}\\
\beta_{1}&\beta_{2}&\beta_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is an
\big(A^{q}_{2},A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}\big)
-Manin matrix iff

\displaystyle q(a_{ji}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}+a_{ij}\alpha_{j}\beta_{i})=a_{ji}\beta_{i}\alpha_{j}+a_{ij}\beta_{j}\alpha_{i},

(3.35)

\displaystyle q(a_{ij}\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}+\kappa\alpha_{i}\beta_{j})=a_{ij}\beta_{k}\alpha_{k}+\kappa\beta_{i}\alpha_{j}

(3.36)

for all cyclic permutation
(i,j,k)
of
(1,2,3)
. The relation (
3.35
) is exactly the cross relation (
3.19
) for the parameters
q_{ij}=q^{\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(j-i)}
and
p_{ij}=a_{ij}^{2}
, while the relation (
3.36
) is a generalisation of the
q
-commutation (
3.18
).

A
3\times 2
matrix

M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha^{1}&\beta^{1}\\
\alpha^{2}&\beta^{2}\\
\alpha^{3}&\beta^{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is an
\big(A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa},A^{q}_{2}\big)
-Manin matrix iff the relations (
3.33
) are satisfied by the substitutions
x^{i}=\alpha^{i}
and
x^{i}=\beta^{i}
and

\displaystyle a_{ij}\big(\alpha^{i}\beta^{j}+q\beta^{i}\alpha^{j}\big)-a_{ji}\big(\alpha^{j}\beta^{i}+q\beta^{j}\alpha^{i}\big)=\kappa\big(\alpha^{k}\beta^{k}+q\beta^{k}\alpha^{k}\big)

for all cyclic permutations
(i,j,k)
of
(1,2,3)
.

4
Lax operators

Lax operators are different square matrices and endomorphisms of vector spaces arisen in the theories of integrable systems and quantum groups. We will consider Lax operators satisfying
RLL
-relations with some
R
-matrices (solutions of the Yang–Baxter equation). Different
R
-matrices give different types of Lax operators. Since many quantum groups can be defined by
RLL
-relations the Lax operators of a certain type are related with the representation theory of the corresponding quantum group. Here we consider connections between Manin matrices associated with some quadratic algebras and the Lax operators associated with the quantum groups
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
,
Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. Notice also that a connection between the
q
-Manin matrices and Lax operators associated the affine quantum group
U_{q}\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}\big)
was described in
[
6
]
.

4.1
Lax operators of
\boldsymbol{U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})}
type and
\boldsymbol{q}
-Manin matrices

A relationship between Lax operator and
q
-Manin matrices was first described by Manin, see
[
24
]
. We investigate this relationship by applying a decomposition of the corresponding
R
-matrix.

Let us first write the relations for a transposed
q
-Manin matrix. Recall that the matrices
P_{n}
defined by (
3.1
) permute the factors
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
in the following way:

\displaystyle P_{n}TP_{m}=T^{(21)},\qquad P_{n}M^{(1)}N^{(2)}P_{m}=M^{(2)}N^{(1)},

(4.1)

where
T\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
,
M,N\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
. Let us note that

\displaystyle\big(P_{n}^{q}\big)^{(21)}=P_{n}^{q^{-1}},\qquad\big(A_{n}^{q}\big)^{(21)}=A_{n}^{q^{-1}},\qquad\big(S_{n}^{q}\big)^{(21)}=S_{n}^{q^{-1}}.

(4.2)

Note also that transposition gives the same:

\displaystyle\big(P_{n}^{q}\big)^{\top}=P_{n}^{q^{-1}},\qquad\big(A_{n}^{q}\big)^{\top}=A_{n}^{q^{-1}},\qquad\big(S_{n}^{q}\big)^{\top}=S_{n}^{q^{-1}}.

(4.3)

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
. The transposed matrix
M^{\top}
is a
q
-Manin matrix iff the matrix
M
satisfies one of the following equivalent relations:

\displaystyle S^{q^{-1}}_{n}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}A_{m}^{q^{-1}}=0,

(4.4)

\displaystyle S_{n}^{q}M^{(2)}M^{(1)}A_{m}^{q}=0.

(4.5)

Proof.

The relation (
3.13
) for the
m\times n
matrix
M^{\top}
has the form
A_{m}^{q}\big(M^{\top}\big)^{(1)}\big(M^{\top}\big)^{(2)}S_{n}^{q}=0
. If we transpose the both hand sides and take into account
\big(M^{(1)}M^{(2)}\big)^{\top}=\big(M^{\top}\big)^{(1)}\big(M^{\top}\big)^{(2)}
and (
4.3
) we obtain (
4.4
). Due to (
4.2
) the permutation of tensor factors yields (
4.5
). ∎

Suppose that
q^{2}\neq-1
. Consider the
R
-matrix

\displaystyle R^{q}=R^{q}_{n}=q^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}+\sum_{i\neq j}E_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}+\big(q^{-1}-q\big)\sum_{i>j}E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{i}.

It satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation

\displaystyle(R^{q})^{(12)}(R^{q})^{(13)}(R^{q})^{(23)}=(R^{q})^{(23)}(R^{q})^{(13)}(R^{q})^{(12)}.

(4.6)

A Lax operator of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
type is an
n\times n
matrix
L\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
satisfying the
RLL
-relation

\displaystyle R^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}=L^{(2)}L^{(1)}R^{q}.

More generally, consider an
n\times m
matrix
L\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
satisfying

\displaystyle R^{q}_{n}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}=L^{(2)}L^{(1)}R^{q}_{m}.

(4.7)

Remark 4.2
.

The commutation relations for the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
can be written as three matrix relations
R^{q}_{n}L_{\pm}^{(1)}L_{\pm}^{(2)}=L_{\pm}^{(2)}L_{\pm}^{(1)}R^{q}_{n}
,
R^{q}_{n}L_{+}^{(1)}L_{-}^{(2)}=L_{-}^{(2)}L_{+}^{(1)}R^{q}_{n}
for some matrices
L_{+},L_{-}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})

[
9
,
33
]
.

By multiplying the relation (
4.7
) by
P_{n}
from the left and by taking into account (
4.1
) we obtain the equivalent relation

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}=L^{(1)}L^{(2)}\widehat{R}^{q}_{n},

(4.8)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{q}=\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}:=P_{n}R^{q}_{n}=q^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}+\sum_{i\neq j}E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{i}+\big(q^{-1}-q\big)\sum_{i<j}E_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}.

(4.9)

The matrix (
4.9
) satisfies the braid relation

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{R}^{q}\big)^{(23)}\big(\widehat{R}^{q}\big)^{(12)}\big(\widehat{R}^{q}\big)^{(23)}=\big(\widehat{R}^{q}\big)^{(12)}\big(\widehat{R}^{q}\big)^{(23)}\big(\widehat{R}^{q}\big)^{(12)},

(4.10)

which is obtained by multiplying left and right hand sides of (
3.3
) and (
4.6
).

Lemma 4.3

(
[
33
]
)
.

The matrix (
4.9
) can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{q}=q^{-1}\widehat{R}_{+}^{q}-q\widehat{R}_{-}^{q},

(4.11)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{+}^{q}

\displaystyle=\widehat{R}_{n+}^{q}:=\frac{q+\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}}{q+q^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\!E_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}\!+\!\frac{1}{q\!+\!q^{-1}}\sum_{i\neq j}\!E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{i}\!+\!\frac{1}{q\!+\!q^{-1}}\sum_{i<j}\!\big(q^{-1}E_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}\!+\!qE_{j}{}^{j}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}\big),

(4.12)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}

\displaystyle=\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}:=\frac{q^{-1}-\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}}{q+q^{-1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{q+q^{-1}}\sum_{i\neq j}E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{i}+\frac{1}{q+q^{-1}}\sum_{i<j}\big(qE_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}+q^{-1}E_{j}{}^{j}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}\big)

(4.13)

are orthogonal idempotents:

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{+}^{q}+\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}=1,\qquad\big(\widehat{R}_{+}^{q}\big)^{2}=\widehat{R}_{+}^{q},\qquad\big(\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}\big)^{2}=\widehat{R}_{-}^{q},\qquad\widehat{R}_{+}^{q}\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}=\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}\widehat{R}_{+}^{q}=0.

(4.14)

The matrix (
4.9
) satisfies the Hecke relation:

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{R}^{q}-q^{-1}\big)\big(\widehat{R}^{q}+q\big)=0.

(4.15)

Proof.

The formulae (
4.11
), (
4.12
), (
4.13
) and the first of (
4.14
) are obtained directly from the definitions of
\widehat{R}_{+}^{q}=\frac{q+\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}}{q+q^{-1}}
and
\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}=\frac{q^{-1}-\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}}{q+q^{-1}}
. Further

\displaystyle\big(q+q^{-1}\big)^{2}\big(\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}\big)^{2}=\bigg({-}\sum_{i\neq j}E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{i}+\sum_{i<j}\big(qE_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}+q^{-1}E_{j}{}^{j}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}\big)\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big(q+q^{-1}\big)^2\big(\widehat{R}_-^q\big)^2}{}=\sum_{i\neq j}E_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}-\sum_{i<j}\big(qE_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}+q^{-1}E_{j}{}^{i}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{(q+q^{-1})^2\big(\widehat{R}_-^q\big)^2=}{}-\!\sum_{i<j}\big(q^{-1}E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}+qE_{j}{}^{i}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}\big)+\!\sum_{i<j}\big(q^{2}E_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}+q^{-2}E_{j}{}^{j}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big(q+q^{-1}\big)^2\big(\widehat{R}_-^q\big)^2}{}=-(q\!+\!q^{-1})\sum_{i\neq j}\!E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{i}+\!\sum_{i<j}\!\big(\big(q^{2}+1\big)E_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}\!+\!\big(1\!+\!q^{-2}\big)E_{j}{}^{j}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big(q+q^{-1}\big)^2\big(\widehat{R}_-^q\big)^2}{}=\big(q+q^{-1}\big)\bigg({-}\sum_{i\neq j}E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{j}{}^{i}+\sum_{i<j}\big(qE_{i}{}^{i}\otimes E_{j}{}^{j}+q^{-1}E_{j}{}^{j}\otimes E_{i}{}^{i}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\big(q+q^{-1}\big)^2\big(\widehat{R}_-^q\big)^2}{}=\big(q+q^{-1}\big)^{2}\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}.

Thus we obtain
\big(\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}\big)^{2}=\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}
. Other relations (
4.14
) follows from
\widehat{R}_{+}^{q}=1-\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}
. Then the Hecke relation (
4.15
) is a consequence of
\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}\widehat{R}_{+}^{q}=0
. ∎

We see that

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{n-}^{q}=\frac{P_{n}P^{q}_{n}-P_{n}}{q+q^{-1}}=-\frac{2}{q+q^{-1}}P_{n}A_{n}^{q}.

(4.16)

This means that the idempotents
A_{n}^{q}
and
\widehat{R}_{n-}^{q}
are left-equivalent. Thus an
n\times m

q
-Manin matrix is exactly the Manin matrix for the pair
\big(\widehat{R}_{n-}^{q},\widehat{R}_{m-}^{q}\big)
. Due to (
4.2
) we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{n-}^{q}=-\frac{2}{q+q^{-1}}A_{n}^{q^{-1}}P_{n},

(4.17)

so idempotent
\widehat{R}_{n-}^{q}
is right-equivalent to
A_{n}^{q^{-1}}
.

Note that the relation
\big(\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}\big)^{2}=\widehat{R}_{-}^{q}
implies

\displaystyle\bigg({-}\frac{2}{q+q^{-1}}\bigg)^{2}P_{n}A_{n}^{q}P_{n}A_{n}^{q}=-\frac{2}{q+q^{-1}}P_{n}A_{n}^{q},

so that

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q^{-1}}A_{n}^{q}=-\frac{q+q^{-1}}{2}P_{n}A_{n}^{q},\qquad A_{n}^{q}A_{n}^{q^{-1}}=-\frac{q+q^{-1}}{2}A_{n}^{q}P_{n}.

(4.18)

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
L\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
, then the following statements are equivalent:

•

L
satisfies (
4.7
), that is
R^{q}_{n}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}=L^{(2)}L^{(1)}R^{q}_{m}
.

•

L
satisfies (
4.8
), that is
\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}=L^{(1)}L^{(2)}\widehat{R}^{q}_{m}
.

•

L
satisfies the relation

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{n+}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}=L^{(1)}L^{(2)}\widehat{R}_{m+}^{q}.

(4.19)

•

L
satisfies the relation

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{n-}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}=L^{(1)}L^{(2)}\widehat{R}_{m-}^{q}.

(4.20)

•

L
satisfies the relation

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}=L^{(2)}L^{(1)}A_{m}^{q}.

(4.21)

•

L
satisfies the relations

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}S_{m}^{q}=0,\qquad S_{n}^{q}L^{(2)}L^{(1)}A_{m}^{q}=0.

(4.22)

•

The matrices
L
and
L^{\top}
are both
q
-Manin matrices.

Proof.

By adding
qL^{(1)}L^{(2)}
to the both hand sides of (
4.8
) and by dividing by
q+q^{-1}
we obtain the equivalent relation (
4.19
). The equivalence of the relations (
4.8
) and (
4.20
) is proved similarly. Further, by using (
4.16
) one establishes the equivalence of (
4.20
) and (
4.21
). The relations (
4.22
) are obtained from (
4.21
) by multiplying by
S_{m}^{q}
from the right and by multiplying by
S_{n}^{q}
from the left respectively. Conversely, suppose that
L
satisfies the relations (
4.22
). By virtue of the formulae (
4.2
) the second of the relations (
4.22
) can be written in the form

S^{q^{-1}}_{n}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q^{-1}}=0
. Thus we have

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q}=A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)},\qquad L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q^{-1}}=A_{n}^{q^{-1}}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q^{-1}}.

These relations implies

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q}A_{m}^{q^{-1}}=A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q^{-1}}=A_{n}^{q}A_{n}^{q^{-1}}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q^{-1}}.

By using (
4.18
) one yields

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q}P_{m}=A_{n}^{q}P_{n}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q^{-1}}.

Multiplication by
P_{m}
from the right gives

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q}=A_{n}^{q}L^{(2)}L^{(1)}A_{m}^{q}.

By taking into account (
4.22
) we obtain (
4.21
) in the following way:

\displaystyle A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}

\displaystyle=A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}\big(A_{m}^{q}+S_{m}^{q}\big)=A_{n}^{q}L^{(1)}L^{(2)}A_{m}^{q}=A_{n}^{q}L^{(2)}L^{(1)}A_{m}^{q}

\displaystyle=\big(A_{n}^{q}+S_{n}^{q}\big)L^{(2)}L^{(1)}A_{m}^{q}=L^{(2)}L^{(1)}A_{m}^{q}.

Finally, by virtue of Lemma
4.1
the relations (
4.22
) mean exactly that
L
and
L^{\top}
are
q
-Manin matrices. ∎

Theorem
4.4
implies that a Lax operator of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
type is a particular case of
q
-Manin matrix. Some properties of these Lax operators can be generalised to the case of
q
-Manin matrices. The
q
-determinant (
3.15
) arose as a natural generalisation of the determinant for the Lax operators of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
type, its properties were generalised for the case of
q
-Manin matrices in
[
6
]
.

Note that the fact that the
RLL
-relation (
4.7
) is equivalent to the claim that
L
and
L^{\top}
are both
q
-Manin matrices can be proved in the same way as Proposition
3.1
(see
[
5
,
24
]
). The approach considered here explains this fact in terms of left equivalence of idempotents, which will be applied in Section
6.2
. This allows to explain why the Newton identities for the
q
-Manin matrices proved in
[
6
]
differs from the Newton identities for
L
-operators and
\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}
-Manin matrices deduced in
[
15
,
16
,
17
,
32
]
.

Remark 4.5
.

Replacement of
\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}
by the idempotent
A^{q}_{n}
in the equation (
4.8
) leads to another relations for the entries of the matrix
L
. Though
L
is also a
q
-Manin matrix in this case, the matrix
L^{\top}
is not. For
n=2
it is described in details in
[
12
]
.

Decomposition of the operator
\widehat{R}^{q}
into dual idempotents described in Lemma
4.3
gives a general idea how to connect Lax operators with Manin matrices. It can be applied to some general class of
R
-matrices.

4.2
Lax operators of Yangian type as Manin operators

The decomposition method described in Section
4.1
is generalised here to the case of the rational
R
-matrix. This gives an interpretation of the corresponding Lax matrices as a class of Manin operators.

Let
h\colon Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\to{\mathfrak{R}}
be a homomorphism from the Yangian to some algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
. It is defined by the image of the matrix
T(u)
. It has the form
L(u)=1+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\ell^{(r)}_{ij}E_{i}{}^{j}u^{-r}
, where
\ell^{(r)}_{ij}=h(t^{(r)}_{ij})
, and satisfies the
RLL
-relation

\displaystyle R(u_{1}-u_{2})L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2})=L^{(2)}(u_{2})L^{(1)}(u_{1})R(u_{1}-u_{2}),

(4.23)

where
R(u)=R_{n}(u)=u-P_{n}
. Conversely, any
n\times n
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
which has this form and satisfies (
4.23
) defines a homomorphism
Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\to{\mathfrak{R}}
. These are Lax operators of Yangian type.

We consider a more general matrix
L(u)\in{\mathfrak{R}}((u^{-1}))\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
satisfying the
RLL
-relation

\displaystyle R_{n}(u_{1}-u_{2})L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2})=L^{(2)}(u_{2})L^{(1)}(u_{1})R_{m}(u_{1}-u_{2}).

(4.24)

It could be the Lax operator of the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}

XXX
-model (which is an image of
T(u)
under some representation of
Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
multiplied by a polynomial), the Lax operator of the
n
particle Toda chain etc.

Note that the matrix
R(u)
satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation

\displaystyle R^{(12)}(u_{12})R^{(13)}(u_{13})R^{(23)}(u_{23})=R^{(23)}(u_{23})R^{(13)}(u_{13})R^{(12)}(u_{12})

(4.25)

and

\displaystyle R^{(21)}(u_{21})R^{(12)}(u_{12})=1-u_{12}^{2},

(4.26)

where
u_{ij}=u_{i}-u_{j}
.

Define an operator on
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big(\big(u^{-1}\big)\big)^{\otimes 2}={\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big(\big(u_{1}^{-1},u_{2}^{-1}\big)\big)
by the formula

\displaystyle\widehat{R}=\widehat{R}_{n}:=P_{u_{1},u_{2}}P_{n}R_{n}(u_{12})=P_{u_{1},u_{2}}(u_{12}P_{n}-1),

(4.27)

where
P_{u_{1},u_{2}}
is the operator permuting
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
, that is
(P_{u_{1},u_{2}}f)(u_{1},u_{2})=f(u_{2},u_{1})
. The relation (
4.24
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{n}L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2})=L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2})\widehat{R}_{m}.

(4.28)

The relation (
4.26
) takes the from

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{n}\widehat{R}_{n}=1-u_{12}^{2}.

(4.29)

Remark 4.6
.

The operator (
4.27
) satisfies the braid relation

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{(23)}\widehat{R}^{(12)}\widehat{R}^{(23)}=\widehat{R}^{(12)}\widehat{R}^{(23)}\widehat{R}^{(12)}.

(4.30)

It is obtained from the Yang–Baxter equation (
4.25
) via multiplication by
P_{u_{2},u_{3}}P_{u_{1},u_{2}}P_{u_{2},u_{3}}=P_{u_{1},u_{2}}P_{u_{2},u_{3}}P_{u_{1},u_{2}}
and (
3.3
) from the left. Due to the formula (
4.29
) this operator gives a representation of the group
{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
for an arbitrary
k
after some renormalization. Namely, the normalised rational
R
-matrix
\overline{R}(u)=(1-u)^{-1}R(u)
satisfies
\overline{R}^{(21)}(u_{21})\overline{R}^{(12)}(u_{12})=1
and the same Yang Baxter equation (
4.25
). Hence the operator
\widetilde{R}=P_{u_{1},u_{2}}P_{n}\widetilde{R}(u_{12})
satisfies
\widetilde{R}^{2}=1
and the braid relation (
4.30
). This implies that the map
\sigma_{a}\mapsto\widetilde{R}^{(a,a+1)}
gives a representation of
{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
on the space
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
.

Proposition 4.7
.

The operators

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{+}=\widehat{R}_{n+}:=\frac{1-u_{12}+\widehat{R}_{n}}{2},\qquad\widehat{R}^{-}=\widehat{R}_{n-}:=\frac{1+u_{12}-\widehat{R}_{n}}{2}

are orthogonal idempotents dual to each other:

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{+}+\widehat{R}^{-}=1,\qquad\big(\widehat{R}^{+}\big)^{2}=\widehat{R}^{+},\qquad\big(\widehat{R}^{-}\big)^{2}=\widehat{R}^{-},\qquad\widehat{R}^{+}\widehat{R}^{-}=\widehat{R}^{-}\widehat{R}^{+}=0.

(4.31)

Proof.

The first of (
4.31
) is obvious. By using (
4.29
) and
\widehat{R}f(u_{1},u_{2})=f(u_{2},u_{1})\widehat{R}
we obtain
\big(\widehat{R}+1-u_{12}\big)\big(\widehat{R}-1-u_{12}\big)=0
. This implies the rest of (
4.31
). ∎

Now consider

\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{n}:=\widehat{R}_{n-}\cdot\frac{1}{u_{12}}=\frac{1+u_{12}^{-1}-P_{u_{1},u_{2}}P_{n}R(u_{12})u_{12}^{-1}}{2}=\frac{1+u_{12}^{-1}+P_{u_{1},u_{2}}\big(u_{12}^{-1}-P_{n}\big)}{2}.

(4.32)

Lemma 4.8
.

The operator
\widehat{A}_{n}
is an idempotent acting on the space
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[u_{1},u_{1}^{-1}\big]\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[u_{2},u_{2}^{-1}\big]
. It preserves the subspaces
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}[u_{1}]\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}[u_{2}]
and
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[u_{1}^{-1}\big]\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[u_{2}^{-1}\big]
.

Proof.

Let us rewrite (
4.32
) in the form

\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{n}=\frac{1-P_{u_{1},u_{2}}P_{n}}{2}+\frac{1}{2(u_{1}-u_{2})}(1-P_{u_{1},u_{2}}).

The first term is obviously preserves all tree spaces. Let us prove that so does the second term. By acting by
(1-P_{u_{1},u_{2}})
on a Laurent polynomial
p(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{C}}\big[u_{1},u_{1}^{-1},u_{2},u_{2}^{-1}\big]
we obtain the Laurent polynomial
q(u_{1},u_{2})=p(u_{1},u_{2})-p(u_{2},u_{1})
. Since
q(u_{1},u_{1})=0
we have the formula
q(u_{1},u_{2})=(u_{1}-u_{2})r(u_{1},u_{2})
for some
r(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{C}}\big[u_{1},u_{1}^{-1},u_{2},u_{2}^{-1}\big]
. Hence
\frac{1}{2(u_{1}-u_{2})}(1-P_{u_{1},u_{2}})p(u_{1},u_{2})=\frac{1}{2}r(u_{1},u_{2})
is also a Laurent polynomial. If
p(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{C}}[u_{1},u_{2}]
then
r(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{C}}[u_{1},u_{2}]
. Analogously, for a Laurent polynomial
p(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{C}}\big[u_{1}^{-1},u_{2}^{-1}\big]
we obtain
q(u_{1},u_{2})=p(u_{1},u_{2})-p(u_{2},u_{1})=\big(u_{1}^{-1}-u_{2}^{-1}\big)r(u_{1},u_{2})
for some
