r(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{C}}\big[u_{1}^{-1},u_{2}^{-1}\big]
and hence
\frac{1}{2(u_{1}-u_{2})}(1-P_{u_{1},u_{2}})p(u_{1},u_{2})=-\frac{u_{1}^{-1}u_{2}^{-1}}{2}r(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{C}}\big[u_{1}^{-1},u_{2}^{-1}\big]
.

By multiplying
2\widehat{A}_{n}=1+u_{12}^{-1}+u_{12}^{-1}\widehat{R}
with itself we obtain

\displaystyle 4\widehat{A}_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big(1+u_{12}^{-1}\big)^{2}+\big(1+u_{12}^{-1}\big)u_{12}^{-1}\widehat{R}+u_{12}^{-1}\widehat{R}\big(1+u_{12}^{-1}\big)+u_{12}^{-1}\widehat{R}u_{12}^{-1}\widehat{R}

\displaystyle=1+2u_{12}^{-1}+u_{12}^{-2}+\big(1+u_{12}^{-1}+1-u_{12}^{-1}\big)u_{12}^{-1}\widehat{R}-u_{12}^{-2}\widehat{R}^{2}.

By taking into account (
4.29
) we obtain
4\widehat{A}_{n}^{2}=2+2u_{12}^{-1}+2u_{12}^{-1}\widehat{R}=4\widehat{A}_{n}
. ∎

The basis of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[u_{1},u_{1}^{-1},u_{2},u_{2}^{-1}\big]
is
\big(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}u_{1}^{k}u_{2}^{l}\big)
, where
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
i,j=1,\dots,n
, and the completion with respect to this basis is the space
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[\big[u_{1},u_{1}^{-1},u_{2},u_{2}^{-1}\big]\big]
. Since
\widehat{A}_{n}
preserves the space
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[u_{1},u_{1}^{-1},u_{2},u_{2}^{-1}\big]
its matrix in this basis satisfies the column finiteness condition introduced in Section
2.7
. However it does not satisfies the row finiteness condition since it can not be extended to the completed tensor product space
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[\big[u_{1},u_{1}^{-1},u_{2},u_{2}^{-1}\big]\big]
. Explicitly these can be seen from the formulae

\displaystyle 2\widehat{A}_{n}\big(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}u_{1}^{k}u_{2}^{l}\big)=e_{i}\otimes e_{j}u_{1}^{k}u_{2}^{l}-e_{j}\otimes e_{i}u_{1}^{k}u_{2}^{l}+e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\frac{u_{1}^{k}u_{2}^{l}-u_{1}^{l}u_{2}^{k}}{u_{1}-u_{2}},

(4.33)

\displaystyle\frac{u_{1}^{k}u_{2}^{l}-u_{1}^{l}u_{2}^{k}}{u_{1}-u_{2}}=\begin{cases}-\sum_{m=k}^{l-1}u_{1}^{m}u_{2}^{k+l-1-m},&k<l,\\
0,&k=l,\\
\sum_{m=l}^{k-1}u_{1}^{m}u_{2}^{k+l-1-m},&k>l.\end{cases}

(4.34)

For instance, for any
k\geqslant 1
the vector
\widehat{A}_{n}\big(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}u_{1}^{k}u_{2}^{1-k}\big)
has a non-zero coefficient at the term
e_{i}\otimes e_{j}u_{1}^{0}u_{2}^{0}
.

Consider the topology of the space
V={\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[u,u^{-1}\big]
defined by the neighbourhoods of
0
of the form
V_{r}=\big\{\sum_{k=-r}^{N}t_{k}u^{k}\mid N\geqslant-r,t_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big\}
. The completion of
V
with respect to this topology is the space
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big(\big(u^{-1}\big)\big)
. The corresponding completion of the space
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
is
{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V={\mathfrak{R}}\big(\big(u^{-1}\big)\big)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. The neighbourhoods
V_{r}\otimes V_{s}
defines the topology of
V\otimes V
which gives the completion
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big(\big(u_{1}^{-1},u_{2}^{-1}\big)\big)
. The operator
\widehat{A}_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V\otimes V)
is continuous with respect to this topology. In particular, it means that
\widehat{A}_{n}
is extended to the completion
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big(\big(u_{1}^{-1},u_{2}^{-1}\big)\big)
.

Thus an
\big(\widehat{A}_{n},\widehat{A}_{m}\big)
-Manin operator is an element
M
of the space
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\big[\big[u,u^{-1}\big]\big],{\mathfrak{R}}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}V\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\big[\big[u,u^{-1}\big]\big],{\mathfrak{R}}\big(\big(u^{-1}\big)\big)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big)
, satisfying

\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{n}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}=\widehat{A}_{n}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}\widehat{A}_{m}.

Note also that the operators
e^{a(\partial_{u_{1}}+\partial_{u_{2}})}=e^{a\partial_{u}}\otimes e^{a\partial_{u}}
commute with
\widehat{A}_{n}
. Hence if
M
is an
\big(\widehat{A}_{n},\widehat{A}_{m}\big)
-Manin operator then
e^{a\partial_{u}}Me^{b\partial_{u}}
is also an
\big(\widehat{A}_{n},\widehat{A}_{m}\big)
-Manin operator for any
a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}
(this follows from Proposition
2.22
generalised to the infinite-dimensional case).

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
L(u)\in{\mathfrak{R}}\big(\big(u^{-1}\big)\big)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
. The matrix
L(u)
is an
\big(\widehat{A}_{n},\widehat{A}_{m}\big)
-Manin operator iff it satisfies
RLL
-relation (
4.24
).

Proof.

Remind first that the relation (
4.24
) is equivalent to (
4.28
).
Let us multiply

\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{n}L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2})=\widehat{A}_{n}L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2})\widehat{A}_{m}

by
4u_{12}
from the right and substitute
2u_{12}\widehat{A}_{n}=1+u_{12}+\widehat{R}_{n}
,
2\widehat{A}_{m}=1+u_{12}^{-1}+u_{12}^{-1}\widehat{R}_{m}
. This gives the equivalent relation

\displaystyle\big(u_{12}-u_{12}^{-1}\big)L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2})+u_{12}^{-1}\widehat{R}_{n}L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2})\widehat{R}_{m}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\big(1+u_{12}^{-1}\big)L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2})\widehat{R}_{m}-\big(1+u_{12}^{-1}\big)\widehat{R}_{n}L^{(1)}(u_{1})L^{(2)}(u_{2}).

(4.35)

The left hand side of (
4.35
) does not change at the conjugation
T\mapsto u_{12}Tu_{12}^{-1}
while the right hand side changes the sign. This means that (
4.35
) is valid iff the both hand sides vanish.
Due to (
4.29
) the vanishing of each hand side of (
4.35
) is equivalent to (
4.28
). ∎

Let
L(u)
be an
\big(\widehat{A}_{n},\widehat{A}_{m}\big)
-Manin operator. Then
M=L(u+a)e^{b\partial_{u}}
is also an
\big(\widehat{A}_{n},\widehat{A}_{m}\big)
-Manin operator for any
a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}
. In particular, the
A_{n}
-Manin matrix
M=L(u)e^{-\partial_{u}}
considered in Section
3.1
is an
\widehat{A}_{n}
-Manin operator.

Remark 4.10
.

One can renormalise the matrix
L(u)\in{\mathfrak{R}}\big(\big(u^{-1}\big)\big)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
by multiplying it by a function in
u
. Such renormalization does not violate the
RLL
-relation (
4.24
). Hence one can suppose that
L(u)\in{\mathfrak{R}}[[u^{-1}]]\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
by multiplying
L(u)
by
u^{k}
for big enough
k
if needed. A matrix
L(u)\in{\mathfrak{R}}\big[\big[u^{-1}\big]\big]\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
satisfying
RLL
-relation is exactly an
\big(\big(\widehat{A}_{n}\big)^{-}_{\rm res},\big(\widehat{A}_{m}\big)^{-}_{\rm res}\big)
-Manin operator, where
\big(\widehat{A}_{n}\big)^{-}_{\rm res}
is the restriction of
\widehat{A}_{n}
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[u_{1}^{-1},u_{2}^{-1}\big]
. We see from the formulae (
4.33
), (
4.34
) that
\big(\widehat{A}_{n}\big)^{-}_{\rm res}
satisfies the row finiteness condition. In particular, we have the right quantum algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{(\widehat{A}_{n})^{-}_{\rm res}}
and Theorem
4.9
implies that the Yangian
Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
is a factor algebra of
{\mathfrak{U}}_{(\widehat{A}_{n})^{-}_{\rm res}}
.

Remark 4.11
.

The facts described in this Section works also for a matrix
L(u)\in{\mathfrak{R}}((u))\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
since we can consider another completion of
{\mathfrak{R}}\big[u,u^{-1}\big]
. Again, one can suppose that
L(u)\in{\mathfrak{R}}[[u]]\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
making a renormalization if needed. A matrix
L(u)\in{\mathfrak{R}}[[u]]\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
satisfying
RLL
-relation is an
\big(\big(\widehat{A}_{n}\big)^{+}_{\rm res},\big(\widehat{A}_{m}\big)^{+}_{\rm res}\big)
-Manin operator, where
\big(\widehat{A}_{n}\big)^{+}_{\rm res}
is the restriction of
\widehat{A}_{n}
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}[u_{1},u_{2}]
.

5
Minors of Manin matrices

The notion of minor (determinant of a submatrix) is an important tool in the classical matrix theory. It can be interpreted in terms of the Grassmann algebra as some coefficients. This gives minors of
(A_{n},A_{m})
-Manin matrices which we defined in Section
3.1
as column determinants of submatrices. The same can be done for
q
- and
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrices by considering the quadratic algebras
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
for
A=A_{n}^{q}
and
A_{\widehat{q}}
respectively. There is a dual notion of minors corresponding to the quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. In the case of the polynomial algebras these dual minors are written via permanent.

For a general
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
M
we define two types of minors corresponding to the homomorphisms
f_{M}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
and
f^{M}\colon\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
. In fact these minors give the graded components of these homomorphisms. As usual minors they have a good behaviour at the multiplication of Manin matrices and under permutations of rows and columns. In future works we hope to find more properties of these minors by generalising the properties of the usual minors.

5.1
The
\boldsymbol{q}
-minors and permanents

First we remind that the
q
-determinant (
3.15
) is the coefficient of proportionality for the product of the
q
-Grassmann variables
[
6
]
(see also the formula (
3.27
) for the
\widehat{q}
-version). Namely, let
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
be the generators of
\Xi_{A_{n}^{q}}({\mathbb{C}})
,
M
be an
n\times n
matrix over an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
\phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}M^{i}_{j}
, where
j=1,\dots,n
, then

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\cdots\phi_{n}=\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M)\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\cdots\psi_{n}.

More generally, let
M
be
n\times m
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
\phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}M^{i}_{j}
, where
j=1,\dots,m
. For two
k
-tuples of indices
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
we denote by
M_{IJ}
the
k\times k
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
with entries

\displaystyle(M_{IJ})_{ab}=M^{i_{a}}_{j_{b}},\qquad a,b=1,\dots,k,

where we suppose
1\leqslant i_{a}\leqslant n
and
1\leqslant j_{b}\leqslant m
. If
i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}
and
j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}
then
M_{IJ}
is a
k\times k
submatrix of
M
and
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M_{IJ})
is a
q
-analogue of minor defined in Section
3.1
. The
q
-determinants
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M_{IJ})
are the coefficients in the decomposition

\displaystyle\phi_{j_{1}}\phi_{j_{2}}\cdots\phi_{j_{k}}=\sum_{I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k})}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M_{IJ})\psi_{i_{1}}\psi_{i_{2}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}},

(5.1)

where the sum is taken over
k
-tuples
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
such that
1\leqslant i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n
.

If
M
is a
q
-Manin matrix then
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}
are also
q
-anticommuting and hence the elements
\phi_{j_{1}}\phi_{j_{2}}\cdots\phi_{j_{k}}
with
1\leqslant j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leqslant m
span the subalgebra of
\Xi_{A_{n}^{q}}({\mathfrak{R}})
generated by
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}
. This means that the
q
-determinants of submatrices of
M
are enough to describe the decompositions (
5.1
) in this case.

A dual notion to column determinant is row permanent. Recall that the
permanent
of an
n\times n
matrix
M
is

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits(M)=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}M^{1}_{\sigma(1)}\cdots M^{n}_{\sigma(n)}.

(5.2)

This is the same expression as for the determinant but without the factors
(-1)^{\sigma}
. If
M
is over non-commutative algebra the factors in this expression should be placed in a certain way. The formula (
5.2
) defines the
row permanent
of
M
(see
[
4
,
5
]
). It is invariant under a permutation of columns:
\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits(_{\tau}M)=\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits(M)
. Contrary to the determinant it does not change sign, so, in particular, the permanent of a matrix with coinciding rows can be non-zero. If
M
is an
A_{n}
-Manin matrix, then the permanent is invariant under a permutation of rows:
\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits(^{\tau}M)=\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits(M)
.

Now consider the analogues decompositions of products of
y^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{i}_{j}x^{j}
, where
x^{1},\dots,x^{m}
are generators of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{m}}({\mathbb{C}})
(for simplicity we consider the case
q=1
). Define an action of the group
{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
on
k
-tuples by the formula
\sigma(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})=\big(j_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dots,j_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}\big)
. For any tuple
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
we have (cf.
[
5
]
)

\displaystyle y^{i_{1}}\cdots y^{i_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1},\dots,l_{k}=1}^{m}M^{i_{1}}_{l_{1}}\cdots M^{i_{k}}_{l_{k}}x^{l_{1}}\cdots x^{l_{k}}=\sum_{J=(j_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant j_{k})}x^{j_{1}}\cdots x^{j_{k}}\sum_{(l_{1},\dots,l_{k})\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}J}M^{i_{1}}_{l_{1}}\cdots M^{i_{k}}_{l_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{J=(j_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant j_{k})}x^{j_{1}}\cdots x^{j_{k}}\frac{1}{|({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{J}|}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits(M_{IJ}),

where
|({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{J}|
is the order of the stabiliser subgroup
({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{J}=\{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}\mid\sigma J=J\}
. For a fixed
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
let
\nu_{j}=|\{a\mid j_{a}=j\}|
, then
|({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{J}|=\nu_{1}!\nu_{2}!\cdots\nu_{m}!
. The normalised permanents
\frac{1}{|({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{J}|}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits(M_{IJ})=\frac{1}{\nu_{1}!\nu_{2}!\cdots\nu_{m}!}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits(M_{IJ})
are analogues of the minors for the polynomial algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{m}}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathbb{C}}\big[x^{1},\dots,x^{m}\big]
.

For a general idempotent
A
we will define minors of two types and they will be coefficients of decomposition of
y^{i_{1}}\cdots y^{i_{k}}
and
\phi_{i_{1}}\cdots\phi_{i_{k}}
into sums of
x^{j_{1}}\cdots x^{j_{k}}
and
\psi_{j_{1}}\cdots\psi_{j_{k}}
respectively (up to a factor).

5.2
Dual quadratic algebras and their pairings

Consider the question of decomposition coefficients in general settings. Let
V
and
W
be vector spaces with a non-degenerate pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon V\times W\to{\mathbb{C}}
. Let them have dual bases
(v^{i})
and
(w_{i})
,
\langle v^{i},w_{j}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}
. Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be an algebra, then
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
is a free
{\mathfrak{R}}
-module with the basis
(v^{i})
. Consider a decomposition
\alpha=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}v^{i}
of an element
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
in the basis
(v^{i})
. The coefficients
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
can be calculated via the pairing:
\alpha_{i}=\langle\alpha,w_{i}\rangle
, where
\langle r\otimes v,w\rangle:=r\cdot\langle v,w\rangle

\forall r\in{\mathfrak{R}},\,v\in V
,
w\in W
.

More generally, let
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
and
A^{*}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(W)
be adjoint operators:
\langle Av,w\rangle=\langle v,A^{*}w\rangle

\forall v\in V,\,w\in W
. They act on basis vectors as
Av^{i}=\sum_{j}A^{i}_{j}v^{j}
,
A^{*}w_{j}=\sum_{i}A_{j}^{i}w_{i}
,
A^{i}_{j}\in{\mathbb{C}}
(the both sums are finite). Suppose they are idempotents:
\sum_{j}A^{i}_{j}A^{j}_{k}=A^{i}_{k}
. Consider the subspaces
\overline{V}=AV
and
\overline{W}=A^{*}W
. These subspaces are spanned by the vectors
\overline{v}^{i}=Av^{i}
and
\overline{w}_{i}=A^{*}w_{i}
respectively. We have
\langle\overline{v}^{i},\overline{w}_{j}\rangle=A^{i}_{j}
. A decomposition
\alpha=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\overline{v}^{i}
of an element
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\overline{V}
is not unique since
\overline{v}^{i}
are not linearly independent in general. However we can fix the coefficients
\alpha_{i}
by imposing some “symmetry” conditions.

Proposition 5.1
.

1.

The restriction of the non-degenerate pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon V\times W\to{\mathbb{C}}
to
\overline{V}\times\overline{W}
is also non-degenerate.

2.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\overline{V}
there is a unique
(
finite
)
sequence
(\alpha_{i})
such that
\alpha=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\overline{v}^{i}
and
\sum_{i}A_{j}^{i}\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
. The coefficients
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
fixed by these conditions can be found by the formula
\alpha_{i}=\langle\alpha,\overline{w}_{i}\rangle
.

Proof.

(1) If
w\in\overline{W}
and
\langle v,w\rangle=0
for all
v\in\overline{V}
, then for any
v\in V
we have
\langle v,w\rangle=\langle v,A^{*}w\rangle=\langle Av,w\rangle=0
and hence
w=0
. (2) Since
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\overline{V}
we have
A\alpha=\alpha
, where the action of
A
is extended on
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
by the formula
A(r\otimes v)=r\otimes Av
. Let
\alpha_{i}=\langle\alpha,\overline{w}_{i}\rangle
, then
\alpha_{i}=\langle\alpha,A^{*}w_{i}\rangle=\langle A\alpha,w_{i}\rangle=\langle\alpha,w_{i}\rangle
. Hence
\sum_{i}A_{j}^{i}\alpha_{i}=\sum_{i}A_{j}^{i}\langle\alpha,w_{i}\rangle=\langle\alpha,\overline{w}_{j}\rangle=\alpha_{j}
. Moreover,
\langle\alpha-\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\overline{v}^{i},w_{j}\rangle=\alpha_{j}-\sum_{i}A_{j}^{i}\alpha_{i}=0
, so that
\alpha-\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\overline{v}^{i}=0
. To show the uniqueness suppose that
\alpha=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\overline{v}^{i}
for some
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
such that
\sum_{i}A_{j}^{i}\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
, then we have
\langle\alpha,\overline{w}_{j}\rangle=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}A^{i}_{j}=\alpha_{j}
. ∎

Let
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be an idempotent. Consider the corresponding quadratic algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. We need to define a vector space
{{\mathfrak{X}}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and a non-degenerate pairing with
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. This implies that
{{\mathfrak{X}}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
is a graded vector space and the pairing respects the grading.

Define the algebra
{{\mathfrak{X}}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
as the graded algebra generated by the elements
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
with the “dual” quadratic commutation relations:
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{j}A^{ij}_{kl}=0
, that is

\displaystyle(X^{*}\otimes X^{*})A=0,

(5.3)

where
X^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}e^{i}
. We have the algebra isomorphisms
{{\mathfrak{X}}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{1-A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\top}}({\mathbb{C}})
. Let us introduce a pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{{\mathfrak{X}}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\times{{\mathfrak{X}}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
respecting the grading, i.e., the product
\langle x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{l}},x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{k}}\rangle
vanishes if
k\neq l
. The pairing for the elements of the degree
0
and
1
are defined as follows:
\langle 1,1\rangle=1
and
\langle x^{i},x_{j}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}
. For the higher degree elements the pairing has the form

\displaystyle\langle x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}},x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{k}}\rangle=S^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}},

(5.4)

for some
S^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. The commutation relations (
2.3
) and (
5.3
) implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{a},i_{a+1}=1}^{n}A^{lm}_{i_{a}i_{a+1}}S^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=0,\qquad\sum_{j_{s},j_{s+1}=1}^{n}S^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}A^{j_{a}j_{a+1}}_{lm}=0,

(5.5)

for all
a=1,\dots,k-1
and
l,m=1,\dots,n
. Under these conditions the formula (
5.4
) correctly defines a pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
that respects the grading.

Remark 5.2
.

In contrast to the situation described in Remark
2.32
one can not correctly define an operation on quadratic algebras by mapping an algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
to
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. It happens since the equality
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
does not imply an isomorphism of
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
(see Section
5.6
for details).

To write the formulae (
5.4
), (
5.5
) in a matrix form we introduce some conventions. Let
V
and
W
be vector spaces and
W^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,{\mathbb{C}})
. The product
\pi\xi
of elements
\pi\in V
and
\xi\in W^{*}
is usually identified with the linear operator
W\to V
acting as
(\pi\xi)(w)=\xi(w)\cdot\pi
,
w\in W
. For example,
e_{i}e^{j}=E_{i}{}^{j}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
. More generally,
e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}e^{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=E_{i_{1}}{}^{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{i_{k}}{}^{j_{k}}
, where
e^{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=e^{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{j_{k}}
and
e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{k}}
.

Let us introduce the notation
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
for
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes V
and
\beta\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes W^{*}
. The pairing acts on the first tensor factors while in the second factor the elements are multiplied as above:
\langle u\pi,v\xi\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle\pi\xi\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
, where
u\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
,
v\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}({\mathbb{C}})
,
\pi\in V
and
\xi\in W^{*}
. In particular,

\bigg\langle\sum\limits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}u_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}},\sum\limits_{j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}v_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}e^{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\bigg\rangle=\sum\limits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j_{1}\dots,j_{k}}\langle u_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}},v_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\rangle E_{i_{1}}{}^{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{i_{k}}{}^{j_{k}}.

By using this notation we can write the pairing of degree
1
elements in matrix form:

\displaystyle\langle X,X^{*}\rangle=\langle x^{i}e_{i},x_{j}e^{j}\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}E_{i}{}^{j}=1.

Consider the operators
S_{(k)}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}S^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}E_{i_{1}}{}^{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{i_{k}}{}^{j_{k}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
. For
k=1
the operator
S_{(1)}
is the
n\times n
unit matrix. The formula (
5.4
) in matrix form reads

\displaystyle\langle X\otimes\cdots\otimes X,X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*}\rangle=S_{(k)}

(5.6)

(here and below dots mean that we have
k
tensor factors).
The conditions (
5.5
) is written as

\displaystyle A^{(a,a+1)}S_{(k)}=S_{(k)}A^{(a,a+1)}=0,

(5.7)

a=1,\dots,k-1
. In terms of
P=1-2A
we can rewrite (
5.7
) in the form

\displaystyle P^{(a,a+1)}S_{(k)}=S_{(k)}P^{(a,a+1)}=S_{(k)}.

Analogously, one can define the algebra
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
generated by the elements
\psi^{1},\dots,\psi^{n}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
with the commutation relations
\psi^{i}\psi^{j}=\sum_{k,l}A^{ij}_{kl}\psi^{k}\psi^{l}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle(1-A)(\Psi^{*}\otimes\Psi^{*})=0,

where
\Psi^{*}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\psi^{i}e_{i}
. The pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{\Xi}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\Xi}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
is defined by the formula

\displaystyle\langle\psi^{i_{1}}\cdots\psi^{i_{l}},\psi_{j_{1}}\cdots\psi_{j_{k}}\rangle=\delta_{kl}A^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}},

(5.8)

where
A^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
A^{i}_{j}=\delta^{i}_{j}
,
A^{\varnothing}_{\varnothing}=1
. The formula (
5.8
) for
k=l
can be written as

\displaystyle\langle\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*},\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi\rangle=A_{(k)},

(5.9)

where
A_{(k)}=\sum\limits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}A^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}E_{i_{1}}{}^{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{i_{k}}{}^{j_{k}}
,
A_{(1)}=1
. Again, we should require

\displaystyle S^{(a,a+1)}A_{(k)}=A_{(k)}S^{(a,a+1)}=0,

where
a=1,\dots,k-1
,
S=1-A
, or, equivalently,

\displaystyle P^{(a,a+1)}A_{(k)}=A_{(k)}P^{(a,a+1)}=-A_{(k)}.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
V
and
W
be vector spaces. For any operators
T\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k},V\big)
and
\widetilde{T}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(W,({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
we have

\displaystyle\big\langle T(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X),(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\widetilde{T}\big\rangle=TS_{(k)}\widetilde{T},

(5.10)

\displaystyle\big\langle T(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*}),(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)\widetilde{T}\big\rangle=TA_{(k)}\widetilde{T}

(5.11)

(
we understand
T(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)
,
(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\widetilde{T}
,
T(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})
and
(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)\widetilde{T}
as elements of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes V
,
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes W^{*}
,
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes V
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes W^{*}
respectively
)
.

Proof.

By substituting
T(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=\sum\limits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}}Te_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
and
(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\widetilde{T}=\sum\limits_{j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{k}}e^{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\widetilde{T}
we derive

\displaystyle\big\langle T(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X),(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\widetilde{T}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}\langle x^{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x^{i_{k}},x_{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{j_{k}}\rangle Te_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}e^{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\widetilde{T}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}S^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}T(E_{i_{1}}{}^{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{i_{k}}{}^{j_{k}})\widetilde{T}=TS_{(k)}\widetilde{T}.

The formula (
5.11
) are proved in a similar way (it can be considered as the formula (
5.10
) for the algebras
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={{\mathfrak{X}}}^{*}_{1-A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\Xi}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{1-A}({\mathbb{C}})
).
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
T\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k},V\big)
and
\widetilde{T}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(W,({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
.

(a)

If
T(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=0
then
TS_{(k)}=0
.

(b)

If
(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\widetilde{T}=0
then
S_{(k)}\widetilde{T}=0
.

(c)

If
(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)\widetilde{T}=0
then
A_{(k)}\widetilde{T}=0
.

(d)

If
T(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})=0
then
TA_{(k)}=0
.

In particular, these statements are valid for
T,\widetilde{T}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
and for
T\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}
,
\widetilde{T}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
.

Proof.

The statement
(a)
are obtained from (
5.10
) in the case
W=({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
,
\widetilde{T}=1\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
. For
V=({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
,
T=1\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
we obtain
(b)
. The statements
(c)
and
(d)
are obtained from (
5.11
) in the same way. In the cases
V=W=({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
and
V=W={\mathbb{C}}
we obtain
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k},V\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(W,({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k})\big)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k},V\big)=\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(W,({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)=({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
respectively. ∎

5.3
Pairing operators

Let
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be an idempotent and
S=1-A
. We formulate some conditions on the operators (
5.6
) and (
5.9
) that guarantee non-degeneracy of the corresponding pairings.

Definition 5.5
.

Operators
S_{(k)},\,A_{(k)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
are called
pairing operators
(for the idempotent
A
) if they satisfy the following conditions:

\displaystyle A^{(a,a+1)}S_{(k)}=S_{(k)}A^{(a,a+1)}=0,

(5.12)

\displaystyle(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})S_{(k)}=(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*}),

(5.13)

\displaystyle S_{(k)}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X),

(5.14)

\displaystyle S^{(a,a+1)}A_{(k)}=A_{(k)}S^{(a,a+1)}=0,

(5.15)

\displaystyle(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)A_{(k)}=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi),

(5.16)

\displaystyle A_{(k)}(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})=(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*}).

(5.17)

We call them the
k
-th
S
-operator
and the
k
-th
A
-operator
.

The conditions (
5.14
) and (
5.16
) for
k=1
implies that
S_{(1)}=A_{(1)}=1
. For
k=2
the equations (
5.12
)–(
5.14
) and (
5.15
)–(
5.17
) have the solutions
S_{(k)}=S
and
S_{(k)}=A
respectively. Let us prove the uniqueness of solutions of these equations for any
k
(we do not prove their existence for
k>2
in a general case).

Proposition 5.6
.

The pairing operators are unique. In particular,
S_{(2)}=S
and
A_{(2)}=A
.

Proof.

Let
S^{\prime}_{(k)}
and
\bar{S}_{(k)}
be
k
-th
S
-operators for the same idempotent
A
. By applying the part
(a)
of Corollary
5.4
for
S_{(k)}=S^{\prime}_{(k)}
,
T=1-\bar{S}_{(k)}
and the part
(b)
of Corollary
5.4
for
S_{(k)}=\bar{S}_{(k)}
,
T=1-S^{\prime}_{(k)}
we obtain
S^{\prime}_{(k)}=\bar{S}_{(k)}S^{\prime}_{(k)}
and
\bar{S}_{(k)}=\bar{S}_{(k)}S^{\prime}_{(k)}
. Hence we obtain
S^{\prime}_{(k)}=\bar{S}_{(k)}
. The uniqueness of the
A
-operators follows similarly from the parts
(c)
and
(d)
of Corollary
5.4
. ∎

Proposition 5.7
.

Pairing operators
S_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
are idempotents; they are orthogonal for
k\geqslant 2
:

\displaystyle S_{(k)}^{2}=S_{(k)},\qquad A_{(k)}^{2}=A_{(k)},\qquad S_{(k)}A_{(k)}=A_{(k)}S_{(k)}=0.

(5.18)

\displaystyle(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})A_{(k)}=0,\qquad A_{(k)}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=0,

(5.19)

\displaystyle(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)S_{(k)}=0,\qquad S_{(k)}(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})=0.

(5.20)

Proof.

Due to Corollary
5.4
the formula (
5.14
) gives
S_{(k)}^{2}=S_{(k)}
, while the formula (
5.16
) implies
A_{(k)}^{2}=A_{(k)}
. Further, from (
5.12
) and (
5.15
) we derive
S_{(k)}A_{(k)}=S_{(k)}P^{(a,a+1)}A_{(k)}=-S_{(k)}A_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}S_{(k)}=A_{(k)}P^{(a,a+1)}S_{(k)}=-A_{(k)}S_{(k)}
. The formulae (
5.19
) and (
5.20
) follows from the orthogonality and (
5.13
), (
5.14
), (
5.16
), (
5.17
). For example, the relation (
5.14
) implies
A_{(k)}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=A_{(k)}S_{(k)}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=0
. ∎

Below we suppose that
S_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
are pairing operators for an idempotent
A
.
Note that in general the sum
S_{(k)}+A_{(k)}
does not coincide with the identity operator.

The following formulae generalise the equalities (
5.18
). They are proved in the same way.

Proposition 5.8
.

If
\ell\leqslant k
then

\displaystyle S_{(\ell)}^{(a,\dots,a+\ell-1)}S_{(k)}=S_{(k)}S_{(\ell)}^{(a,\dots,a+\ell-1)}=S_{(k)},\quad A_{(\ell)}^{(a,\dots,a+\ell-1)}A_{(k)}=A_{(k)}A_{(\ell)}^{(a,\dots,a+\ell-1)}=A_{(k)},

\displaystyle A_{(\ell)}^{(a,\dots,a+\ell-1)}S_{(k)}=S_{(k)}A_{(\ell)}^{(a,\dots,a+\ell-1)}=0,\qquad S_{(\ell)}^{(a,\dots,a+\ell-1)}A_{(k)}=A_{(k)}S_{(\ell)}^{(a,\dots,a+\ell-1)}=0,

where
a=1,\dots,k-\ell+1
and we used the notation
T^{(a,\dots,a+\ell-1)}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{\otimes(a-1)}\otimes T\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{\otimes(k-\ell-a+1)}
for
T\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes\ell}\big)
.

Since
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={{\mathfrak{X}}}^{*}_{S}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\Xi}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{S}({\mathbb{C}})
the conditions (
5.12
), (
5.13
), (
5.14
) interchange with the conditions (
5.15
), (
5.16
), (
5.17
) under the substitutions

\displaystyle A\leftrightarrow S,\qquad P\leftrightarrow-P,\qquad A_{(k)}\leftrightarrow S_{(k)},\qquad X\leftrightarrow\Psi^{*},\qquad X^{*}\leftrightarrow\Psi.

(5.21)

Thus if
S_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
are pairing operators for
A
then
A_{(k)}
and
S_{(k)}
are pairing operators for
S=1-A
.

The following property of the pairing operators shows their role for the quadratic algebras. Recall that we have identifications
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{1}=({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{*}
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{1}={\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Let us identify the higher graded components
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
with subspaces of
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}
and
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
by using the idempotents
S_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
respectively.

Proposition 5.9
.

We have the following isomorphisms of vector spaces:

\displaystyle\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}\cong{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k},\qquad\xi\mapsto\xi(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X),

\displaystyle S_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)\cong{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k},\qquad\ \pi\mapsto(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\pi,

\displaystyle A_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)\cong\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k},\qquad\ \pi\mapsto(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)\pi,

\displaystyle\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}A_{(k)}\cong\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k},\qquad\xi\mapsto\xi(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*}).

(5.22)

The pairings
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\times{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\times\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
correspond to the multiplication
(\xi,\pi)\mapsto\xi\pi\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

All these maps are linear. Consider the map (
5.22
). Due to (
5.14
) the image of the covector
\xi=e^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}S_{(k)}
is
e^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}S_{(k)}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=e^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}}
. Hence this map is surjective. Let us check injectivity. If
\xi(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=0
then by using Corollary
5.4
for
T=\xi
we obtain
\xi S_{(k)}=0
. Since
\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}
we have
\xi=\xi S_{(k)}=0
. The bijectivity of other maps are proved in the same way. The last statement follows from Lemma
5.3
, for example,
\big\langle\xi(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X),(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\pi\big\rangle=\xi S_{(k)}\pi=\xi\pi
. ∎

Let
d_{k}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits S_{(k)}
and
r_{k}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A_{(k)}
. Then Proposition
5.9
implies

\displaystyle\dim{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\dim{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=d_{k},\qquad\dim\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\dim\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=r_{k}.

(5.23)

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
S_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
be the pairing operators for the idempotent
A
. Then the pairings
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\times\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
defined by the formulae (
5.6
) and (
5.9
) are non-degenerate.

Proof.

Since the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
respects the grading its non-degeneracy is equivalent to non-degeneracy of the restricted pairings
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\times{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
. Due to Proposition
5.9
it is enough to prove that the pairings
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}\times S_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)\to{\mathbb{C}}
given by
(\xi,\pi)\mapsto\xi\pi
are non-degenerate, but this fact follows from the part (1) of Proposition
5.1
. The non-degeneracy of the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\times\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
is obtained in the same way. ∎

The space
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
is decomposed into the direct sum of
S_{(k)}({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
and
\big(1-S_{(k)}\big)({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
. Let
v_{\alpha}=v_{\alpha}^{S_{(k)}}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
be eigenvectors:
S_{(k)}v_{\alpha}=v_{\alpha}
for
\alpha=1,\dots,d_{k}
and
S_{(k)}v_{\alpha}=0
for
\alpha=d_{k}+1,\dots,n^{k}
.
Then
(v_{\alpha})
is a basis of
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
such that
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha\leqslant d_{k}}
and
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha>d_{k}}
are bases of the subspaces
S_{(k)}({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
and
\big(1-S_{(k)}\big)({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
respectively. Let
(v^{\alpha}=v^{\alpha}_{S_{(k)}})
be dual basis of
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}
, i.e.,
v^{\alpha}v_{\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
.
Since
v^{\alpha}S_{(k)}v_{\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
for
\beta\geqslant d_{k}
and
v^{\alpha}S_{(k)}v_{\beta}=0
for
\beta>d_{k}
we obtain
v^{\alpha}S_{(k)}=v_{\alpha}
,
\alpha=1,\dots,d_{k}
,
v^{\alpha}S_{(k)}=0
,
\alpha=d_{k}+1,\dots,n^{k}
. In coordinates we have
v_{\alpha}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}v_{\alpha}^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
,
v^{\alpha}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}v^{\alpha}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}e^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
for some
v_{\alpha}^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}},v^{\alpha}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}v_{\alpha}^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}v^{\beta}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha}
and
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n^{k}}v_{\alpha}^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}v^{\alpha}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\delta^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}\cdots\delta^{i_{k}}_{j_{k}}
.

Analogously, let
w_{\alpha}=v_{\alpha}^{A_{(k)}}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}w_{\alpha}^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
and
w^{\alpha}=v^{\alpha}_{A_{(k)}}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}w^{\alpha}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}e^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
form dual bases of
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
and
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}
such that
A_{(k)}w_{\alpha}=w_{\alpha}
for
\alpha=1,\dots,r_{k}
and
A_{(k)}w_{\alpha}=0
for
\alpha=r_{k}+1,\dots,n^{k}
. Then
w^{\alpha}A_{(k)}=w^{\alpha}
,
\alpha=1,\dots,r_{k}
and
w^{\alpha}A_{(k)}=0
,
\alpha=r_{k}+1,\dots,n^{k}
.

Proposition 5.11
.

The elements

\displaystyle x_{(k)}^{\alpha}=v^{\alpha}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}v^{\alpha}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}},\qquad\alpha=1,\dots,d_{k},

(5.24)

\displaystyle x^{(k)}_{\alpha}=(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})v_{\alpha}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}v_{\alpha}^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{k}},\qquad\alpha=1,\dots,d_{k},

(5.25)

\displaystyle\psi^{(k)}_{\alpha}=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)w_{\alpha}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}w_{\alpha}^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\psi_{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}},\qquad\alpha=1,\dots,r_{k},

(5.26)

\displaystyle\psi_{(k)}^{\alpha}=w^{\alpha}(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}w^{\alpha}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\psi^{i_{1}}\cdots\psi^{i_{k}},\qquad\alpha=1,\dots,r_{k},

(5.27)

form the dual bases of the
k
-th graded components
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
,
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
,
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
,
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
.

Proof.

This is consequence of Proposition
5.9
and the fact that
(v^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d_{k}}
,
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d_{k}}
,
(w_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{r_{k}}
and
(w^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{r_{k}}
are dual bases of

\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}
,
S_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
,
A_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}A_{(k)}
respectively. ∎

In contrast to the uniqueness the existence of the pairing operators is not guaranteed for an arbitrary idempotent
A
. In many interesting cases the pairing operators can be found explicitly. In general situation we can claim the existence of
S_{(1)}
,
A_{(1)}
,
S_{(2)}
and
A_{(2)}
only. In Section
6.3
we consider cases when the third pairing operators do not exist. Now we give necessary and sufficient conditions for existence of a pairing operator.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be an idempotent and
k\geqslant 2
. Consider the subspaces

\displaystyle V_{k}=\big\{\pi\in({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\mid A^{(a,a+1)}\pi=0\big\},\qquad\overline{V}_{k}=\big\{\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}\mid\xi A^{(a,a+1)}=0\big\},

\displaystyle W_{k}=\big\{\pi\in({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\mid S^{(a,a+1)}\pi=0\big\},\qquad\overline{W}_{k}=\big\{\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}\mid\xi S^{(a,a+1)}=0\big\}.

We have isomorphisms
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\cong V_{k}^{*}
,
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\cong\overline{V}_{k}^{*}
,
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\cong\overline{W}_{k}^{*}
,
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\cong W_{k}^{*}
given by the pairings

\displaystyle\big\langle\xi(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X),\pi\big\rangle=\xi\pi,\qquad\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*},\quad\pi\in V_{k},

(5.28)

\displaystyle\big\langle(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\pi,\xi\big\rangle=\xi\pi,\qquad\pi\in({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k},\quad\xi\in\overline{V}_{k},

(5.29)

\displaystyle\big\langle(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)\pi,\xi\big\rangle=\xi\pi,\qquad\pi\in({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k},\quad\xi\in\overline{W}_{k},

(5.30)

\displaystyle\big\langle\xi(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*}),\pi\big\rangle=\xi\pi,\qquad\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*},\quad\pi\in W_{k}.

(5.31)

•

The
k
-th
S
-operator
S_{(k)}
for the idempotent
A
exists iff the spaces
V_{k}
and
\overline{V}_{k}
are dual via the natural pairing, that is
\dim V_{k}=\dim\overline{V}_{k}
and there are bases
(v_{\alpha})
and
(v^{\alpha})
of
V_{k}
and
\overline{V}_{k}
such that
v^{\alpha}v_{\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
. This pairing operator has the form
S_{(k)}=\sum_{\alpha}v_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}
.

•

The
k
-th
A
-operator
A_{(k)}
for the idempotent
A
exists iff the spaces
W_{k}
and
\overline{W}_{k}
are dual via the natural pairing, that is
\dim W_{k}=\dim\overline{W}_{k}
and there are bases
(w_{\alpha})
and
(w^{\alpha})
of
W_{k}
and
\overline{W}_{k}
such that
w^{\alpha}w_{\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
. This pairing operator is
A_{(k)}=\sum_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}w^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Due to the symmetry (
5.21
) it is enough to prove the statements concerning
V_{k}
and
\overline{V}_{k}
.

By definition the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
is the quotient of the tensor algebra
T{\mathbb{C}}^{n}=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
by its two sided ideal
I
generated by the elements
\sum_{s,t=1}^{n}e_{\rm st}A^{\rm st}_{ij}
. In the
k
-th graded component we have
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}/I_{k}
, where
I_{k}
is a subspace of
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
spanned by the vectors
\sum_{s,t=1}^{n}e_{i_{1}\dots i_{a-1}}\otimes\big(e_{\rm st}A^{\rm st}_{i_{a}i_{a+1}}\big)\otimes e_{i_{a+2}\dots i_{k}}=A^{(a,a+1)}e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
, where
a=1,\dots,k-1
,
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}=1,\dots,n
. That is
I_{k}=\sum_{a=1}^{k-1}A^{(a,a+1)}({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
.
This definition implies that the element
x_{i}
is the class
[e_{i}]
, hence
[e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}]=[e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{k}}]=x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{k}}=(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
, so the canonical projection
p_{k}\colon({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\to{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
has the form
p_{k}\colon\pi\mapsto(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\pi
. The subspace orthogonal to
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits p_{k}=I_{k}
is
I_{k}^{\bot}=\big\{\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}\mid\xi I_{k}=0\big\}=\overline{V}_{k}
.

Note that for any subspace
U_{0}
of a vector space
U
we have an isomorphism
U_{0}^{\bot}\cong(U/U_{0})^{*}
via the pairing
\big\langle[u],\lambda\big\rangle=\lambda(u)
, where
\lambda\in U_{0}^{\bot}
and
[u]\in U/U_{0}
is the class of a vector
u\in U
. Hence we obtain the isomorphism
\overline{V}_{k}=I_{k}^{\bot}\cong\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}/I_{k}\big)^{*}=\big({\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\big)^{*}
given by the pairing (
5.29
). In the same way we obtain the isomorphism
V_{k}\cong\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\big)^{*}
.

If
S_{(k)}
exists then there are dual bases
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d_{k}}
and
(v^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d_{k}}
of the spaces
S_{(k)}({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
and
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}
. Since
S_{(k)}A^{(a,a+1)}=0
we have
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}\subset\overline{V}_{k}
. The equality (
5.23
) and the isomorphism
\overline{V}_{k}^{*}\cong{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
imply
\dim\overline{V}_{k}=\dim{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=d_{k}=\dim\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}
, hence
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}=\overline{V}_{k}
. Similarly we obtain
\dim V_{k}=d_{k}
and
S_{(k)}({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}=V_{k}
. Thus
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d_{k}}
and
(v^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d_{k}}
are dual bases of
V_{k}
and
\overline{V}_{k}
respectively.

Conversely, let
d=\dim V_{k}=\dim\overline{V}_{k}
and let
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d}
and
(v^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d}
be dual bases of
V_{k}
and
\overline{V}_{k}
. Since the vectors
v_{\alpha}
are not orthogonal to
\overline{V}_{k}
they do not belong to
I_{k}
, that is
V_{k}\cap I_{k}=0
. Moreover,
\dim I_{k}=n^{k}-\dim\overline{V}_{k}=n^{k}-\dim V_{k}
and hence
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}=V_{k}\oplus I_{k}
. This implies that the restriction of the projection
p_{k}
to the subspace
V_{k}
is an isomorphism and hence the elements
x_{\alpha}^{(k)}:=(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})v_{\alpha}
form a basis of
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
. In particular,
x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{k}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{d}c_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}^{\alpha}x^{(k)}_{\alpha}
for some
c_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{C}}
, so that
(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{d}x^{(k)}_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}
, where
\xi^{\alpha}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}=1}^{n}c_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}^{\alpha}e^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}
. By multiplying this by
A^{(a,a+1)}
from the right and taking into account (
5.3
) we obtain
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{d}x^{(k)}_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}A^{(a,a+1)}=0
. Since the elements
x^{(k)}_{\alpha}
are linearly independent, we have
\xi^{\alpha}A^{(a,a+1)}=0
for all
a=1,\dots,k-1
, so that
\xi^{\alpha}\in\overline{V}_{k}
. Multiplication of the same relation by
v_{\beta}
from the right gives
\xi^{\alpha}v_{\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
, hence
\xi^{\alpha}=v^{\alpha}
. Let
S_{(k)}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{d}v_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}
, then we have
(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})S_{(k)}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{d}x^{(k)}_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}v_{\beta}v^{\beta}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{d}x^{(k)}_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}=(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})
. Analogously we obtain
S_{(k)}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)
. Since
S_{(k)}
satisfies also
A^{(a,a+1)}S_{(k)}=S_{(k)}A^{(a,a+1)}=0
it is the
k
-th
S
-operator for the idempotent
A
. ∎

Remark 5.13
.

Theorem
5.12
means in fact that the non-degeneracies of the pairings
\overline{V}_{k}\times V_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
\overline{W}_{k}\times W_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
given by
\langle\xi,\pi\rangle=\xi\pi
imply that they induce non-degenerate pairings
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\times{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\times\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
respectively. Conversely, if there exists a non-degenerate pairing
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\times{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
or
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\times\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
then the induced pairing
\overline{V}_{k}\times V_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
or
\overline{W}_{k}\times W_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is non-degenerate but it may differ from the corresponding pairing defined by
\langle\xi,\pi\rangle=\xi\pi
. In other words, the conditions
\dim{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\dim{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
and
\dim\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\dim\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
do not guarantee the existence of
S_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
respectively (e.g., see Section
6.3
).

Remark 5.14
.

If some pairing
S
- or
A
-operators do not exist then one can consider the dual space
\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\big)^{*}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}V_{k}
or
\big(\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\big)^{*}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\overline{W}_{k}
(without a structure of algebra) instead of the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
or
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
respectively. The algebra structures on the spaces
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
are auxiliary. They are not in agreement with the algebra structures of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
, but they are used to define these spaces in a more convenient way.

5.4
Minor operators

Let
S_{(k)},A_{(k)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
\widetilde{S}_{(k)},\widetilde{A}_{(k)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)
be pairing operators for idempotents
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
\widetilde{A}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
respectively.
Let
X
,
X^{*}
,
\Psi
,
\Psi^{*}
denote the same as previous subsection. The corresponding column- and row-vectors for
\widetilde{A}
we denote by
\widetilde{X}
,
\widetilde{X}^{*}
,
\widetilde{\Psi}^{*}
,
\widetilde{\Psi}
.

By virtue of Proposition
5.9
any graded linear operator
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathfrak{R}})
is given by the formula

\displaystyle\xi(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)\mapsto\xi T_{k}\big(\widetilde{X}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}\big),\qquad\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}

(5.32)

for some operators
T_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k},({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
such that
S_{(k)}T_{k}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}=T_{k}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
. In the same time a graded linear operator
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
has the form

\displaystyle(\widetilde{X}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}^{*})\pi\mapsto(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})T_{k}\pi,\qquad\pi\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}

(5.33)

for some
T_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k},({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
such that
S_{(k)}T_{k}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}=S_{(k)}T_{k}
.

Analogously, a graded linear operator
\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
can be written as

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\Psi}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\Psi})\pi\mapsto(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)R_{k}\pi,\qquad\pi\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}

(5.34)

for
R_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k},({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
such that
A_{(k)}R_{k}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}=A_{(k)}R_{k}
, while a graded linear operator
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathfrak{R}})
has the form

\displaystyle\xi(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})\mapsto\xi R_{k}\big(\widetilde{\Psi}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\Psi}^{*}\big),\qquad\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}

(5.35)

for some operators
R_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k},({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
such that
A_{(k)}R_{k}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}=R_{k}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
.

Note that
T_{k}
and
R_{k}
can be replaced by
S_{(k)}T_{k}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}R_{k}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
respectively and this does not change the maps (
5.32
), (
5.33
), (
5.34
), (
5.35
). Hence we can always suppose
S_{(k)}T_{k}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}=T_{k}
and
A_{(k)}R_{k}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}=R_{k}
.

Denote
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
. The pairings are invariant in the following sense.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
t\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathfrak{R}})
and
t^{*}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
be the operators (
5.32
) and (
5.33
) defined by the same
T_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k},({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
satisfying
S_{(k)}T_{k}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}=T_{k}
. Let
r\colon\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
and
r^{*}\colon\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathfrak{R}})
be the operators (
5.34
) and (
5.35
) defined by the same
R_{k}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k},({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
satisfying
A_{(k)}R_{k}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}=R_{k}
. Then for any
u\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
,
v\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})
,
\nu\in\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
,
\mu\in\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})
we have

\displaystyle\langle u,t^{*}(v)\rangle=\langle t(u),v\rangle,\qquad\langle\nu,r(\mu)\rangle=\langle r^{*}(\nu),\mu\rangle,

(5.36)

where the pairings
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
,
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\times\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
,
\Xi^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\times\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
are defined by the pairing operators
S_{(k)}
,
A_{(k)}
and
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
,
\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
respectively.

Proof.

Let
u=\xi(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)
and
v=\big(\widetilde{X}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}^{*}\big)\pi
. Then by using Lemma
5.3
we have

\displaystyle\langle u,t^{*}(v)\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\xi(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X),(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})T_{k}\pi\big\rangle=\xi S_{(k)}T_{k}\pi=\xi T_{k}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}\pi

\displaystyle=\big\langle\xi T_{k}\big(\widetilde{X}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}\big),\big(\widetilde{X}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}^{*}\big)\pi\big\rangle=\langle t(u),v\rangle.

The second formula (
5.36
) is proved similarly. ∎

Recall that in Section
2.5
we introduced the homomorphisms
f_{M}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
f^{M}\colon\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
for a
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
. Their definition on generators can be written in the matrix form as

\displaystyle f_{M}(X)=M\widetilde{X},\qquad f^{M}(\widetilde{\Psi})=\Psi M.

Since homomorphisms preserve multiplications we obtain

\displaystyle f_{M}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{X}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}\big),

(5.37)

\displaystyle f^{M}(\widetilde{\Psi}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\Psi})=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}.

(5.38)

For general elements of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})
the values of the maps
f_{M}
and
f^{M}
are obtained via multiplication of these formulae by
\xi
and
\pi
respectively, so these maps have the form (
5.32
) and (
5.34
) for
T_{k}=R_{k}=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}
. In this way we obtain the following generalisation of (
2.13
) and (
2.16
).

Proposition 5.16
.

Any
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
satisfy the relations

\displaystyle M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}=S_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}_{(k)},

(5.39)

\displaystyle A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}=A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}.

(5.40)

Proof.

Note that the left hand sides of (
5.37
) and (
5.38
) are invariant under multiplication by
S_{(k)}
from the left and by
\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
from the right respectively. As consequence, we obtain

\displaystyle S_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{X}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}\big)=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{X}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}\big),

\displaystyle(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}.

Then, (
5.39
) and (
5.40
) are derived by application of Corollary
5.4
for
V={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
,
T=S_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}-M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k},V\big)
and for
W={\mathfrak{R}}^{*}\otimes({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}
,
\widetilde{T}=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}-M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(W,({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
respectively. ∎

For an arbitrary
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
define the linear operators
t_{M}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathfrak{R}})
and
t^{*}_{M}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
by the operators
T_{k}=S_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
, that is

\displaystyle t_{M}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=S_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{X}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}\big),

(5.41)

\displaystyle t^{*}_{M}\big(\widetilde{X}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}^{*}\big)=(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}.

(5.42)

Analogously, for an arbitrary matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
define the linear operators
r_{M}\colon\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
and
r^{*}_{M}\colon\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathfrak{R}})
by the operators
R_{k}=A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
, that is

\displaystyle r_{M}\big(\widetilde{\Psi}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\Psi}\big)=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{A}_{(k)},

(5.43)

\displaystyle r_{M}^{*}(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})=A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{\Psi}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\Psi}^{*}\big).

(5.44)

Remark 5.17
.

If
M
is an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix then the maps (
5.41
) and (
5.43
) are homomorphisms:
t_{M}=f_{M}
and
r_{M}=f_{M}
(however, the maps
t^{*}_{M}
and
r_{M}^{*}
are not homomorphisms). Conversely, if
t_{M}
or
r_{M}
is a homomorphism then
M
is an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix. The maps
t^{*}_{M}
and
r_{M}^{*}
are homomorphisms iff
M
is a
\big(S,\widetilde{S}\big)
-Manin matrix, where
S=1-A
,
\widetilde{S}=1-\widetilde{A}
.

For a matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
we introduce
minor operator
corresponding to a pair of operators
T\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
\widetilde{T}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)
by the formula

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{T}_{\widetilde{T}}M

\displaystyle:=TM^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{T}.

(5.45)

From (
5.41
)–(
5.44
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{S_{(k)}}_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M

\displaystyle=\big\langle t_{M}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X),\widetilde{X}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}^{*}\big\rangle=\big\langle X\otimes\cdots\otimes X,t^{*}_{M}\big(\widetilde{X}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}^{*}\big)\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}_{\widetilde{A}_{(k)}}M

\displaystyle=\big\langle\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*},r_{M}(\widetilde{\Psi}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\Psi})\big\rangle=\big\langle r^{*}_{M}(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*}),\widetilde{\Psi}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\Psi}\big\rangle.

If
M
is an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix then due to Proposition
5.16
these operators take the form

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{S_{(k)}}_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M

\displaystyle=S_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}_{(k)},

(5.46)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}_{\widetilde{A}_{(k)}}M

\displaystyle=A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}=A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}.

(5.47)

In these case these minor operators are defined by one operator only:
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
respectively, so we can denote them as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M

\displaystyle:=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}=\big\langle f_{M}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X),\widetilde{X}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}^{*}\big\rangle,

(5.48)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M

\displaystyle:=A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}=\big\langle\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*},f^{M}\big(\widetilde{\Psi}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\Psi}\big)\big\rangle.

(5.49)

Definition 5.18
.

Let
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix. Then the minor operators (
5.48
) and (
5.49
) are called
S
-minor
and
A
-minor operators
respectively. We also call them
minor operators for
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix
. Their entries

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}

\displaystyle=e^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)e_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\big\langle f_{M}\big(x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}}\big),\widetilde{x}_{j_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{x}_{j_{k}}\big\rangle,

(5.50)

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}

\displaystyle=e^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)e_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\big\langle\psi^{i_{1}}\cdots\psi^{i_{k}},f^{M}\big(\widetilde{\psi}_{j_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{\psi}_{j_{k}}\big)\big\rangle

(5.51)

are called
S
-minors
and
A
-minors of the order
k
or simply
minors for
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix
(here
\widetilde{x}_{i}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{i}
are the generators of
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})
respectively).

In terms of
y^{i}=f_{M}(x^{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{i}_{j}\widetilde{x}^{j}
and
\phi_{j}=f^{M}\big(\widetilde{\psi}_{j}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}M^{i}_{j}
the minors can be written as

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\big\langle y^{i_{1}}\cdots y^{i_{k}},\widetilde{x}_{j_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{x}_{j_{k}}\big\rangle,

(5.52)

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\big\langle\psi^{i_{1}}\cdots\psi^{i_{k}},\phi_{j_{1}}\cdots\phi_{j_{k}}\big\rangle.

(5.53)

They are coefficients in the decompositions

\displaystyle y^{i_{1}}\cdots y^{i_{k}}=\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{k}=1}^{m}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\;\widetilde{x}^{j_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{x}^{j_{k}},

(5.54)

\displaystyle\phi_{j_{1}}\cdots\phi_{j_{k}}=\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}=1}^{n}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\;\psi_{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}}.

(5.55)

In the matrix form these formulae are written as

\displaystyle(Y\otimes\cdots\otimes Y)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\big(\widetilde{X}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}\big),

\displaystyle(\Phi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi)=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big),

where
Y=\sum_{i=1}^{n}y^{i}e_{i}
and
\Phi=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\phi_{j}e^{j}
.

The formulae (
5.54
) and (
5.55
) are not decompositions by bases. However, due to Proposition
5.9
they have the form of the decompositions considered in the part (2) of Proposition
5.1
. For example, for the formula (
5.54
) we need to set
V=\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}
and
W=({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}
, while the operator
S_{(k)}
acting on
\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}
from the right plays the role of the idempotent
A
. Thus the minors of an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix
M
are the coefficients of the decompositions (
5.54
) and (
5.55
) satisfying the conditions

\displaystyle\sum_{l_{1},\dots,l_{k}=1}^{m}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}\widetilde{S}^{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}},

\displaystyle\sum_{l_{1},\dots,l_{k}=1}^{n}A^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}.

In operator form these symmetries can be written as

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{S}_{(k)}=\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M,\qquad A_{(k)}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M.

The expression for the
S
- and
A
-minors of an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix
M
depends on the pairing operators
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
only, hence they are defined if these pairing operators exist even if
S_{(k)}
and
\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
do not exist. The condition that
M
is a
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix implies the symmetry of the minor
S
- and
A
-operators with respect to the upper and lower indices:

\displaystyle S^{(a,a+1)}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M,\qquad\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{A}^{(a,a+1)}=\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M.

If
S_{(k)}
and
\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
do exist these symmetries can be written in the form

\displaystyle S_{(k)}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M,\qquad\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{A}_{(k)}=\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M.

5.5
Properties of the minor operators

The determinant of usual complex matrices is a homomorphism:
\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(MN)=\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(M)\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(N)
. The generalisation of this property to the case of
k\times k
minors is (a generalisation of the) Cauchy–Binet formula:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits\big((MN)_{IJ}\big)=\sum_{L=(l_{1}<\dots<l_{k})}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(M_{IL})\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits(N_{LJ}).

The right hand side corresponds to the product of the
A
-minor operators. This property is generalised to Manin matrices.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
S_{(k)}
,
A_{(k)}
,
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
,
\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
and
\widehat{S}_{(k)}
,
\widehat{A}_{(k)}
be the pairing operators for idempotents
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
,
\widetilde{A}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
and
\widehat{A}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big({\mathbb{C}}^{l}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{l}\big)
respectively. Let
M
and
N
be
n\times m
and
m\times l
matrices over an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
. Suppose that the entries of the first one commute with the entries of the second one:
\big[M^{i}_{j},N^{k}_{l}\big]=0
.

•

If
N
is an
(\widetilde{A},\widehat{A})
-Manin matrix then

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{S_{(k)}}_{\widehat{S}_{(k)}}(MN)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{S_{(k)}}_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widehat{S}_{(k)}}N\big).

(5.56)

•

If
M
is an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix then

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}_{\widehat{A}_{(k)}}(MN)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{\widetilde{A}_{(k)}}_{\widehat{A}_{(k)}}N\big).

(5.57)

•

If
M
and
N
are Manin matrices for
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
and
\big(\widetilde{A},\widehat{A}\big)
respectively then
MN
is a Manin matrix for
\big(A,\widehat{A}\big)
and the formulae (
5.56
), (
5.57
) take the form

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widehat{S}_{(k)}}(MN)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widehat{S}_{(k)}}N\big),

(5.58)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}(MN)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{\widetilde{A}_{(k)}}N\big).

(5.59)

Proof.

The first and second statements follow from Proposition
5.16
. For instance, the formula (
5.57
) is derived in the following way:

\displaystyle A_{(k)}(MN)^{(1)}\cdots(MN)^{(k)}\widehat{A}_{(k)}

\displaystyle=A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}N^{(1)}\cdots N^{(k)}\widehat{A}_{(k)}

\displaystyle=A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}N^{(1)}\cdots N^{(k)}\widehat{A}_{(k)},

where we used the commutativity in the form
N^{(i)}M^{(j)}=M^{(j)}N^{(i)}
,
i<j
. The last statement is implied by Proposition
2.27
and the formulae (
5.46
), (
5.47
). ∎

Now we give formulae for permutations of rows and columns. For any
n
and
\sigma\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
denote the conjugation by the element
\sigma^{\otimes k}=\sigma\otimes\cdots\otimes\sigma
as

\displaystyle\iota_{\sigma}T:=\sigma^{\otimes k}T\big(\sigma^{\otimes k}\big)^{-1},

(5.60)

where
T\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
\big(\sigma^{\otimes k}\big)^{-1}=\sigma^{-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\sigma^{-1}
.
Note that
\iota_{\sigma}S_{(k)}
and
\iota_{\sigma}A_{(k)}
are pairing operators for the idempotent
\iota_{\sigma}A=(\sigma\otimes\sigma)A\big(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma^{-1}\big)
.

Proposition 5.20
.

For any matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and operators
\sigma\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
,
\tau\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{C}})
we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{\iota_{\sigma}S_{(k)}}_{\iota_{\tau}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}\big(\sigma M\tau^{-1}\big)=\sigma^{\otimes k}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{S_{(k)}}_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\big(\tau^{\otimes k}\big)^{-1},

(5.61)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{\iota_{\sigma}A_{(k)}}_{\iota_{\tau}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}}\big(\sigma M\tau^{-1}\big)=\sigma^{\otimes k}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}_{\widetilde{A}_{(k)}}M\big)\big(\tau^{\otimes k}\big)^{-1}.

(5.62)

If
M
is an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix then

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\iota_{\tau}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}\big(\sigma M\tau^{-1}\big)=\sigma^{\otimes k}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\big(\tau^{\otimes k}\big)^{-1},

(5.63)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{\iota_{\sigma}A_{(k)}}\big(\sigma M\tau^{-1}\big)=\sigma^{\otimes k}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\big(\tau^{\otimes k}\big)^{-1}.

(5.64)

Proof.

The formulae (
5.61
), (
5.62
) follow directly from the definition (
5.45
). If
M
is an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix, then Proposition
2.22
implies that
\sigma M\tau^{-1}
is a
\big(\iota_{\sigma}A,\iota_{\tau}\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix. Thus we obtain (
5.63
), (
5.64
). ∎

In particular, Proposition
5.20
gives the minors of
{}^{\sigma}M=\sigma M
and
{}_{\tau}M=M\tau^{-1}
for a matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and permutations
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
,
\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{m}
.

Let us consider the minor operators
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M
for an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix
M
as
n^{k}\times m^{k}
matrices over
{\mathfrak{R}}
with the entries (
5.52
) and (
5.53
). They are also Manin matrices for some pairs of idempotents.

Proposition 5.21
.

Let
M
be an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix. Let
S_{(k)}
,
A_{(k)}
and
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
,
\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
are the pairing operators for
A
and
\widetilde{A}
. For any
k,\ell\geqslant 1
we have

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\otimes\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(\ell)}}M\big)\widetilde{S}_{(k+\ell)}=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k+\ell)}\widetilde{S}_{(k+\ell)}=\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k+\ell)}}M,

(5.65)

\displaystyle A_{(k+\ell)}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\otimes\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(\ell)}}M\big)=A_{(k+\ell)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k+\ell)}=\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k+\ell)}}M.

(5.66)

In particular,
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M
are
\big(1-S_{(2k)},1-\widetilde{S}_{(2k)}\big)
- and
\big(A_{(2k)},\widetilde{A}_{(2k)}\big)
-Manin matrices respectively.

Proof.

The formulae (
5.65
) and (
5.66
) follow from Proposition
5.8
. To prove the second statement one needs to put
\ell=k
in these formulae and to apply Proposition
5.16
for
2k
. ∎

Let us finally write the minor operators in terms of bases. Denote
y^{\alpha}_{(k)}=v^{\alpha}(Y\otimes\cdots\otimes Y)
,
\widetilde{x}^{\alpha}_{(k)}=\widetilde{v}^{\alpha}\big(\widetilde{X}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{X}\big)
,
\phi_{\alpha}^{(k)}=(\Phi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi)\widetilde{w}_{\alpha}
, where
\widetilde{v}^{\alpha}=v^{\alpha}_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}
and
\widetilde{w}_{\alpha}=v_{\alpha}^{\widetilde{A}_{(k)}}
are eigenvectors of the idempotents
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
and
(\widetilde{A}_{(k)})^{\top}
respectively. Let
\widetilde{d}_{k}
and
\widetilde{r}_{k}
be the ranks of
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
and
\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
. Consider matrix entries of the minor operators:

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{\alpha}_{\beta}:=v^{\alpha}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{v}_{\beta}=v^{\alpha}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{v}_{\beta},

(5.67)

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)_{\delta}^{\gamma}:=w^{\gamma}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{w}_{\delta}=w^{\gamma}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{w}_{\delta},

(5.68)

where
\alpha\leqslant d_{k}
,
\beta\leqslant\widetilde{d}_{k}
,
\gamma\leqslant r_{k}
and
\delta\leqslant\widetilde{r}_{k}
. Then the formulae (
5.54
), (
5.55
) are rewritten as

\displaystyle y^{\alpha}_{(k)}=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\widetilde{d}_{k}}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{\alpha}_{\gamma}\;\widetilde{x}^{\gamma}_{(k)},\qquad\phi_{\beta}^{(k)}=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{r_{k}}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)_{\beta}^{\gamma}\;\psi_{\gamma}^{(k)},

(5.69)

where
\alpha=1,\dots,d_{k}
,
\beta=1,\dots,\widetilde{r}_{k}
. These are decompositions by bases. They generalise the formulae given in Section
5.1
.

Note that
y^{\alpha}_{(k)}=f_{M}\big(x^{\alpha}_{(k)}\big)
and
\phi^{(k)}_{\alpha}=f^{M}\big(\widetilde{\psi}^{(k)}_{\alpha}\big)
. Thus the formulae (
5.69
) describe the homomorphisms
f_{M}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathfrak{R}})
and
f^{M}\colon\Xi_{\widetilde{A}}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
in terms of bases (
5.24
)–(
5.27
).

The formulae (
5.58
) and (
5.59
) are written in terms of bases in the form

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widehat{S}_{(k)}}(MN)\big)^{\alpha}_{\gamma}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{\widetilde{d}_{k}}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{\alpha}_{\beta}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widehat{S}_{(k)}}N\big)^{\beta}_{\gamma},

(5.70)

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}(MN)\big)^{\alpha}_{\gamma}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{\widetilde{r}_{k}}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{\alpha}_{\beta}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{\widetilde{A}_{(k)}}N\big)^{\beta}_{\gamma}.

(5.71)

5.6
Minors for left-equivalent idempotents

In contrast with the quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
their dual algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
do not coincide with the corresponding algebras for a left-equivalent idempotent
A^{\prime}
in general, but they do coincide for a right-equivalent idempotent
A^{\prime}
. Indeed, by applying Proposition
2.12
to for
A^{\top}
and
(A^{\prime})^{\top}
we see that the following 4 conditions are equivalent:
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
,
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi^{*}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
,
A
is right-equivalent to
A^{\prime}
,
S=1-A
is left-equivalent to
S^{\prime}=1-A^{\prime}
.

Proposition 5.22
.

Let
S_{(k)}
,
A_{(k)}
and
S^{\prime}_{(k)}
,
A^{\prime}_{(k)}
be the pairing operators for idempotents
A
and
A^{\prime}
. If
A
is left-equivalent to
A^{\prime}
then
A_{(k)}
is left-equivalent to
A^{\prime}_{(k)}
, while
S_{(k)}
is right-equivalent to
S^{\prime}_{(k)}
for each
k\geqslant 0
. If
A
is right-equivalent to
A^{\prime}
then
A_{(k)}
is right-equivalent to
A^{\prime}_{(k)}
, while
S_{(k)}
is left-equivalent to
S^{\prime}_{(k)}
for each
k\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

The left equivalence of
A
and
A^{\prime}
implies
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
and hence we obtain the equality
(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)A_{(k)}=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)A^{\prime}_{(k)}
. By applying Corollary
5.4
we obtain
A^{\prime}_{(k)}\big(1-A_{(k)}\big)=0
and
A_{(k)}\big(1-A^{\prime}_{(k)}\big)=0
. Then, due to Lemma
2.11
the idempotents
A_{(k)}
and
A^{\prime}_{(k)}
are left-equivalent. Analogously, we obtain
\big(1-S_{(k)}\big)S^{\prime}_{(k)}=0
,
\big(1-S^{\prime}_{(k)}\big)S_{(k)}=0
. By Lemma
2.11
this implies the left equivalence of
1-S_{(k)}
and
1-S^{\prime}_{(k)}
, which means in turn the right equivalence of
S_{(k)}
and
S^{\prime}_{(k)}
by Proposition
2.8
. ∎

Let
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be left-equivalent idempotents and let
S_{(k)}
,
A_{(k)}
and
S^{\prime}_{(k)}
,
A^{\prime}_{(k)}
be the corresponding pairing operators. From Proposition
5.22
we obtain

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{(k)}=G_{[k]}A_{(k)},\qquad S^{\prime}_{(k)}=S_{(k)}G_{(k)}

for some
G_{[k]},G_{(k)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
. Then, by means of Proposition
5.9
the identification
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
induces an isomorphism
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}\cong\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S^{\prime}_{(k)}
. Explicitly it has the form

\displaystyle\xi\mapsto\xi^{\prime}=\xi G_{(k)}=\xi S^{\prime}_{(k)},\qquad\xi\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}.

Indeed, we have
\xi^{\prime}\in\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S^{\prime}_{(k)}
and
\xi^{\prime}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=\xi S^{\prime}_{(k)}(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=\xi(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)
. The inverse map is
\xi^{\prime}\mapsto\xi^{\prime}G_{(k)}^{-1}=\xi S_{(k)}
.
Analogously, the identification
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
gives
A_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)\cong A^{\prime}_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
,

\displaystyle\pi\mapsto\pi^{\prime}=G_{[k]}\pi=A^{\prime}_{(k)}\pi,\qquad\pi\in A_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big).

Proposition
2.10
implies the equalities of subspaces:
S_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)=S^{\prime}_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
