\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}A_{(k)}=\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}A^{\prime}_{(k)}
. Proposition
5.9
in turn gives the isomorphisms of vector spaces
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\cong{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
and
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\cong\Xi^{*}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
(these are not homomorphisms of algebras). They are given by the formulae
x^{A}_{i_{1}}\cdots x^{A}_{i_{k}}\mapsto x^{A^{\prime}}_{i_{1}}\cdots x^{A^{\prime}}_{i_{k}}
and
\psi_{A}^{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{A}^{i_{k}}\mapsto\psi_{A^{\prime}}^{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{A^{\prime}}^{i_{k}}
, where
x^{A}_{i}
,
x^{A^{\prime}}_{i}
,
\psi^{i}_{A}
and
\psi^{i}_{A^{\prime}}
are the generators of the algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
,
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
,
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi^{*}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})
respectively.

Let
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d_{k}}
,
(v^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{d_{k}}
and
(w_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{r_{k}}
,
(w^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{r_{k}}
be dual bases of
S_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)
,
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S_{(k)}
,
A_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}A_{(k)}
respectively. Then

\displaystyle v^{\prime}_{\alpha}=v_{\alpha},\qquad(v^{\prime})^{\alpha}=v^{\alpha}G_{(k)}=v^{\alpha}S^{\prime}_{(k)},\qquad\alpha=1,\dots,d_{k},

\displaystyle(w^{\prime})^{\alpha}=w^{\alpha},\qquad w^{\prime}_{\alpha}=G_{[k]}w_{\alpha}=A^{\prime}_{(k)}w_{\alpha},\qquad\alpha=1,\dots,r_{k},

(5.72)

are dual bases of
S^{\prime}_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)
,
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}S^{\prime}_{(k)}
,
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}A^{\prime}_{(k)}
and
A^{\prime}_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)
.

By substituting
(v^{\prime})^{\alpha}
and
w^{\prime}_{\alpha}
to the formulae (
5.24
) and (
5.26
) we obtain the same bases of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}={\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
and
\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
:

\displaystyle(v^{\prime})^{\alpha}(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})=v^{\alpha}S^{\prime}_{(k)}(X^{*}\otimes\cdots X^{*})=v^{\alpha}(X^{*}\otimes\cdots X^{*})=x_{(k)}^{\alpha},

\displaystyle(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)w^{\prime}_{\alpha}=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)A^{\prime}_{(k)}w_{\alpha}=(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)w_{\alpha}=\psi_{\alpha}^{(k)}.

(5.73)

Note that the bases of
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
defined by the formula (
5.25
) for
v^{\prime}_{\alpha}=v_{\alpha}
are not identified since they are elements of different algebras. The same is valid for the bases (
5.27
) of
\Xi_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
.

Let
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
,
\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
and
\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}
,
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{(k)}
be the pairing operators for two left-equivalent idempotents
\widetilde{A},\widetilde{A}^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
. We have
\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}=\widetilde{S}_{(k)}\widetilde{G}_{(k)}
and
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{(k)}=\widetilde{G}_{[k]}\widetilde{A}_{[k]}
for some matrices
\widetilde{G}_{(k)},\widetilde{G}_{[k]}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)
. Let
M
be an
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix. Due to Proposition
2.21
this means that
M
is an
\big(A^{\prime},\widetilde{A}^{\prime}\big)
-Manin matrix. We can consider different minor operators for
M
, but they are related by a multiplication of a complex matrix in the following way.

Proposition 5.23
.

For any
\big(A,\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}}M=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{G}_{(k)}=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M=G_{[k]}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)=A^{\prime}_{(k)}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big).

(5.74)

Proof.

The formulae are obtained by the definitions of the minors:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}}M=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}\widetilde{G}_{(k)}=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{G}_{(k)},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}}M=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M=A^{\prime}_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}=G_{[k]}A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}=G_{[k]}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big),

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M=A^{\prime}_{(k)}A^{\prime}_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}=A^{\prime}_{(k)}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big).

∎

Note that an
n^{k}\times m^{k}
matrix is a
\big(A_{(2k)},\widetilde{A}_{(2k)}\big)
-Manin matrix iff it is an
\big(A^{\prime}_{(2k)},\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{(2k)}\big)
-Manin matrix. It is a
\big(1-S_{(k)},1-\widetilde{S}_{(k)}\big)
-Manin matrix iff it is a
\big(1-S^{\prime}_{(2k)},1-\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(2k)}\big)
-Manin matrix. Due to Proposition
5.21
the both matrices
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}}M
are
\big(1-S_{(k)},1-\widetilde{S}_{(k)}\big)
-Manin matrices as well as
\big(1-S^{\prime}_{(2k)},1-\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(2k)}\big)
-Manin matrices. They are related by the change of basis in the space
({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}
corresponding to the matrix
\widetilde{G}_{(k)}^{-1}
(see Section
2.4
). In the same way the matrices
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M
are
\big(A_{(k)},\widetilde{A}_{(k)}\big)
-Manin matrices as well as
\big(A^{\prime}_{(2k)},\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{(2k)}\big)
-Manin matrices, and they are related by the change of basis in the space
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
corresponding to the matrix
G_{[k]}
.

Let
(\widetilde{v}_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{\widetilde{d}_{k}}
,
(\widetilde{v}^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{\widetilde{d}_{k}}
and
(\widetilde{w}_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{\widetilde{r}_{k}}
,
(\widetilde{w}^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{\widetilde{r}_{k}}
be dual bases of
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)
,
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
,
\widetilde{A}_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{m})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}
. Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\widetilde{v}_{\alpha},\qquad(\widetilde{v}^{\prime})^{\alpha}=\widetilde{v}^{\alpha}\widetilde{G}_{(k)}=\widetilde{v}^{\alpha}\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)},\qquad\alpha=1,\dots,\widetilde{d}_{k},

\displaystyle(\widetilde{w}^{\prime})^{\alpha}=\widetilde{w}^{\alpha},\qquad\widetilde{w}^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\widetilde{G}_{[k]}\widetilde{w}_{\alpha}=\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{(k)}\widetilde{w}^{\alpha},\qquad\alpha=1,\dots,\widetilde{r}_{k}.

Proposition 5.24
.

Consider the entries of minor operators (
5.67
) and (
5.68
) in the bases defined above:

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{\alpha}_{\beta}=v^{\alpha}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{v}_{\beta},\qquad\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)^{\alpha}_{\beta}=(v^{\prime})^{\alpha}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{v}^{\prime}_{\beta},

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{\gamma}_{\delta}=w^{\gamma}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{w}_{\delta},\qquad\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)^{\gamma}_{\delta}=(w^{\prime})^{\gamma}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{w}^{\prime}_{\delta}.

These entries coincide:

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{\alpha}_{\beta}=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)^{\alpha}_{\beta},\qquad\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{\gamma}_{\delta}=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)^{\gamma}_{\delta}.

Proof.

By using
\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{(k)}=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)
, Proposition
5.23
and
(w^{\prime})^{\gamma}A^{\prime}_{(k)}=(w^{\prime})^{\gamma}
we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)_{\delta}^{\gamma}

\displaystyle=(w^{\prime})^{\gamma}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{w}^{\prime}_{\delta}=(w^{\prime})^{\gamma}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{(k)}\widetilde{w}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=(w^{\prime})^{\gamma}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{w}_{\delta}=(w^{\prime})^{\gamma}A^{\prime}_{(k)}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{w}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=(w^{\prime})^{\gamma}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{w}_{\delta}=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)_{\delta}^{\gamma}.

The equality of the entries of the
S
-minors is proved similarly. ∎

5.7
Construction of pairing operators

Theorem
5.12
gives a formula for the pairing operators via dual bases. However there is basisless method of construction. It uses the representation theory of groups and algebras, for which some appropriate idempotents are already constructed.

The operators
A^{(1,2)},A^{(2,3)},\dots,A^{(k-1,k)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
generate a subalgebra
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
of the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
. Equivalently, the algebra
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
can be defined as a subalgebra of
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
generates by
P^{(a,a+1)}
or
S^{(a,a+1)}
.
Let
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{+}
and
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{-}
be ideals of
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
generated by
A^{(a,a+1)}
and
S^{(a,a+1)}
respectively.
The commutation relations for the algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
,
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
,
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{A}^{*}({\mathbb{C}})
imply

\displaystyle(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})T=0,\qquad T(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=0\qquad\forall\,T\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{+},

\displaystyle(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)T=0,\qquad T(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})=0\qquad\forall\,T\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{-}.

(5.75)

Thus, if
S_{(k)}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
satisfies (
5.12
) and
1-S_{(k)}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{+}
then
S_{(k)}
is the
k
-th
S
-operator. Analogously, an operator
A_{(k)}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
satisfying (
5.15
) and
1-A_{(k)}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{-}
is the
k
-th
A
-operator. If the algebra
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
admits an involution
\omega\colon P^{(a,a+1)}\mapsto-P^{(a,a+1)}
then
A_{(k)}=\omega\big(S_{(k)}\big)
, so by using this involution one can obtain the
k
-th
A
-operator from the
k
-th
S
-operator and vice versa.

Let us consider a case when the algebra
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
is a group algebra of a finite group.

Proposition 5.25
.

Let
G^{+}_{k}
and
G^{-}_{k}
be subgroups of
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
generated by the operators
P^{(1,2)},P^{(2,3)},\dots,P^{(k-1,k)}
and
-P^{(1,2)},-P^{(2,3)},\dots,-P^{(k-1,k)}
respectively. The group
G^{+}_{k}
is finite iff the group
G^{-}_{k}
is finite. In this case the pairing operators exist and have the form

\displaystyle S_{(k)}=\frac{1}{|G^{+}_{k}|}\sum_{g\in G^{+}_{k}}g,\qquad A_{(k)}=\frac{1}{|G^{-}_{k}|}\sum_{g\in G^{-}_{k}}g.

(5.76)

Proof.

Suppose
G_{k}^{+}
is finite. If
-1\in G_{k}^{-}
then
G_{k}^{-}=G_{k}^{+}\cup\big({-}G_{k}^{+}\big)
, so that
G_{k}^{-}
is also finite (more precisely we have
G_{k}^{-}=G_{k}^{+}
if
-1\in G_{k}^{+}
or
G_{k}^{-}=G_{k}^{+}\sqcup\big({-}G_{k}^{+}\big)
if
-1\notin G_{k}^{+}
). Hence we can suppose
-1\notin G_{k}^{-}
. For brevity we denote
g_{a}=P^{(a,a+1)}
and
g^{*}_{a}=-g_{a}=-P^{(a,a+1)}
. The finiteness of the group
G_{k}^{+}
means that there exists
N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
such that any element
g\in G_{k}^{+}
can be written as
g_{a_{1}}g_{a_{2}}\cdots g_{a_{m}}
with
m\leqslant N
. Then any product
g^{*}_{a_{1}}\cdots g^{*}_{a_{N+1}}=(-1)^{N+1}g_{a_{1}}\cdots g_{a_{N+1}}
can be written as
(-1)^{N+1}g_{a^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots g_{a^{\prime}_{m}}=(-1)^{N+1+m}g^{*}_{a^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots g^{*}_{a^{\prime}_{m}}
for some
m\leqslant N
. By using
(g^{*}_{s})^{2}=1
we obtain
(-1)^{N+1+m}=g^{*}_{a_{1}}\cdots g^{*}_{a_{N+1}}g^{*}_{a^{\prime}_{m}}\cdots g^{*}_{a^{\prime}_{1}}\in G_{k}^{-}
. Since
-1\notin G_{k}^{-}
we have
(-1)^{N+1+m}=1
, so for any
a_{1},\dots,a_{N},a_{N+1}
there exist
m\leqslant N
and
a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{m}
such that
g^{*}_{a_{1}}\cdots g^{*}_{a_{N+1}}=g^{*}_{a^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots g^{*}_{a^{\prime}_{m}}
. This means that by induction we can write any element of
G_{k}^{-}
as a product
g^{*}_{a_{1}}\cdots g^{*}_{a_{m}}
for some
m\leqslant N
, which implies the finiteness of the group
G_{k}^{-}
. The converse implication is obtained by changing the sign of
P
.

We have
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}={\mathbb{C}}[G_{k}^{+}]={\mathbb{C}}[G_{k}^{-}]
. Moreover,
1-g\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\pm}
for any
g\in G_{k}^{\pm}
. Indeed, for
g=\pm P^{(a,a+1)}
it follows from the definition of the ideals
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\pm}
; further, if
1-g
and
1-g^{\prime}
belongs to
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\pm}
, then
1-gg^{\prime}=(1-g)+(1-g^{\prime})-(1-g)(1-g^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\pm}
. Note that the operators (
5.76
) satisfy
gS_{(k)}=S_{(k)}g=S_{(k)}

\;\forall\,g\in G_{k}^{+}
and
AS_{(k)}=A_{(k)}g=A_{(k)}

\forall g\in G_{k}^{-}
, hence conditions (
5.12
) and (
5.15
) are valid. Since
1-S_{(k)}=\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{+}|}\sum_{g\in G_{k}^{+}}(1-g)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{+}
and
1-A_{(k)}=\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{-}|}\sum_{g\in G_{k}^{-}}(1-g)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{-}
, the operators
S_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
are the pairing operators for the idempotent
A
. ∎

At
k=2
the groups consist of exactly two elements:
G_{2}^{+}=\{1,P\}
,
G^{-}_{2}=\{1,-P\}
. Hence we obtain
S_{(2)}=\frac{1}{2}(1+P)=S
and
A_{(2)}=\frac{1}{2}(1-P)=A
, which is in accordance with Proposition
5.6
.

Let
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
be an abstract algebra with an augmentation
\varepsilon\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
. We call an element
s_{(k)}

left invariant
or
right invariant
(with respect to
\varepsilon
) if
us_{(k)}=\varepsilon(u)s_{(k)}\;\forall\,u\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
or
s_{(k)}u=\varepsilon(u)s_{(k)}\;\forall\,u\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
respectively. If an element
s_{(k)}\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
is left or right invariant and normalised as
\varepsilon(s_{(k)})=1
, then it is an idempotent.

Let
\rho\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
be an algebra homomorphism (representation) satisfying the condition
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}\subset\rho({\mathfrak{U}}_{k})
. By applying it to the conditions of left and right invariance of
s_{(k)}
we see that
S_{(k)}=\rho(s_{(k)})
satisfy (
5.12
), if we additionally suppose that
\varepsilon(u_{1})=\dots=\varepsilon(u_{k-1})=0
for some
u_{1},\dots,u_{k-1}\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
such that
\rho(u_{a})=A^{(a,a+1)}
. In the case
\rho({\mathfrak{U}}_{k})={\mathcal{U}}_{k}
we have
S_{(k)}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
, so due to the formulae (
5.75
) the operator
S_{(k)}
is the
k
-th
S
-operator. In more general case one need to check the conditions (
5.13
), (
5.14
); due to the normalisation
\varepsilon(s_{(k)})=1
it is enough to show that the operators
\rho(u)-\varepsilon(u)
annihilate
(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})
and
(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)
by acting from the right and left respectively, where
u
runs over the algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
or at least over a set of its generators.

The pairing operators
A_{(k)}
are obtained in the same way. Usually one needs to consider the same
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
,
\varepsilon
,
s_{(k)}
with different representation
\rho
or the same representation with different
s_{(k)}
and
\varepsilon
. Let us formulate a general statement.

Theorem 5.26
.

Let
\rho\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
\varepsilon\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be algebra homomorphisms. Let
s_{(k)}\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
be a normalised left and right invariant element:

\displaystyle us_{(k)}=s_{(k)}u=\varepsilon(u)s_{(k)}\qquad\forall\,u\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k},\qquad\varepsilon(s_{(k)})=1.

(5.77)

•

Suppose there exist elements
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
such that
\rho(u_{a})=A^{(a,a+1)}
and
\varepsilon(u_{a})=0
. If
\rho
and
\varepsilon
satisfy

\displaystyle(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*})\rho(u)=\varepsilon(u)(X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*}),

(5.78)

\displaystyle\rho(u)(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)=\varepsilon(u)(X\otimes\cdots\otimes X)

(5.79)

for all
u\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
, then
S_{(k)}=\rho(s_{(k)})\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
is the
k
-th
S
-operator.

•

Suppose there exist elements
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
such that
\rho(u_{a})=S^{(a,a+1)}
and
\varepsilon(u_{a})=0
. If
\rho
and
\varepsilon
satisfy

\displaystyle(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi)\rho(u)=\varepsilon(u)(\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi),

(5.80)

\displaystyle\rho(u)(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})=\varepsilon(u)(\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*})

(5.81)

for all
u\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
, then
A_{(k)}=\rho(s_{(k)})\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
is the
k
-th
A
-operator.

Remark 5.27
.

If
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\neq 0
or
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\neq 0
, then
X^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{*}\neq 0
or
X\otimes\cdots\otimes X\neq 0
. Analogously,
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}\neq 0
and
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
imply
\Psi\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi\neq 0
and
\Psi^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi^{*}\neq 0
respectively. In these cases the condition
\varepsilon(u_{a})=0
can be derived from (
5.78
), (
5.79
), (
5.80
) or (
5.81
) by the substitution
u=u_{a}
. However, to prove the non-vanishing of a component of a quadratic algebra (and, more generally, to find its dimension) one needs sometimes to construct the corresponding pairing operator by using Theorem
5.26
.

Remark 5.28
.

The augmentation
\varepsilon\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
defines the ideal
\mathfrak{I}_{k}:=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\varepsilon)
of
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
. It is a maximal ideal consisting of the elements
u-\varepsilon(u)
, where
u\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
. In terms of this ideal the conditions (
5.77
) take the form
us_{(k)}=s_{(k)}u=0

\;\forall\,u\in\mathfrak{I}_{k}
and
1-s_{(k)}\in\mathfrak{I}_{k}
.

Conversely, for any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{I}_{k}
of a finite-dimensional algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
there is a unique algebra isomorphism
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}/\mathfrak{I}_{k}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
, so the canonical projection
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}/\mathfrak{I}_{k}
defines an augmentation
\varepsilon\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
. In particular, if the ideal
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\pm}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
is proper, it gives an augmentation
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}_{k}/{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\pm}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
.

If the algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
admits an anti-automorphism which does not change generators then it is enough to check only left invariance (or only right invariance) due to the following fact.

Proposition 5.29
.

Let
\varepsilon\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a homomorphism.

•

Let
s_{(k)}
be left invariant and
\bar{s}_{(k)}
be right invariant elements of the algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
. If
\varepsilon(s_{(k)})=\varepsilon(\bar{s}_{(k)})=1
, then
s_{(k)}=\bar{s}_{(k)}
.

•

Let
s_{(k)}\in{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
be left-invariant and
\varepsilon(s_{(k)})=1
. If there exists an anti-automorphism
\alpha\colon{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}\to{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
such that
\varepsilon\alpha=\varepsilon
then
\bar{s}_{(k)}:=\alpha(s_{(k)})
is right invariant and
\varepsilon(\bar{s}_{(k)})=1
. Hence
s_{(k)}=\bar{s}_{(k)}
, and
s_{(k)}
is right invariant as well.

•

If a solution of (
5.77
) exists then it is unique.

Proof.

The first part is proved as Proposition
5.6
, that is
\bar{s}_{(k)}s_{(k)}=\varepsilon(\bar{s}_{(k)})s_{(k)}=s_{(k)}
,
\bar{s}_{(k)}s_{(k)}=\varepsilon(s_{(k)})\bar{s}_{(k)}=\bar{s}_{(k)}
. The second part follows from the fact that
\bar{s}_{(k)}=\alpha(s_{(k)})
is right invariant with respect to the augmentation
\varepsilon\alpha^{-1}=\varepsilon
. ∎

Consider the case when
P
satisfies the braid relation
P^{(12)}P^{(23)}P^{(12)}=P^{(23)}P^{(12)}P^{(23)}
. Since
P^{2}=1
we have the homomorphisms
\rho^{\pm}\colon{\mathbb{C}}[{\mathbf{S}}_{k}]\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
defined by the formulae
\rho^{\pm}(\sigma_{a})=\pm P^{(a,a+1)}
. The role of
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
is played by
{\mathbb{C}}[{\mathbf{S}}_{k}]
. Since
G_{k}^{\pm}=\rho^{\pm}({\mathbf{S}}_{k})
the groups
G_{k}^{+}
and
G_{k}^{-}
are finite. The pairing operator can be obtained by Proposition
5.25
or by Theorem
5.26
. The augmentation
\varepsilon\colon{\mathbb{C}}[{\mathbf{S}}_{k}]\to{\mathbb{C}}
is the counit
\varepsilon(\sigma)=1

\;\forall\,\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
. The elements
u_{a}
have the form
\frac{1-\sigma_{a}}{2}
. The operators (
5.76
) coincide with the image of
s_{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}\sigma
under the homomorphisms
\rho^{+}
and
\rho^{-}
, these are

\displaystyle S_{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}\rho^{+}(\sigma),\qquad A_{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}\rho^{+}(\sigma).

(5.82)

Note that
A_{(k)}
can be obtained as the image of
a_{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}\sigma
under
\rho^{+}
. In this case one need to consider the augmentation
\varepsilon(\sigma_{a})=-1
and
u_{a}=\frac{1+\sigma_{a}}{2}
.

6
Examples of minor operators

Here we construct pairing operators for the examples given in Section
3
and consider the corresponding minors. Since the Manin matrices described in Sections
3.1
and
3.2
are particular cases of the
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrices it is sufficient to consider minors for the case of Section
3.3
. The formulae for
S
- and
A
-minors of the
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrices are valid for more general case: for
(B,A_{\widehat{p}})
- and
(A_{\widehat{q}},B)
-Manin matrices respectively. By starting with the idempotents
\widehat{R}^{q}_{-}
introduced in Section
4.1
we write another pairing operators, which gives related minor operators for
q
-Manin matrices. Finally we investigate the case of Section
3.4
.

6.1
Minors for the
\boldsymbol{(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})}
-Manin matrices

Consider the idempotent
A=A_{\widehat{q}}
. The pairing operators for
A
are given by the formulae (
5.82
), where
\rho^{+}=\rho_{\widehat{q}}\colon{\mathbb{C}}[{\mathbf{S}}_{k}]\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
,
\rho_{\widehat{q}}(\sigma_{a})=P_{\widehat{q}}^{(a,a+1)}
.

Let
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
\widehat{q}_{II}
be the corresponding
k\times k
matrix with entries
(\widehat{q}_{II})_{\rm st}=q_{i_{s}i_{t}}
. Then we have homomorphism
\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q}_{II}}}({\mathbb{C}})\to\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
given by the formula
\psi_{s}\mapsto\psi_{i_{s}}
. By applying it to (
3.25
) one yields

\displaystyle\psi_{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\psi_{i_{\sigma(k)}}=\frac{(-1)^{\sigma}}{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)}\psi_{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}},

(6.1)

where

\displaystyle\mu(\widehat{q},\sigma):=\!\!\!\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\sigma^{-1}(s)>\sigma^{-1}(t)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!q_{\rm st},\qquad\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)=\!\!\!\!\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\sigma^{-1}(s)>\sigma^{-1}(t)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!q_{i_{s}i_{t}}.\vskip-4.30554pt

Since
\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q}}}^{*}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q^{\prime}}}}({\mathbb{C}})
, where
q^{\prime}_{ij}=q_{ij}^{-1}
we obtain

\displaystyle\psi^{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\psi^{i_{\sigma(k)}}=(-1)^{\sigma}\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)\psi^{i_{1}}\cdots\psi^{i_{k}}.\vskip-4.30554pt

(6.2)

Denote
e_{I}:=e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{k}}
and
e^{J}:=e^{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=e^{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{j_{k}}
for arbitrary
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
. Let us write
I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n)
if
I=(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k})
such that
1\leqslant i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n
.

Since
\rho_{\widehat{q}}(\sigma)e_{I}
is proportional to
e_{\sigma I}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\psi^{j_{1}}\cdots\psi^{j_{k}},\psi_{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}}\rangle=A^{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}e^{J}\rho_{\widehat{q}}(\sigma)e_{I}=\frac{1}{k!}e^{J}e_{I}=\frac{1}{k!}\delta^{J}_{I}\vskip-4.30554pt

(6.3)

for any
I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n)
and
J=(j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leqslant n)
.

The formulae (
6.1
), (
6.2
) and (
6.3
) give the formula for the entries of the
A
-operator:

\displaystyle A^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{j_{\tau(1)}\dots j_{\tau(k)}}=\big\langle\psi^{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\psi^{i_{\sigma(k)}},\psi_{j_{\tau(1)}}\cdots\psi_{j_{\tau(k)}}\big\rangle=\frac{1}{k!}(-1)^{\tau}(-1)^{\sigma}\frac{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)}{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\tau)}\delta_{J}^{I},

(6.4)

where
I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n)
,
J=(j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leqslant n)
and
\sigma,\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
. Due to the formula (
3.24
) the other entries vanish.

Note that the vectors
\psi_{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}}
, where
1\leqslant i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n
, form a basis of
\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
, so its dimension is
r_{k}=\dim\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\dim\Xi_{A_{\widehat{q}}}^{*}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}={n\choose k}
. By using the formula (
6.4
) one can directly check that
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits A_{(k)}=r_{k}
:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits A_{(k)}=\sum_{l_{1},\dots,l_{k}=1}^{n}A^{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}_{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n\\
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}\end{subarray}}A^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}=\sum_{1\leqslant i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n}1={n\choose k}.

(6.5)

Let us calculate the
A
-minors of an
\big(A_{\widehat{q}},\widetilde{A}\big)
-Manin matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
, where
\widetilde{A}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
is an arbitrary idempotent. For any
I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n)
and
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
, where
j_{1},\dots,j_{k}=1,\dots,m
, we have

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1},\dots,l_{k}=1}^{n}A^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}M^{l_{1}}_{j_{1}}\cdots M^{l_{k}}_{j_{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\tau}(-1)^{\sigma}\frac{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)}{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\tau)}M^{i_{\tau(1)}}_{j_{1}}\cdots M^{i_{\tau(k)}}_{j_{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}(-1)^{\sigma}\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IJ})

(6.6)

(the other entries vanish). Note that this is in agreement with the formulae (
5.51
) and (
6.2
). Together with the properties the
\widehat{q}
-determinant with respect to a change of rows formulated in Corollary
3.9
the relation (
6.6
) implies

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\frac{1}{k!}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IJ})

(6.7)

for any
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
.

Let
\widetilde{A}=A_{\widehat{p}}
, then
M
is a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix. The formulae (
5.51
) and (
6.1
) gives us the symmetry with respect to the lower indices in the form

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{\tau(1)}\dots j_{\tau(k)}}=\frac{(-1)^{\tau}}{\mu(\widehat{p}_{JJ},\tau)}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}},

where
J=(j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leqslant m)
and
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
are arbitrary (the entries for other lower indices vanish). This also follows from the formula (
6.7
) and Corollary
3.9
.

Since
A_{(k)}e_{\sigma I}
is proportional to
A_{(k)}\rho_{\widehat{q}}(\sigma)e_{I}=(-1)^{\sigma}A_{(k)}e_{I}
the vectors

\displaystyle w_{I}=A_{(k)}e_{I}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\tau}\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\tau)e_{i_{\tau(1)}\dots i_{\tau(k)}},\qquad I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n),

(6.8)

form the basis of
A_{(k)}({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
. The corresponding elements of the dual basis are

\displaystyle w^{I}=k!e^{I}A_{(k)}=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}\frac{1}{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)}e^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}.

The
A
-minors in these bases are exactly
\widehat{q}
-minors of the matrix
M
:

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{I}_{J}

\displaystyle=w^{I}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{w}_{J}=k!e^{I}A_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{A}_{(k)}e_{J}=w^{I}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}e_{J}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}\frac{1}{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)}M^{i_{\sigma(1)}}_{j_{1}}\cdots M^{i_{\sigma(k)}}_{j_{k}}=\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IJ}),

where
I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n)
and
J=(j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leqslant m)
,
\widetilde{w}_{J}=\widetilde{A}_{(k)}e_{J}
. Thus we have
\phi^{(k)}_{J}=\sum_{I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n)}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IJ})\psi^{(k)}_{I}
, where

\displaystyle\phi^{(k)}_{J}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}\mu(\widehat{p}_{JJ},\sigma)\phi_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\phi_{j_{\sigma(k)}}=\phi_{j_{1}}\cdots\phi_{j_{k}},\qquad\phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M^{i}_{j}\psi_{i},

\displaystyle\psi^{(k)}_{I}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)\psi_{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\psi_{i_{\sigma(k)}}=\psi_{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}}.

To consider
S
-operators for
A=A_{\widehat{q}}
and corresponding
S
-minors we first note the formula

\displaystyle x^{\sigma(1)}\cdots x^{\sigma(n)}=x^{1}\cdots x^{n}\!\!\!\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\sigma^{-1}(i)>\sigma^{-1}(j)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!q_{ij},\qquad\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n},

(6.9)

It is proved in the same way as (
3.25
). Let
I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)
, i.e.,
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
such that
1\leqslant i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n
. By applying the homomorphism

{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}_{II}}}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
,
x^{s}\mapsto x^{i_{s}}
, to (
6.9
) we obtain

\displaystyle x^{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots x^{i_{\sigma(k)}}

\displaystyle=\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}}.

(6.10)

By changing
x^{i}\to x_{i}
,
q_{ij}\to q_{ij}^{-1}
we derive

\displaystyle x_{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots x_{i_{\sigma(k)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{k}}.

(6.11)

Recall that for a tuple
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
we defined
\nu_{i}=|\{s\mid i_{s}=i\}|
(see Section
5.1
). As we mentioned the stabiliser
({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{I}
has order
\nu_{1}!\cdots\nu_{n}!
. This means that the group
({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{I}
is generated by
\sigma_{s}\in({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{I}
, since the subgroup of
{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
generated by these elements has exactly the order
\nu_{1}!\cdots\nu_{n}!
. In other words, we have
({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{I}\cong{\mathbf{S}}_{\nu_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\mathbf{S}}_{\nu_{n}}
. Denote

\displaystyle\nu_{I}:=\big|({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{I}\big|=\nu_{1}!\cdots\nu_{n}!.

(6.12)

By taking into account
P_{\widehat{q}}(e_{i}\otimes e_{i})=e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
we obtain
\rho_{\widehat{q}}(\sigma)e_{I}=e_{I}
for any
\sigma\in({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{I}
, so that

\displaystyle\big\langle x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}},x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{k}}\big\rangle=S^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\frac{\nu_{I}}{k!}\delta^{I}_{J},

where
I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)
,
J=(j_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant j_{k}\leqslant n)
. Any
k
-tuple is a permutation of some
I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)
, so an arbitrary entry of
S_{(k)}
has the form

\displaystyle S_{j_{\tau(1)}\dots j_{\tau(k)}}^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}=\big\langle x^{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots x^{i_{\sigma(k)}},x_{j_{\tau(1)}}\cdots x_{j_{\tau(k)}}\big\rangle=\frac{\nu_{I}}{k!}\cdot\frac{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)}{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\tau)}\delta^{I}_{J}.

(6.13)

Note that
\sigma^{-1}I\equiv(i_{\sigma(1)},\dots,i_{\sigma(k)})=(i_{\tau(1)},\dots,i_{\tau(k)})\equiv\tau^{-1}I
iff
({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{I}\cdot\sigma=({\mathbf{S}}_{k})_{I}\cdot\tau
. Hence by permuting
I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)
by all the permutations
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
we obtain
\frac{k!}{\nu_{I}}
groups of
\nu_{I}
identical tuples. This implies the formula

\displaystyle\sum_{l_{1},\dots,l_{k}=1}^{n}C_{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}=\sum_{I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)}\frac{1}{\nu_{I}}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}C_{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}.

(6.14)

Since the vectors
x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}}
,
1\leqslant i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n
form a basis of the vector space
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
, its dimension is
d_{k}=\dim{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\dim{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}^{*}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}={k+n-1\choose k}
. The equality
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits S_{(k)}=d_{k}
for (
6.13
) can be checked as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits S_{(k)}=\sum_{l_{1},\dots,l_{k}=1}^{n}S^{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}_{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)\\
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\nu_{I}}S^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}=\sum_{I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)}1={k+n-1\choose k}.

Let
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be a
(B,A_{\widehat{p}})
-Manin matrix and
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
,
1\leqslant i_{s}\leqslant n
,
J=(j_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant j_{k}\leqslant m)
. Let
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
be the
k
-th
S
-operator for
\widetilde{A}=A_{\widehat{p}}
. Then by using the formula (
6.14
) we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\sum_{l_{1},\dots,l_{k}=1}^{m}M^{i_{1}}_{l_{1}}\cdots M^{i_{k}}_{l_{k}}\widetilde{S}^{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}\mu(\widehat{p}_{JJ},\sigma)M^{i_{1}}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots M^{i_{k}}_{j_{\sigma(k)}},

where
\widetilde{S}^{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}
are entries of
\widetilde{S}_{(k)}
.

Let us introduce the
\widehat{q}
-permanent for a
n\times n
matrix
M
by the formula

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M)=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}}M^{1}_{\sigma(1)}\cdots M^{n}_{\sigma(n)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\sigma^{-1}(i)<\sigma^{-1}(j)\end{subarray}}q_{ij},

Then we can write

\displaystyle\big\langle f_{M}(x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}}),\widetilde{x}_{j_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{x}_{j_{k}}\big\rangle=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\frac{1}{k!}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}_{JJ}}(M_{IJ}),

where
\widetilde{x}_{j}
are the generators of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{p}}}^{*}({\mathbb{C}})
. The other entries of the
S
-minors are calculated by means of the formulae (
6.11
):

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{\tau(1)}\dots j_{\tau(k)}}

\displaystyle=\big\langle f_{M}\big(x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}}\big),\widetilde{x}_{j_{\tau(1)}}\cdots\widetilde{x}_{j_{\tau(k)}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu(\widehat{p}_{JJ},\tau)}\big\langle f_{M}\big(x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{k}}\big),\widetilde{x}_{j_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{x}_{j_{k}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\,\frac{1}{\mu(\widehat{p}_{JJ},\tau)}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}_{JJ}}(M_{IJ}).

(6.15)

Note that
\widehat{q}
-determinant becomes
\widehat{q}
-permanent of the transposed matrix after the substitution
q_{ij}\to-q_{ij}^{-1}
(
i\neq j
), so the formula (
3.29
) gives the corresponding properties of
\widehat{q}
-permanent. In this way we obtain

\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}_{LL}}(M_{IL})=\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}_{JJ}}(M_{IJ})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\tau^{-1}(s)>\tau^{-1}(t)\end{subarray}}p_{i_{s}i_{t}}^{-1}

for arbitrary matrix
M
and
k
-tuples
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
,
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
,
L=(j_{\tau(1)},\dots,j_{\tau(k)})
(cf. Corollary
3.9
). By using this formula and the relation (
6.15
) we derive

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\frac{1}{k!}\,\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}_{JJ}}(M_{IJ}).

for any
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
,
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
.

Let
B=A_{\widehat{q}}
, so
M
is a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix. The symmetry of the
S
-minors with respect to the upper indices follows from the formulae (
6.10
):

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}=\big\langle f_{M}\big(x^{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots x^{i_{\sigma(k)}}\big),\widetilde{x}_{j_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{x}_{j_{k}}\big\rangle=\mu(\widehat{p}_{II},\sigma)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}},

where
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
are arbitrary.

The basis of
S_{(k)}({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}
and its dual are formed by the vectors

\displaystyle v_{I}=\frac{k!}{\nu_{I}}S_{(k)}e_{I}=\frac{1}{\nu_{I}}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)e_{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}},

\displaystyle v^{I}=e^{I}S_{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}\frac{1}{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)}e^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}},

where
I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)
. The
S
-minors in these bases are

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)^{I}_{J}

\displaystyle=v^{I}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{S}_{(k)}}M\big)\widetilde{v}_{J}=\frac{k!}{\nu_{J}}e^{I}S_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}e_{J}=e^{I}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}\widetilde{v}_{J}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu_{J}}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}\mu(\widehat{p}_{JJ},\sigma)M^{i_{1}}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots M^{i_{k}}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}=\frac{1}{\nu_{J}}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}_{JJ}}(M_{IJ}),

where
I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)
,
J=(j_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant j_{k}\leqslant m)
and
\widetilde{v}_{J}=\frac{k!}{\nu_{J}}\widetilde{S}_{(k)}e_{J}
. We derive
y_{(k)}^{I}=\sum_{J=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant m)}\frac{1}{\nu_{J}}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{JJ}}(M_{IJ})\;\widetilde{x}_{(k)}^{J}
, where

\displaystyle y_{(k)}^{I}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}\frac{1}{\mu(\widehat{q}_{II},\sigma)}y^{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots y^{i_{\sigma(k)}}=y^{i_{1}}\cdots y^{i_{k}},\qquad y^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{i}_{j}\widetilde{x}^{j},

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{(k)}^{J}=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}\frac{1}{\mu(\widehat{p}_{JJ},\sigma)}\widetilde{x}^{j_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\widetilde{x}^{j_{\sigma(k)}}=\widetilde{x}^{j_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{x}^{j_{k}}.

Let
M
be an
n\times m

(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix and
N
be an
m\times\ell

(\widehat{p},\widehat{r})
-Manin matrix such that
[M^{i}_{j},N^{k}_{l}]=0
. From (
5.70
) and (
5.71
) we obtain the formulae

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{r}_{LL}}\big((MN)_{IL}\big)=\sum_{J=(j_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant j_{k}\leqslant m)}\frac{1}{\nu_{J}}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}_{JJ}}(M_{IJ})\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{r}_{LL}}(N_{JL}),

(6.16)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}\big((MN)_{IL}\big)=\sum_{J=(j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leqslant m)}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}_{II}}(M_{IJ})\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}_{JJ}}(N_{JL}),

(6.17)

where
I=(i_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant n)
and
L=(l_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant l_{k}\leqslant\ell)
in (
6.16
) and
I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n)
and
L=(l_{1}<\dots<l_{k}\leqslant\ell)
in (
6.17
).

Now let us demonstrate how the general formulae (
5.63
), (
5.64
) can be applied to
\widehat{q}
-permanent and
\widehat{q}
-determinant. Let
M
be an
n\times n

(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix. According to Proposition
3.3
the matrix
{}^{\sigma}_{\tau}M=\sigma M\tau^{-1}
is a
\big(\sigma\widehat{q}\sigma^{-1},\tau\widehat{p}\tau^{-1}\big)
-Manin matrix. Let us use the formulae (
5.63
), (
5.64
) for
M
and
\sigma,\tau\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
(this is the case
m=n=k
). The formula (
3.21
) implies
\iota_{\tau}\big(\rho_{\widehat{q}}(\sigma)\big)=\tau^{\otimes k}\rho_{\widehat{q}}(\sigma)\big(\tau^{\otimes k}\big)^{-1}=\rho_{\tau\widehat{q}\tau^{-1}}(\sigma)
. Hence
\iota_{\tau}\widetilde{S}_{(n)}
and
\iota_{\sigma}A_{(n)}
are the
n
-th
S
- and
A
-operators for
A_{\tau\widehat{p}\tau^{-1}}
and
A_{\sigma\widehat{q}\sigma^{-1}}
respectively.
Note that
\big(\sigma^{\otimes n}T(\tau^{\otimes n})^{-1}\big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{n}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{n}}=T^{\sigma^{-1}(i_{1})\dots\sigma^{-1}(i_{n})}_{\tau^{-1}(j_{1})\dots\tau^{-1}(j_{n})}
. By taking
i_{s}=s
and
j_{s}=s
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\tau\widehat{p}\tau^{-1}}(\sigma M\tau^{-1})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\sigma(s)>\sigma(t)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!q_{\rm st}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\tau(s)>\tau(t)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!p_{\rm st}^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}}(M),

(6.18)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\sigma\widehat{q}\sigma^{-1}}(\sigma M\tau^{-1})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\sigma(s)>\sigma(t)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!q_{\rm st}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<t\\
\tau(s)>\tau(t)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!p_{\rm st}^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}(M).

(6.19)

Consider the
2\times 2
example of a
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrix:

\displaystyle M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\!,\qquad\widehat{q}=\begin{pmatrix}1&q\\
q^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix}\!,\qquad\widehat{p}=\begin{pmatrix}1&p\\
p^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix}\!.

The formulae (
3.18
), (
3.19
) have the form

\displaystyle ac=q^{-1}ca,\qquad bd=q^{-1}db,\qquad ad-q^{-1}pda+pbc-q^{-1}cb=0.

The
\widehat{q}
-determinant and
\widehat{p}
-permanent have the from

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{\widehat{q}}M=ad-q^{-1}cb=\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}M,\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{p}M:=\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{\widehat{p}}M=ad+pbc.

Let
\sigma=\tau=\sigma_{12}\in{\mathbf{S}}_{2}
. Since
\sigma M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}c&d\\
a&b\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
M\tau^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b&a\\
d&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M\tau^{-1})=bc-q^{-1}da=-p^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}M,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{p^{-1}}(M\tau^{-1})=bc+p^{-1}ad=p^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{p}M,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q^{-1}}(\sigma M)=cb-qad=-q\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}M,\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{p}(\sigma M)=cb+pda=q\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits_{p}M.

These formulae are particular cases of (
6.18
), (
6.19
).

6.2
Pairing operators for
\boldsymbol{q}
-Manin matrices from Hecke algebras

The formulae of the Section
6.1
at
\widehat{q}=q^{[n]}
and
\widehat{p}=q^{[m]}
give the corresponding formulae for the
q
-Manin case. Here we construct the minors for the
q
-Manin matrices by using the idempotent (
4.13
) by supposing that
q\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0\}
is not a root of unity. In particular, this condition implies that the
q
-numbers

\displaystyle k_{q}:=\frac{q^{k}-q^{-k}}{q-q^{-1}}=q^{k-1}+q^{k-3}+q^{k-5}+\dots+q^{1-k}

do not vanish for any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geqslant 1}
.

Remind that due to the formula (
4.16
) the idempotents
A=\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}=2_{q}^{-1}\big(q^{-1}-\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}\big)
and
A^{\prime}=A^{q}_{n}
are left-equivalent, so the quadratic algebras for these idempotents coincide with each other:
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{q}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{A^{q}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
. The “dual” quadratic algebras do not coincide. Namely, since the idempotent
\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}
is right-equivalent to
A^{q^{-1}}_{n}
(the formula (
4.17
)) we have
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A^{q^{-1}}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{q}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi^{*}_{\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi^{*}_{A^{q^{-1}}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi_{A^{q}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
.

Let us construct pairing operators for the idempotent
A=R^{q}_{n-}
. Due to Lemma
4.3
the dual idempotent
S=1-A=R^{q}_{n+}
has the form (
4.12
). The algebra
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
in this case is a subalgebra of
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
generated by the matrices
\big(\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}\big)^{(a,a+1)}
, while its maximal ideals
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}^{\pm}
are generated by
\big(\widehat{R}^{q}_{n\pm}\big)^{(a,a+1)}
.
Since
\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}
satisfies the braid relation (
4.10
) and Hecke relation (
4.15
) the role of this algebra is
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
is played by the Hecke algebra.

Recall that the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}_{k}^{q}
is the algebra generated by
h_{1},\dots,h_{k-1}
with the relations

\displaystyle\big(h_{a}-q^{-1}\big)(h_{a}+q)=0\qquad a=1,\dots,k-1,

(6.20)

\displaystyle h_{a}h_{b}=h_{b}h_{a},\qquad|a-b|>1,

(6.21)

\displaystyle h_{a}h_{a+1}h_{a}=h_{a+1}h_{a}h_{a+1},\qquad a=1,\dots,k-2.

(6.22)

For
b\leqslant k
we will identify the subalgebra generated by
h_{1},\dots,h_{b}
with
{\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{b}
. Note that the relations (
6.21
) implies that the elements of
{\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{b}
commute with
h_{b+1},\dots,h_{k-1}
.

The algebra
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
is the image of the representation

\displaystyle\rho\colon\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{k}^{q}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big),\qquad\rho(h_{a})=\big(\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}\big)^{(a,a+1)}.

(6.23)

The relations (
6.20
), (
6.22
) and the conditions on the parameter
q
imply that there are exactly two augmentations

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{+}\colon\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{k}^{q}\to{\mathbb{C}},\qquad\varepsilon^{+}(h_{a})=q^{-1},\qquad\varepsilon^{-}\colon\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{k}^{q}\to{\mathbb{C}},\qquad\varepsilon^{-}(h_{a})=-q.

(6.24)

Since
\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}-q^{-1}=-2_{q}\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}
and
\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}+q=2_{q}\widehat{R}^{q}_{n+}
, we have
\rho(u_{a}^{\pm})=\big(\widehat{R}^{q}_{n\mp}\big)^{(a,a+1)}
and
\varepsilon(u_{a}^{\pm})=0
for
u_{a}^{\pm}=\frac{q^{\mp 1}\mp h_{a}}{2_{q}}
. Hence to apply Theorem
5.26
we only need idempotents
s^{+}_{(k)},s^{-}_{(k)}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{k}
invariant with respect to the augmentations
\varepsilon^{+}
and
\varepsilon^{-}
respectively. Such idempotents were constructed by D. Gurevich on the level of representation (
6.23
). We define the idempotents
s^{\pm}_{(k)}
(as some elements of the abstract Hecke algebra) by following his work
[
11
]
.

Consider the elements

\displaystyle t^{+}_{k}=q^{k-1}+q^{k-2}h_{k-1}+q^{k-3}h_{k-2}h_{k-1}+\dots+h_{1}\cdots h_{k-1},

(6.25)

\displaystyle t^{-}_{k}=q^{1-k}-q^{2-k}h_{k-1}+q^{3-k}h_{k-2}h_{k-1}-\dots+(-1)^{k-1}h_{1}\cdots h_{k-1}.

(6.26)

By applying the augmentations (
6.24
) we obtain

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\pm}(t^{\pm}_{k})=k_{q}.

(6.27)

The elements (
6.25
), (
6.26
) can be defined iteratively by the formulae
t^{\pm}_{1}=1
and

\displaystyle t^{\pm}_{k}=q^{\pm(k-1)}\pm t^{\pm}_{k-1}h_{k-1}.

Define the elements
s^{\pm}_{(k)}
iteratively as

\displaystyle s^{\pm}_{(k)}=k_{q}^{-1}t^{\pm}_{k}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)},\qquad s^{\pm}_{(1)}=1.

(6.28)

Note that
\rho\big(s^{\pm}_{(2)}\big)=\widehat{R}^{q}_{n\pm}
.

It was proved in
[
11
]
that
\rho\big(s^{\pm}_{(k)}\big)
are left and right invariant with respect to corresponding augmentations of
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}=\rho({\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{k})
. This proof is suitable to establish the invariance of
s^{\pm}_{(k)}
with respect to
\varepsilon^{\pm}
, but we give an alternative proof.

Proposition 6.1
.

The formulae (
6.28
) define idempotents
s^{\pm}_{(k)}
satisfying

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\pm}\big(s^{\pm}_{(k)}\big)=1,\qquad us^{\pm}_{(k)}=s^{\pm}_{(k)}u=\varepsilon^{\pm}(u)s^{\pm}_{(k)}\qquad\forall\,u\in{\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{k}.

(6.29)

Hence
S_{(k)}=\rho\big(s^{+}_{(k)}\big)
and
A_{(k)}=\rho\big(s^{-}_{(k)}\big)
are pairing operators for
A=\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}
.

Proof.

The first formula follows from (
6.27
) and the equality
\varepsilon^{\pm}\big(s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}\big)=1
which can be supposed by induction. Further we prove the left invariance. Suppose
us^{\pm}_{(k-1)}=\varepsilon^{\pm}(u)s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}

\forall\,u\in{\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{k-1}
.
We need to prove
h_{a}s^{\pm}_{(k)}=\pm q^{\mp 1}s^{\pm}_{(k)}
for
a=1,\dots,k-1
. Denote by
I_{b,k}^{\pm}
the left ideal of
{\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{k}
generated by the elements
u-\varepsilon^{\pm}(u)
,
u\in{\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{b}
. Due to the induction assumption we have
I^{\pm}_{b,k}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}=0
for
b=1,\dots,k-1
. Then the relations we need to prove follows from the formulae
h_{k-l}t^{\pm}_{k}\in\pm q^{\mp 1}t^{\pm}_{k}+I^{\pm}_{k-l,k}
which we prove by induction in
l\geqslant 1
. For
l=1
we have

h_{k-1}t^{\pm}_{k}=q^{\pm(k-1)}h_{k-1}\pm q^{\pm(k-2)}h_{k-1}^{2}+h_{k-1}\sum_{a=2}^{k-1}(\pm 1)^{a}q^{\mp a}h_{k-a}\cdots h_{k-3}h_{k-2}h_{k-1}.

By using the relations (
6.20
)–(
6.22
) we obtain

\displaystyle h_{k-1}t^{\pm}_{k}=q^{\pm(k-1)}h_{k-1}\pm q^{\pm(k-2)}\pm q^{\pm(k-2)}\big(q^{-1}-q\big)h_{k-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{h_{k-1} t^\pm_k=}{}+\sum_{a=2}^{k-1}(\pm 1)^{a}q^{\mp a}h_{k-a}\cdots h_{k-3}h_{k-2}h_{k-1}h_{k-2}.

By taking into account
q^{k-1}+q^{k-2}\big(q^{-1}-q\big)=q^{-1}q^{k-2}
and
q^{1-k}-q^{2-k}\big(q^{-1}-q\big)=qq^{2-k}
we derive
h_{k-1}t^{\pm}_{k}\in\pm q^{\mp 1}t^{\pm}_{k}+I^{\pm}_{k-1,k}
. Suppose that
h_{k-l}t^{\pm}_{k}\in\pm q^{\mp 1}t^{\pm}_{k}+I^{\pm}_{k-l,k}
for some
l\geqslant 1
and all
k>l
then for any
k>l+1
we obtain

\displaystyle h_{k-l-1}t^{\pm}_{k}=q^{\pm(k-1)}h_{k-l-1}\pm h_{k-l-1}t^{\pm}_{k-1}h_{k-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{h_{k-l-1}t^\pm_k}\in q^{\pm(k-1)}h_{k-l-1}\pm\big({\pm}q^{\mp 1}\big)t^{\pm}_{k-1}h_{k-1}+I^{\pm}_{k-l-1,k-1}h_{k-1}.

Since
uh_{k-1}=h_{k-1}u
for any
u\in{\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{k-l-1}
we have the inclusion
I^{\pm}_{k-l-1,k-1}h_{k-1}\subset I^{\pm}_{k-l-1,k}
, so that
h_{k-l-1}t^{\pm}_{k}\in\pm q^{\mp 1}t^{\pm}_{k}+I^{\pm}_{k-l-1,k}
. The right invariance of
s^{\pm}_{(k)}
now follows from Proposition
5.29
, since the formula
\rho(h_{a})=h_{a}
defines an anti-automorphism of
{\mathcal{H}}^{q}_{k}
. ∎

Corollary 6.2
.

We have the following symmetric recurrent formulae:

\displaystyle s^{\pm}_{(k)}=\frac{q^{\pm(k-1)}}{k_{q}}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}\pm\frac{(k-1)_{q}}{k_{q}}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}h_{k-1}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)},

(6.30)

\displaystyle S_{(k)}=\frac{q^{k-1}}{k_{q}}S_{(k-1)}+\frac{(k-1)_{q}}{k_{q}}S_{(k-1)}\big(\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}\big)^{(k-1,k)}S_{(k-1)},

(6.31)

\displaystyle A_{(k)}=\frac{q^{1-k}}{k_{q}}A_{(k-1)}-\frac{(k-1)_{q}}{k_{q}}A_{(k-1)}\big(\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}\big)^{(k-1,k)}A_{(k-1)}.

(6.32)

Proof.

By multiplying
t^{\pm}_{k}=q^{\pm(k-1)}\pm\sum_{a=0}^{k-2}(\pm 1)^{a}q^{\pm(k-2-a)}h_{k-1-a}\cdots h_{k-1}
by
s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}
from the right and taking into account (
6.29
) for
k-1
we obtain

\displaystyle s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}t^{\pm}_{k}

\displaystyle=q^{\pm(k-1)}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}\pm\sum_{a=0}^{k-2}(\pm 1)^{a}q^{\pm(k-2-a)}\varepsilon^{\pm}(h_{k-1-a}\cdots h_{k-2})s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}h_{k-1}

\displaystyle=q^{\pm(k-1)}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}\pm\sum_{a=0}^{k-2}q^{\pm(k-2-a)}q^{\mp a}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}h_{k-1}

\displaystyle=q^{\pm(k-1)}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}\pm(k-1)_{q}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}h_{k-1}.

Substituting it to
s^{\pm}_{(k)}=s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}s^{\pm}_{(k)}=\frac{1}{k_{q}}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}t^{\pm}_{k}s^{\pm}_{(k-1)}
we obtain (
6.30
). Application of the homomorphism
\rho
to (
6.30
) gives the formulae (
6.31
) and (
6.32
). ∎

Let us calculate the entries of the
A
-operators for the idempotent
A=R^{q}_{n-}
. Since we have
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathfrak{X}}^{*}_{A_{\widehat{p}}}({\mathbb{C}})
for
q_{ij}=q^{\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(j-i)}
and
p_{ij}=q^{\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\nolimits(i-j)}
the formulae (
6.1
), (
6.2
) take the form

\displaystyle\psi_{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\psi_{i_{\sigma(k)}}=(-1)^{\sigma}q^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)}\psi_{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}},

\displaystyle\psi^{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\psi^{i_{\sigma(k)}}=(-1)^{\sigma}q^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)}\psi^{i_{1}}\cdots\psi^{i_{k}},

where
i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}
. Hence

\displaystyle A^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{j_{\tau(1)}\dots j_{\tau(k)}}=(-1)^{\sigma}(-1)^{\tau}q^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\tau)}A^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}},

(6.33)

for
i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}
,
j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}
and the other entries of
A_{(k)}
vanish.

To find
A^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}
we first write the formula (
6.33
) for the longest permutation
\sigma=\tau
:

\displaystyle A^{i_{k}\dots i_{1}}_{j_{k}\dots j_{1}}=q^{-k(k-1)}A^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}.

(6.34)

Let us prove that there are numbers
\lambda_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that

\displaystyle A^{i_{k}\dots i_{1}}_{j_{k}\dots j_{1}}=\lambda_{k}\delta^{i_{k}}_{j_{k}}\cdots\delta^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}

(6.35)

for any
i_{k}>i_{k-1}>\dots>i_{1}
and
j_{k}>j_{k-1}>\dots>j_{1}
. This holds for
k=1
with
\lambda_{1}=1
. Suppose it holds for
k-1
, then by taking the corresponding entries in (
6.32
) we obtain

\displaystyle A^{i_{k}\dots i_{1}}_{j_{k}\dots j_{1}}=\frac{q^{1-k}}{k_{q}}A^{i_{k}\dots i_{2}}_{j_{k}\dots j_{2}}\delta^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}-\frac{(k-1)_{q}}{k_{q}}\sum_{l_{k}\dots l_{2}l^{\prime}_{2}}A^{i_{k}\dots i_{2}}_{l_{k}\dots l_{2}}\big(\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}\big)^{l_{2}i_{1}}_{l^{\prime}_{2}j_{1}}A^{l_{k}\dots l_{3}l^{\prime}_{2}}_{j_{k}\dots j_{3}j_{2}}.

If the term in the sum does not vanish then
l_{2}=i_{s}
for some
s=k,\dots,2
and
l^{\prime}_{2}=j_{t}
for some
t=k,\dots,2
, so we have
l_{2}>i_{1}
and
l^{\prime}_{2}>j_{1}
. The expression (
4.9
) implies that
\big(\widehat{R}^{q}_{n}\big)^{ii^{\prime}}_{jj^{\prime}}=0
for
i>i^{\prime}
and
j>j^{\prime}
. Hence the sum vanishes and we obtain (
6.35
) with
\lambda_{k}=\frac{q^{1-k}}{k_{q}}\lambda_{k-1}
. Iteratively we derive
\lambda_{k}=\frac{q^{-k(k-1)/2}}{k_{q}!}
, where
k_{q}!=k_{q}(k-1)_{q}\cdots 2_{q}1_{q}
. Thus due to (
6.33
), (
6.34
) and (
6.35
) we obtain

\displaystyle A^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{j_{\tau(1)}\dots j_{\tau(k)}}=\frac{q^{k(k-1)/2}}{k_{q}!}(-1)^{\sigma}(-1)^{\tau}q^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\tau)}\delta^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}\cdots\delta^{i_{k}}_{j_{k}}

for
i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}
and
j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}
.

Since
r_{k}=\dim\Xi_{\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\dim\Xi_{A^{q}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}={k\choose n}
we have
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits A_{(k)}={k\choose n}
. Explicit calculation of the trace (cf. (
6.5
)) gives us the formula

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}q^{-2\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)}=q^{-k(k-1)/2}\,k_{q}!

(6.36)

(it can be also checked by induction).

Denote by
A^{\prime}_{(k)}
the
k
-th
A
-operator for
A^{\prime}=A^{q}_{n}
. Its non-zero entries are given by the formula (
6.4
) for
\widehat{q}=q^{[n]}
, that is

\displaystyle(A^{\prime})^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{j_{\tau(1)}\dots j_{\tau(k)}}=\frac{1}{k!}(-1)^{\tau}(-1)^{\sigma}q^{\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\tau)}\delta^{I}_{J},

where
I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leqslant n)
,
J=(j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leqslant n)
. Due Proposition
5.22
the idempotents
A^{\prime}_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
are left-equivalent, so that
A^{\prime}_{(k)}=G_{[k]}A_{(k)}
for some invertible matrix
G_{[k]}
. The latter can be chosen as a diagonal matrix with entries

\displaystyle\big(G_{[k]}\big)^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}=\frac{k_{q}!}{k!}q^{2\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)-k(k-1)/2},\qquad\text{for}\quad i_{1}<\dots<i_{k},\quad\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k},

\displaystyle\big(G_{[k]}\big)^{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}_{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}=1,\qquad\text{if}\quad l_{s}=l_{t}\qquad\text{for some}\quad s\neq t.

(6.37)

Let
M
be an
\big(\widehat{R}^{q}_{n-},A_{\widehat{p}}\big)
-Manin matrix, i.e., a
\big(q^{[n]},\widehat{p}\big)
-Manin matrix. Its
A
-minors are related by the formula (
5.74
). Substitution
\widehat{q}=q^{[n]}
to (
6.6
) yields

\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{j_{\tau(1)}\dots j_{\tau(k)}}=\frac{1}{k!}(-1)^{\tau}(-1)^{\sigma}\frac{q^{\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)}}{\mu(\widehat{p}_{JJ},\tau)}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M_{IJ}).

By dividing this by (
6.37
) we obtain the
A
-minors

\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}M\big)^{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}_{j_{\tau(1)}\dots j_{\tau(k)}}=\frac{q^{k(k-1)/2}}{k_{q}!}(-1)^{\tau}(-1)^{\sigma}\frac{q^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)}}{\mu(\widehat{p}_{JJ},\tau)}\mathop{\mathrm{det}}\nolimits_{q}(M_{IJ}).

Let
w^{\prime}_{I}=A^{\prime}_{(k)}e_{I}
be the basis (
6.8
) for
\widehat{q}=q^{[n]}
. Then by the formula (
5.72
) we obtain the corresponding basis of
A_{(k)}\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
:

\displaystyle w_{I}=G_{[n]}^{-1}w^{\prime}_{I}=G_{[n]}^{-1}A^{\prime}_{(k)}e_{I}=A_{(k)}e_{I}=\frac{q^{k(k-1)/2}}{k_{q}!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}q^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)}e_{i_{\sigma(1)}\dots i_{\sigma(k)}}.

(6.38)

One can directly check that the basis
\psi^{(k)}_{I}
defined by the bases
w_{I}
and
w^{\prime}_{I}
coincide (see (
5.73
)): by using the formulae (
6.38
), (
6.1
) and (
6.36
) we derive

\displaystyle\psi^{(k)}_{I}=\frac{q^{k(k-1)/2}}{k_{q}!}\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}q^{-\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)}\psi_{i_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\psi_{i_{\sigma(k)}}=\psi_{i_{1}}\cdots\psi_{i_{k}}.

Due to Proposition
5.24
the entries of the corresponding
A
-minor operators coincide for the corresponding bases:

w^{I}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A^{\prime}_{(k)}}\big)\widetilde{w}_{J}=w^{I}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}\big)\widetilde{w}_{J},

where
\widetilde{w}_{J}
is the same as in Section
6.1
.

6.3
Pairing operators for the 4-parametric case

Here we consider the case described in Section
3.4
. The idempotent
A=A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}
is parametrised by
a,b,c\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0\}
and
\kappa\in{\mathbb{C}}
. The commutation relations for the quadratic algebra
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
are
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+\sum_{k,l=1}^{3}\psi_{k}\psi_{l}P^{kl}_{ij}=0
. They can be written in the form
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+a_{ij}^{2}\psi_{j}\psi_{i}=0
,
i\neq j
, and
2\psi_{i}^{2}=-\kappa\sum_{k,l=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{ikl}a_{lk}\psi_{k}\psi_{l}
or

\displaystyle\psi_{i}^{2}=\kappa a_{jk}\psi_{k}\psi_{j}=-\kappa a_{kj}\psi_{j}\psi_{k},\qquad(i,j,k)\in C_{3},

(6.39)

where
C_{3}
is the set of cyclic permutations
(1,2,3),(2,3,1)
and
(3,1,2)
.

The algebra
\Xi^{*}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})
is defined by the commutation relations
\psi^{i}\psi^{j}+\sum_{k,l=1}^{3}P^{ij}_{kl}\psi^{k}\psi^{l}=0
, these are
\psi^{i}\psi^{j}+a_{ji}^{2}\psi^{j}\psi^{i}+\kappa a_{ji}\sum_{k=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{ijk}\psi^{k}\psi^{k}=0
. For
i=j
we obtain
\psi^{i}\psi^{i}=0
, so we have
\Xi^{*}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})=\Xi^{*}_{A_{\widehat{q}}}({\mathbb{C}})
, where
q_{ij}=a_{ij}^{2}
. This implies that the idempotents
A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}
and
A_{\widehat{q}}
are right-equivalent. In particular,
\Xi^{*}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{3}\Xi^{*}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
and the dimensions of the components are
\dim\Xi^{*}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{0}=1
,
\dim\Xi^{*}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{1}=3
,
\dim\Xi^{*}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{2}=3
,
\dim\Xi^{*}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}=1
,
\dim\Xi^{*}_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=0
for
k\geqslant 4
.

From the relations (
6.39
) we see that
\dim\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{2}=3
. However, the dimension of
\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}
is not always equal to
1
, it depends on the parameters. The following theorem describes these dependence and gives the necessary and sufficient condition for existence of the corresponding pairing operator. Note that it is enough to consider the case
\kappa\neq 0
since the case of the idempotent
A^{a,b,c}_{0}=A_{\widehat{q}}
was considered in Section
6.1
.

Theorem 6.3
.

Assume
\kappa\neq 0
. Consider the conditions

\displaystyle(i)\phantom{ii}\quad a^{2}=b^{2}=c^{2};

\displaystyle(ii)\phantom{i}\quad a^{4}b^{2}=b^{4}c^{2}=c^{4}a^{2}=-1,\quad\kappa^{3}=-a^{3}b^{-1}c;

\displaystyle(iii)\quad a^{4}c^{2}=b^{4}a^{2}=c^{4}b^{2}=-1,\quad\kappa^{3}=a^{-3}b^{-1}c.

(6.40)

Any two of these conditions implies the third one. We have
\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{3}\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
with the dimensions of the components

\displaystyle\dim\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{0}=1,\qquad\dim\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{1}=3,\qquad\dim\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{2}=3,

\displaystyle\dim\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}=\begin{cases}3&\text{iff all three conditions~\eqref{condThabckappa} hold,}\\
1&\text{iff one and only one of three conditions~\eqref{condThabckappa} holds,}\\
0&\text{iff no one of three conditions~\eqref{condThabckappa} holds,}\end{cases}

\dim\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=0
for
k\geqslant 4
.

The third
A
-operator exists iff the condition
(i)
holds and
(ii)
,
(iii)
do not. It equals

\displaystyle A_{(3)}=w_{1}w^{1},\qquad\text{where}\quad w_{1}=\frac{1}{6}(e_{123}+e_{231}+e_{312})-\frac{a^{2}}{6}(e_{132}+e_{213}+e_{321}),

(6.41)

\displaystyle w^{1}=e^{123}+e^{231}+e^{312}-a^{-2}\big(e^{132}+e^{213}+e^{321}\big)-\kappa\big(b^{-1}e^{111}+c^{-1}e^{222}+a^{-1}e^{333}\big).

(6.42)

In this case the elements
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\psi_{k}\in\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}
have the form

\displaystyle\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\psi_{k}=\psi_{1}^{(3)},\qquad\psi_{i}\psi_{k}\psi_{j}=-a^{-2}\psi_{1}^{(3)},\qquad(i,j,k)\in C_{3},

(6.43)

\displaystyle\psi_{i}^{2}\psi_{j}=\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\psi_{i}=\psi_{j}\psi_{i}^{2}=0,\qquad i\neq j,

(6.44)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}^{3}=-\kappa b^{-1}\psi_{1}^{(3)},\qquad\psi_{2}^{3}=-\kappa c^{-1}\psi_{1}^{(3)},\qquad\psi_{3}^{3}=-\kappa a^{-1}\psi_{1}^{(3)},

(6.45)

where
\psi_{1}^{(3)}=(\Psi\otimes\Psi\otimes\Psi)w_{1}=\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}
.

Proof.

Under the condition
(i)
both conditions
(ii)
and
(iii)
gives
a^{6}=-1
, which in turn implies
-a^{3}b^{-1}c=a^{-3}b^{-1}c
. Hence
(i)
implies equivalence of
(ii)
and
(iii)
. Further, by comparing the conditions
(ii)
and
(iii)
we see that they imply
(i)
.

Now let us use Theorem
5.12
. Since the idempotents
S^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}=1-A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}
and
S_{\widehat{q}}=1-A_{\widehat{q}}
are left-equivalent the space
W_{k}
for the case
A=A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}
coincide with the space
W_{k}
for
A=A_{\widehat{q}}
. In particular,
W_{3}={\mathbb{C}}w_{1}
. The space
\overline{W}_{3}
consists of the covectors
\xi=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{3}\xi_{ijk}e^{ijk}
satisfying
\xi P^{(12)}=\xi P^{(23)}=-\xi
. This gives us the system of equations

\displaystyle\xi_{ijl}=-a_{ij}^{2}\xi_{jil},\qquad\xi_{lij}=-a_{ij}^{2}\xi_{lji},\qquad i\neq j,

(6.46)

\displaystyle\xi_{iil}=\kappa a_{jk}\xi_{kjl},\qquad\xi_{lii}=\kappa a_{jk}\xi_{lkj},\qquad(i,j,k)\in C_{3}.

(6.47)

The coefficients
\xi_{ijk}
can be divided into three sets:

\displaystyle\{\xi_{iii}\mid i=1,2,3\}\cup\{\xi_{ijk}\mid i\neq j\neq k\neq i\},

(6.48)

\displaystyle\{\xi_{iij},\xi_{iji},\xi_{jii}\mid(i,j,k)\in C_{3}\},

(6.49)

\displaystyle\{\xi_{ijj},\xi_{jij},\xi_{jji}\mid(i,j,k)\in C_{3}\}.

(6.50)

The relations (
6.46
), (
6.47
) imply that any two coefficients from the same set are proportional to each other (with a non-zero coefficient of proportionality), so that
\dim\overline{W}_{3}\leqslant 3
. The isomorphism
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}\cong\overline{W}_{3}^{*}
implies that
\dim\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}=\dim\overline{W}_{3}
. Let us prove that non-vanishing of the coefficients from the sets (
6.48
), (
6.49
), (
6.50
) corresponds exactly to the conditions
(i)
,
(ii)
,
(iii)
respectively.

Note that there are two types of symmetries of the system of equations (
6.46
), (
6.47
). First, it is invariant under the cyclic permutations of indices
1\mapsto 2\mapsto 3\mapsto 1
and
1\mapsto 3\mapsto 2\mapsto 1
. Second, it is invariant under other permutations
i\leftrightarrow j
,
i\neq j
, with the sign change
\kappa\mapsto-\kappa
. This system implies that
\xi_{111}=\kappa a_{23}\xi_{132}=\kappa a_{23}a_{13}^{2}a_{12}^{2}\xi_{321}=a_{13}^{2}a_{12}^{2}\xi_{111}
. A cyclic permutation gives
\xi_{222}=a_{21}^{2}a_{23}^{2}\xi_{222}
. If the coefficients from the set (
6.48
) do not vanish then
a_{31}^{2}=a_{12}^{2}=a_{23}^{2}
. This is the condition
(i)
. Conversely, if the condition
(i)
holds then there exists a solution with non-vanishing coefficients (
6.48
), namely
\xi=w^{1}
(the coefficients from (
6.49
) and (
6.50
) vanish in this solution).

Write down some relations between the coefficients (
6.49
) that follows from the system (
6.46
), (
6.47
):

\displaystyle\xi_{112}=-a_{12}^{2}\xi_{121}=a_{12}^{4}\xi_{211}=\kappa a_{23}a_{12}^{4}\xi_{232},

(6.51)

\displaystyle\xi_{112}=\kappa a_{23}\xi_{322}=-\kappa a_{23}a_{32}^{2}\xi_{232},

(6.52)

\displaystyle\xi_{322}=-\kappa a_{13}\xi_{331}=\kappa^{2}a_{13}a_{21}\xi_{121}.

(6.53)

All other relations is obtained by the symmetry of the first type. By equating the same coefficients we obtain the condition of existence of non-vanishing solution of (
6.51
)–(
6.53
), they are
a_{12}^{4}a_{23}^{2}=-1
,
\kappa^{3}=-a_{12}^{3}a_{23}^{-1}a_{31}
. The symmetry gives the whole condition
(ii)
. Thus there is a non-zero solution
\xi=w^{2}
with vanishing coefficients from (
6.48
) and (
6.50
). By applying the symmetry of the second type we obtain the relations between the coefficients (
6.50
) and the condition
(iii)
. This means that
(iii)
is necessary and sufficient condition for existence of a non-zero solution
\xi=w^{3}
with vanishing coefficients (
6.48
) and (
6.49
).

The isomorphism
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}\cong\overline{W}_{3}^{*}
identifies the elements
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\psi_{k}
with linear functions on
\overline{W}_{3}
. By substituting
\pi=e_{ijk}
to the formula (
5.30
) we obtain
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\psi_{k}(\xi)=\xi_{ijk}
, where
i,j,k=1,2,3
and
\xi=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{3}\xi_{ijk}e^{ijk}\in\overline{W}_{k}
. Thus the elements
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\psi_{k}
for
i\neq j\neq k\neq i
and
\psi_{i}^{3}
do not vanish iff
(i)
holds. Similarly,
(ii)
and
(iii)
are conditions of non-vanishing of the elements
\psi_{i}^{2}\psi_{j},\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\psi_{i}^{2}
, where
(i,j)=(1,2),(2,3),(3,1)
and
(i,j)=(2,1),(3,2),(1,3)
respectively.

To prove that
\dim\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=0
for
k\geqslant 0
it is enough to check it for
k=4
. Note that the relations (
6.39
) implies that all the elements
\psi_{i_{1}}\psi_{i_{2}}\psi_{i_{3}}\psi_{i_{4}}
are proportional to each other, that is
\dim\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{4}\leqslant 1
. If
\dim\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}<3
then
\psi_{i_{1}}\psi_{i_{2}}\psi_{i_{3}}
vanishes for some
i_{1}
,
i_{2}
,
i_{3}
and hence
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{4}=0
. In the case
\dim\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}=3
all the conditions (
6.40
) hold. In particular,
a^{6}=-1
. Suppose that
\dim\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{4}\neq 0
and hence all the elements
\psi_{i_{1}}\psi_{i_{2}}\psi_{i_{3}}\psi_{i_{4}}
do not vanish. From the chain of relations
\psi_{3}\psi_{1}^{2}\psi_{2}=\kappa a_{23}\psi_{3}^{2}\psi_{2}^{2}=\kappa^{3}a_{23}a_{12}a_{31}\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{2}\psi_{3}=\kappa^{3}a_{23}a_{12}a_{31}\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{2}\psi_{3}=-\kappa^{3}a_{23}a_{12}a_{31}a_{21}^{4}a_{23}^{2}a_{13}^{4}\psi_{3}\psi_{1}^{2}\psi_{2}
we obtain
\kappa^{3}a_{21}^{3}a_{23}^{3}a_{13}^{3}=-1
. By substituting
(i)
and
(ii)
we obtain
-1=-a^{3}b^{-1}ca^{-1}b^{3}c^{-3}=-a^{2}b^{2}c^{-2}=-a^{2}
, which implies
a^{6}=1
. This contradicts with
a^{6}=-1
, hence
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{4}=0
.

The existence of
A_{(3)}
implies
\dim\overline{W}_{3}=\dim W_{3}=1
, so that one and only one of the conditions (
6.40
) holds. Namely
\overline{W}_{3}
has the form
{\mathbb{C}}w^{1}
,
{\mathbb{C}}w^{2}
or
{\mathbb{C}}w^{3}
under the condition
(i)
,
(ii)
or
(iii)
respectively. But
w^{\alpha}w_{1}=0
for
\alpha=2,3
, so that only the condition
(i)
is relevant. Since
w^{1}w_{1}=1
we have
A_{(3)}=w_{1}w^{1}
. Note that
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\psi_{k}=(X\otimes X\otimes X)e_{ijk}=(X\otimes X\otimes X)A_{(3)}e_{ijk}=\psi_{1}^{(3)}w^{1}e_{ijk}
,
i,j,k=1,2,3
. By substituting the explicit expression for
w^{1}
we derive the formulae (
6.43
)–(
6.45
). ∎

Remark 6.4
.

The three cases when
\dim\Xi_{A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}=1
correspond to the conditions

\displaystyle(I)\phantom{II}\quad a^{2}=b^{2}=c^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\big(a^{6}\neq-1\text{ or }\kappa^{3}\neq-abc\big);

\displaystyle(II)\phantom{I}\quad b^{2}=a^{14},\qquad c^{2}=a^{26},\qquad a^{18}=-1,\qquad a^{6}\neq-1,\qquad\kappa^{3}=a^{7}bc;

\displaystyle(III)\quad b^{2}=a^{26},\qquad c^{2}=a^{14},\qquad a^{18}=-1,\qquad a^{6}\neq-1,\qquad\kappa^{3}=a^{7}bc

(cf.
[
21
, conditions (1.4.1) and (1.4.5)]
). The condition
(I)
is exactly the case when
A_{(3)}
exists.

Remark 6.5
.

The dimension of the space
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}
also depends on the values of the parameters
a
,
b
,
c
,
\kappa
. By using the relations (
3.32
) one can relate
xyz
with
zyx
in two different ways. As a result we obtain two different expressions for
xyz-a^{-2}b^{-2}c^{2}zyx
, namely,

\displaystyle\kappa b^{-1}x^{3}-\kappa cb^{-2}y^{3}+\kappa a^{-1}b^{-2}c^{2}z^{3}=\kappa a^{-1}z^{3}-\kappa ca^{-2}y^{3}+\kappa b^{-1}a^{-2}c^{2}x^{3}.

Assume
\kappa\neq 0
. Then we see that the elements
x^{3}
,
y^{3}
,
z^{3}
are linearly independent iff the condition
(i)
holds. One can also obtain the relation

\displaystyle\big(\kappa a^{-1}bc^{-1}+a^{2}\big)x^{2}y+\big(\kappa a^{-1}b^{3}c^{-1}-a^{-2}\big)yx^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\kappa\big(a+a^{-1}c^{-4}\big)xz^{2}+\kappa a^{-1}\big(c^{-3}+b^{4}c^{-1}\big)y^{2}z.

One can deduce that the dimension of the subspace spanned by the elements
x_{i}^{2}x_{j}
,
x_{i}x_{j}x_{i}
,
x_{j}x_{i}^{2}
,
(i,j,k)\in C_{3}
, equals
4
if the condition
(ii)
holds and it equals
3
if this condition is false. The dimension of the subspace spanned by the elements
x_{j}^{2}x_{i}
,
x_{j}x_{i}x_{j}
,
x_{i}x_{j}^{2}
,
(i,j,k)\in C_{3}
, depends on the condition
(iii)
in the same way (see
[
21
, Theorem 1.4]
). Thus the difference
\dim{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}-\dim\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{C}})_{3}
does not depend on the values of the parameters and equals
9
. Moreover, the third
S
-operator
S_{(3)}
for the idempotent
A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa}
exists iff the
A
-operator
A_{(3)}
exists.

Finally we write the
A
-Minors for an
\big(A^{a,b,c}_{\kappa},B\big)
-Manin matrix
M
. By substituting the expression
A^{ij}_{kl}=\frac{1}{2}(\delta^{i}_{k}\delta^{j}_{l}-a_{ji}^{2}\delta^{i}_{l}\delta^{j}_{k}-\kappa a_{ji}\delta_{kl}\varepsilon_{ijk})
to
\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(2)}}M\big)^{ij}_{j_{1}j_{2}}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{3}A^{ij}_{kl}M^{k}_{j_{1}}M^{l}_{j_{2}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(2)}}M\big)^{ij}_{j_{1}j_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\big(M^{i}_{j_{1}}M^{j}_{j_{2}}-a_{ji}^{2}M^{j}_{j_{1}}M^{i}_{j_{2}}-\kappa a_{ji}M^{k}_{j_{1}}M^{k}_{j_{2}}\big),

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(2)}}M\big)^{ji}_{j_{1}j_{2}}=-a_{ij}^{2}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(2)}}M\big)^{ij}_{j_{1}j_{2}},\qquad\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(2)}}M\big)^{ii}_{j_{1}j_{2}}=0,

where
(i,j,k)\in C_{3}
.

Let
a^{2}=b^{2}=c^{2}
and the condition
(ii)
do not hold (
a^{6}\neq-1
or
\kappa\neq-abc
). The components of the pairing operator
A_{(3)}=w_{1}w^{1}
have the form
A^{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}=w_{1}^{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}w^{1}_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}
. Hence

\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(3)}}M\big)^{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}_{j_{1}j_{2}j_{3}}=w_{1}^{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}=1}^{3}w^{1}_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}M^{k_{1}}_{j_{1}}M^{k_{2}}_{j_{2}}M^{k_{3}}_{j_{3}}
.
By using (
6.41
), (
6.42
) we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(3)}}M\big)^{123}_{j_{1}j_{2}j_{3}}=\frac{1}{6}\sum_{(i,j,k)\in C_{3}}\big(M^{i}_{j_{1}}M^{j}_{j_{2}}M^{k}_{j_{3}}-a^{-2}M^{j}_{j_{1}}M^{i}_{j_{2}}M^{k}_{j_{3}}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{ \big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(3)}}M\big)^{123}_{j_1j_2j_3}=}{}-\frac{\kappa}{6}\big(b^{-1}M^{1}_{j_{1}}M^{1}_{j_{2}}M^{1}_{j_{3}}+c^{-1}M^{2}_{j_{1}}M^{2}_{j_{2}}M^{2}_{j_{3}}+a^{-1}M^{3}_{j_{1}}M^{3}_{j_{2}}M^{3}_{j_{3}}\big),

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(3)}}M\big)^{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}_{j_{1}j_{2}j_{3}}=\begin{cases}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(3)}}M\big)^{123}_{j_{1}j_{2}j_{3}}&\text{if }(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in C_{3},\\[4.30554pt]
-a^{2}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(3)}}M\big)^{123}_{j_{1}j_{2}j_{3}}&\text{if }(i_{2},i_{1},i_{3})\in C_{3},\\
0&\text{if }i_{k}=i_{l}\text{ for some }k\neq l.\end{cases}

7
Manin matrices of types
\boldsymbol{B}
,
\boldsymbol{C}
and
\boldsymbol{D}

Remind that the
A_{n}
-Manin matrices and
A_{n}^{q}
-Manin matrices are related with the Yangians
Y(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
and quantum affine algebras
U_{q}\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}\big)
respectively. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}=\mathfrak{gl}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
is usually considered as the case “type
A
”, since
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\cong{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
, where
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}=\mathfrak{sl}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
is the simple algebra of the type
A_{n-1}
. Hence these Manin matrices can be referred to the type
A
. Moreover, the minor operators for more general
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrices are described by using the symmetric groups
{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
, which are the Weyl groups of the type
A_{k-1}
and participate in Schur–Weyl duality with the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
.

A generalisation of the
A_{n}
-Manin matrices to the types
B
,
C
and
D
was introduced by A. Molev. Remind that
\mathfrak{so}_{2r+1}=\mathfrak{so}(2r+1,{\mathbb{C}})
,
\mathfrak{sp}_{2r}=\mathfrak{sp}(2r,{\mathbb{C}})
and
\mathfrak{so}_{2r}=\mathfrak{so}(2r,{\mathbb{C}})
are simple Lie algebras of types
B_{r}
,
C_{r}
and
D_{r}
respectively (where
r\geqslant 2
for
D_{r}
). In this section we assume that
n=2r+1
for the
B_{r}
case and
n=2r
for the
C_{r}
and
D_{r}
cases.

7.1
Molev’s definition and corresponding quadratic algebras

By starting with the definition of Manin matrices of types
B
,
C
and
D
we interpret them as Manin matrices for quadratic algebras. To do it we use the notations
i^{\prime}=n-i+1
and

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}=\begin{cases}\phantom{-}1,&i=1,\dots,r,\\
-1,&i=r+1,\dots,n;\end{cases}

(the notation
\varepsilon_{i}
is used for the case
C_{r}
only). Introduce the operators
Q_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
for the
B
and
D
cases and
\widetilde{Q}_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
for the
C
case:

\displaystyle Q_{n}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{i^{\prime}}{}^{j^{\prime}},\qquad\widetilde{Q}_{n}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}E_{i}{}^{j}\otimes E_{i^{\prime}}{}^{j^{\prime}}.

One can check that these operators satisfies the following relations:

\displaystyle(Q_{n})^{2}=nQ_{n},\qquad P_{n}Q_{n}=Q_{n}P_{n}=Q_{n},

(7.1)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{Q}_{n})^{2}=n\widetilde{Q}_{n},\qquad P_{n}\widetilde{Q}_{n}=\widetilde{Q}_{n}P_{n}=-\widetilde{Q}_{n},

(7.2)

where
P_{n}
is the permutation operator defined in Section
3.1
.

Definition 7.1

(
[
28
]
)
.

A matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
is a Manin matrix of the type
B
(for odd
n
) or
D
(for even
n
) if it satisfies

\displaystyle(1-P_{n})M^{(1)}M^{(2)}\bigg(\frac{1+P_{n}}{2}-\frac{Q_{n}}{n}\bigg)=0.

(7.3)

A matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
for even
n
is a Manin matrix of the type
C
if it satisfies

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1-P_{n}}{2}-\frac{\widetilde{Q}_{n}}{n}\bigg)M^{(1)}M^{(2)}(1+P_{n})=0.

(7.4)

Introduce operators
B_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
for the
B
and
D
cases and
\widetilde{B}_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
for the
C
case as

B_{n}=\frac{1-P_{n}}{2}+\frac{Q_{n}}{n}=A_{n}+\frac{Q_{n}}{n},\qquad\widetilde{B}_{n}=\frac{1-P_{n}}{2}-\frac{\widetilde{Q}_{n}}{n}=A_{n}-\frac{\widetilde{Q}_{n}}{n}.

The formulae (
7.1
), (
7.2
) implies that these operators are idempotents. We see that the relations (
7.3
) and (
7.4
) can be written as the definition (
2.20
) by means of the idempotents
A_{n}=\frac{1-P_{n}}{2}
,
B_{n}
and
\widetilde{B}_{n}
.

Proposition 7.2
.

A matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
is a Manin matrix of type
B
or
D
iff it is an
(A_{n},B_{n})
-Manin matrix. A matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n})
is a Manin matrix of type
C
iff it is an
\big(\widetilde{B}_{n},A_{n}\big)
-Manin matrix
(
for even
n)
.

Now let us consider the quadratic algebras related with the idempotents
B_{n}
and
\widetilde{B}_{n}
.

Proposition 7.3
.

The commutation relations for the algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{\widetilde{B}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
are

\displaystyle P_{n}(X\otimes X)=X\otimes X,\qquad Q_{n}(X\otimes X)=0

(7.5)

and

\displaystyle(\Psi\otimes\Psi)P_{n}=-\Psi\otimes\Psi,\qquad(\Psi\otimes\Psi)\widetilde{Q}_{n}=0

(7.6)

respectively.

Proof.

The relation
B_{n}(X\otimes X)=0
has the form

\bigg(\frac{1-P_{n}}{2}+\frac{Q_{n}}{n}\bigg)(X\otimes X)=0.

Multiplication by
Q_{n}
from the left gives
Q_{n}(X\otimes X)=0
, so we derive (
7.5
). Analogously, (
7.6
) can obtained from
(\Psi\otimes\Psi)\big(1-\widetilde{B}_{n}\big)=0
by multiplication by
\widetilde{Q}_{n}
from the right. The converse implications are obvious. ∎

The algebra
