{\mathfrak{X}}_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
is the quotient of the polynomial algebra
{\mathbb{C}}[x^{1},\dots,x^{n}]={\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
by the relation

\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{i}x^{i^{\prime}}=0.

The group of matrices
G\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
preserving the symmetric bilinear form
g_{n}(x,y)=\!\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{i}y^{i^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to
O(n,{\mathbb{C}})
.

The algebra
\Xi_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
is generated by
\lambda,\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
with the relations

\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+\psi_{j}\psi_{i}=\lambda\delta_{i,j^{\prime}}.

Note that
\lambda=\psi_{1}\psi_{n}+\psi_{n}\psi_{1}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}\psi_{i^{\prime}}
is a central element and the Grassmann algebra
\Xi_{A_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
is the quotient of
\Xi_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
by the relation
\lambda=0
(by fixing a non-zero value of
\lambda
we obtain the Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
).

The algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{B}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
is generated by
\widetilde{\lambda},x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
with the relations

x^{i}x^{j}-x^{j}x^{i}=\widetilde{\lambda}\varepsilon_{i}\delta_{i,j^{\prime}},

where
n=2r
. If
n\geqslant 4
then the element
\widetilde{\lambda}=x^{1}x^{n}-x^{n}x^{1}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}(x^{i}x^{i^{\prime}}-x^{i^{\prime}}x^{i})
is central and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{B}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
is the universal enveloping of the
(n+1)
-dimensional Heisenberg Lie algebra (by fixing a non-zero value of
\widetilde{\lambda}
we obtain the Weyl algebra
A_{r}({\mathbb{C}})
).

The algebra
\Xi_{\widetilde{B}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
the quotient of the Grassmann algebra
\Xi_{A_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
with the Grassmann variables
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
by the relation

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\psi_{i}\psi_{i^{\prime}}=0.

Note that
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\psi_{i}\psi_{i^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\psi_{i}\psi_{i^{\prime}}-\psi_{i^{\prime}}\psi_{i})
.
The group of matrices
G\in{\rm GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
preserving the antisymmetric bilinear form
\omega_{r}(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(x_{i}y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{i^{\prime}}x_{i})
is isomorphic to
{\rm Sp}(2r,{\mathbb{C}})
.

These forms of the quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{\widetilde{B}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
give us the relation between the Manin matrices of the types
B
,
C
,
D
and the Manin matrices (of type
A
) considered in Section
3.1
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
.

•

If
M
is an
(A_{n},A_{m})
-Manin matrix, then
M
is an
(A_{n},B_{m})
-Manin matrix and an
\big(\widetilde{B}_{n},A_{m}\big)
-Manin matrix in the same time.

•

If
M
is a
(B_{n},B_{m})
-Manin matrix, then
M
is an
(A_{n},B_{m})
-Manin matrix.

•

If
M
is a
\big(\widetilde{B}_{n},\widetilde{B}_{m}\big)
-Manin matrix, then
M
is a
\big(\widetilde{B}_{n},A_{m}\big)
-Manin matrix.

This proposition follows from Propositions
7.3
and
2.17
. Alternatively one can use the algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{B}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\Xi_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
. One can also prove Proposition
7.4
directly by multiplying the definition (
2.20
) from the left or from the right by an appropriate matrix: for example, multiplication of
\frac{1-P_{n}}{2}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}\frac{1+P_{m}}{2}=0
by the operator
1-\frac{Q_{m}}{m}
from the right gives
\frac{1-P_{n}}{2}M^{(1)}M^{(2)}\big(\frac{1+P_{m}}{2}-\frac{Q_{m}}{m}\big)=0
.

Consider a
2\times 2
matrix
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. It is an
(A_{2},B_{2})
-Manin matrix (a Manin matrix of type
D
) iff
[a,c]=[b,d]=0
. Note that
\widetilde{B}_{2}=0
, so the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{B}_{2}}({\mathbb{C}})
is a free non-commutative algebra with 2 generators (without relations). This implies that any
2\times 2
matrix is an
\big(\widetilde{B}_{2},A_{2}\big)
-Manin matrix (a Manin matrix of type
C
).

Let us generalise the relations (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) to the case of Manin matrices of the types
B
,
C
,
D
.

Proposition 7.5
.

A matrix
M\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
is an
(A_{n},B_{m})
-Manin matrix iff

\displaystyle\big[M^{i}_{k},M^{j}_{l}\big]+\big[M^{i}_{l},M^{j}_{k}\big]=\Lambda^{ij}\delta_{k+l,m+1},\qquad i<j,\quad k\leqslant l,

(7.7)

where
\Lambda^{ij}=\big[M^{i}_{1},M^{j}_{m}\big]+\big[M^{i}_{m},M^{j}_{1}\big]
.
The matrix
M
is a
\big(\widetilde{B}_{n},A_{m}\big)
-Manin matrix iff

\displaystyle\big[M^{i}_{k},M^{j}_{l}\big]+\big[M^{i}_{l},M^{j}_{k}\big]=\widetilde{\Lambda}_{kl}\delta_{i+j,n+1},\qquad i<j,\quad k\leqslant l,

(7.8)

where
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{kl}=\big[M^{1}_{k},M^{n}_{l}\big]+\big[M^{1}_{l},M^{n}_{k}\big]
.

Proof.

We use Proposition
2.17
. Let
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}\in\Xi_{A_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
be the Grassmann variables. We need to subject the new variables
\phi_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}M^{i}_{k}\in\Xi_{A_{n}}({\mathfrak{R}})
to the relations of the algebra
\Xi_{B_{m}}({\mathbb{C}})
, we derive
\phi_{k}\phi_{l}+\phi_{l}\phi_{k}=\lambda\delta_{k+l,m+1}
, where
k\leqslant l
and
\lambda=\phi_{1}\phi_{m}+\phi_{m}\phi_{1}
. Substitution
\phi_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}M^{i}_{k}
and pairing with
\psi^{i}\psi^{j}=-\psi^{j}\psi^{i}\in\Xi^{*}_{A_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})
gives the formula (
7.7
). The relations (
7.8
) is obtained similarly by considering the elements
y_{i}=\sum_{k=1}M^{i}_{k}x^{k}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{m}}({\mathfrak{R}})
, where
x^{k}
are the generators of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{m}}({\mathbb{C}})={\mathbb{C}}[x^{1},\dots,x^{m}]
. ∎

Corollary 7.6
.

The algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A_{n},B_{m}}
is generated by
M^{i}_{k}
,
i=1,\dots,n
,
k=1,\dots,m
, and
\Lambda^{ij}
,
i,j=1,\dots,n
,
i<j
, with the relations (
7.7
).
The algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{\widetilde{B}_{n},A_{m}}
is generated by
M^{i}_{k}
,
i=1,\dots,n
,
k=1,\dots,m
, and
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{kl}
,
k,l=1,\dots,m
,
k\leqslant l
, with the relations (
7.8
).

A Manin matrix of type
B
,
C
or
D
does not always keep so under such operations as taking a submatrix, permutation of rows or columns, doubling of a row or column, a composition of them, but sometimes it does.

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{l})
, where
1\leqslant i_{s}\leqslant n
and
1\leqslant j_{t}\leqslant m
for any
s=1,\dots,k
and
t=1,\dots,l
. Let
M=\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)
be an
n\times m
matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
M_{IJ}
be
k\times l
matrix with entries
(M_{IJ})_{\rm st}=M^{i_{s}}_{j_{t}}
.

•

Let
M
be an
(A_{n},B_{m})
-Manin matrix and
j_{s}+j_{t}\neq m+1
for any
s,t=1,\dots,l
, then
M_{IJ}
is an
(A_{k},A_{l})
-Manin matrix.

•

Let
M
be an
(A_{n},B_{m})
-Manin matrix and
j_{s}+j_{t}=m+1
iff
s+t=l+1

(
this condition implies that
j_{1},\dots,j_{l}
are pairwise different and hence
l\leqslant m)
, then the matrix
M_{IJ}
is an
(A_{k},B_{l})
-Manin matrix.

•

Let
M
be a
\big(\widetilde{B}_{n},A_{m}\big)
-Manin matrix and
i_{s}+i_{t}\neq n+1
for any
s,t=1,\dots,k
, then
M_{IJ}
is an
(A_{k},A_{l})
-Manin matrix.

•

Let
M
be a
\big(\widetilde{B}_{n},A_{m}\big)
-Manin matrix and
i_{s}+i_{t}=n+1
iff
s+t=k+1

(
this condition implies that
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
are pairwise different and hence
k\leqslant n)
, then
M_{IJ}
is a
\big(\widetilde{B}_{k},A_{l}\big)
-Manin matrix.

Finally we give the relation of the Manin matrices of types
B
,
C
and
D
with the Yangians
[
1
,
2
,
28
]
. Let
\mathfrak{g}_{n}=\mathfrak{so}_{n}
for the
B
and
D
cases and
\mathfrak{g}_{n}=\mathfrak{sp}_{n}
for the
C
case. Consider the
R
-matrices

R_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(u)=1-\frac{P_{n}}{u}+\frac{Q_{n}}{u-n/2+1},\qquad R_{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}(u)=1-\frac{P_{n}}{u}+\frac{\widetilde{Q}_{n}}{u-n/2-1}.

The Yangian
Y(\mathfrak{g}_{n})
is an algebra generated by
t^{(r)}_{ij}
,
i,j=1,\dots,n
,
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geqslant 1}
, with the commutation relations

\displaystyle R_{\mathfrak{g}_{n}}(u-v)T^{(1)}(u)T^{(2)}(v)

\displaystyle=T^{(2)}(v)T^{(1)}(u)R_{\mathfrak{g}_{n}}(u-v),

(7.9)

and
T^{\prime}(u)T(u)=1
, where
T(u)=1+\sum_{r\geqslant 1}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}t^{(r)}_{ij}E_{i}{}^{j}\,u^{-r}
,

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(u)=1+\sum_{r\geqslant 1}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}t^{(r)}_{j^{\prime}i^{\prime}}E_{i}{}^{j}\,(u+n/2-1)^{-r}\qquad\text{for}\quad\mathfrak{g}_{n}=\mathfrak{so}_{n},

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(u)=1+\sum_{r\geqslant 1}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}t^{(r)}_{j^{\prime}i^{\prime}}E_{i}{}^{j}\,(u+n/2+1)^{-r}\qquad\text{for}\quad\mathfrak{g}_{n}=\mathfrak{sp}_{n}.

Note that
R_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(-1)=2(1-B_{n})
and
R_{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}(1)=2\widetilde{B}_{n}
, hence by substituting
u=v-1
and
v=u-1
to (
7.9
) we obtain

\displaystyle(1-B_{n})T^{(1)}(v-1)T^{(2)}(v)=T^{(2)}(v)T^{(1)}(v-1)(1-B_{n}),\qquad\text{for}\quad\mathfrak{g}_{n}=\mathfrak{so}_{n},

(7.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{n}T^{(1)}(u)T^{(2)}(u-1)=T^{(2)}(u-1)T^{(1)}(u)\widetilde{B}_{n},\qquad\text{for}\quad\mathfrak{g}_{n}=\mathfrak{sp}_{n}.

(7.11)

By multiplying (
7.10
) by
B_{n}
from the left and by exchanging tensor factors we derive that
M=T(v)e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial v}}
is a
B_{n}
-Manin matrix. Due to Propositions
7.2
and
7.4
this is a Manin matrix of type
B
or
D
.

By multiplying (
7.11
) by
1-\widetilde{B}_{n}
from the right we see that
M=T(u)e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial u}}
is a
\widetilde{B}_{n}
-Manin matrix and hence is a Manin matrix of the type
C
.

7.2
Minor operators for
\boldsymbol{B}
,
\boldsymbol{C}
,
\boldsymbol{D}
cases and Brauer algebras

Remind that the pairing operators
S_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
for the idempotent
A_{n}
are the symmetrizers and anti-symmetrizers of the
k
-th tensor power. We will denote them as
S^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}_{(k)}
and
A^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}_{(k)}
to differ them from other pairing operators. The
A
-minors for a Manin matrix of type
B
or
D
(or, more generally, of an
(A_{n},B_{m})
-Manin matrix) is the column determinants of submatrices. The
S
-minors for a Manin matrix
M
of type
C
(or, more generally, of a
\big(\widetilde{B}_{n},A_{m}\big)
-Manin matrix
M
) are the normalised row permanents
\frac{1}{\nu_{J}}\mathop{\mathrm{perm}}\nolimits(M_{IJ})
(see the formulae (
5.2
) and (
6.12
)).

The
S
-minors for the Manin matrices of types
B
,
D
and the
A
-minors for the Manin matrices of type
C
are given by
k
-th
S
-operators for
B_{n}
and by
k
-th
A
-operators for
\widetilde{B}_{n}
respectively. They can be constructed by the method described in Section
5.7
. In this case the role of the algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{k}
is played by the Brauer algebra
[
3
]
. Invariant idempotents in this algebra were constructed in
[
8
,
13
,
18
,
19
]
.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}
. The Brauer algebra
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)
is an algebra generated by the elements
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k-1}
and
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k-1}
with the relations

\displaystyle\sigma_{a}^{2}=1,\qquad\epsilon_{a}^{2}=\omega\epsilon_{a},\qquad\sigma_{a}\epsilon_{a}=\epsilon_{a}\sigma_{a}=\epsilon_{a},\qquad a=1,\dots,k-1,

(7.12)

\displaystyle\sigma_{a}\sigma_{b}=\sigma_{b}\sigma_{a},\qquad\epsilon_{a}\epsilon_{b}=\epsilon_{b}\epsilon_{a},\qquad\sigma_{a}\epsilon_{b}=\epsilon_{b}\sigma_{a},\qquad|a-b|>1,

(7.13)

\displaystyle\sigma_{a}\sigma_{a+1}\sigma_{a}=\sigma_{a+1}\sigma_{a}\sigma_{a+1},\qquad\epsilon_{a}\epsilon_{a+1}\epsilon_{a}=\epsilon_{a},\qquad\epsilon_{a+1}\epsilon_{a}\epsilon_{a+1}=\epsilon_{a+1},\qquad

\displaystyle\sigma_{a}\epsilon_{a+1}\epsilon_{a}=\sigma_{a+1}\epsilon_{a},\qquad\epsilon_{a+1}\epsilon_{a}\sigma_{a+1}=\epsilon_{a+1}\sigma_{a},\qquad a=1,\dots,k-2.

(7.14)

The subalgebra of
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)
generated by
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k-1}
is naturally identified with the group algebra
{\mathbb{C}}[{\mathbf{S}}_{k}]
. Also, for
b\leqslant k
the subalgebra of
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)
generated by
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{b-1},\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{b-1}
is naturally identified with
{\mathcal{B}}_{b}(\omega)
.

If
\omega
is a positive integer or an even negative integer then the Brauer algebra
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)
has the following representation on a tensor power of a finite-dimensional vector space, which extends the representation
\rho^{+}
or
\rho^{-}
of the symmetric group (see, e.g.,
[
3
,
19
,
31
,
28
]
).

Proposition 7.9
.

The formulae

\displaystyle\rho(\sigma_{a})=P_{n}^{(a,a+1)},\qquad\rho(\epsilon_{a})=Q_{n}^{(a,a+1)},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}(\sigma_{a})=-P_{n}^{(a,a+1)},\qquad\widetilde{\rho}(\epsilon_{a})=-\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(a,a+1)}.

define algebra homomorphisms
\rho\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(n)\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
\widetilde{\rho}\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-n)\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
.

Proof.

The relations for the generators
\sigma_{a}
are valid since
P_{n}
satisfies the braid relation. The relations (
7.12
) follow from (
7.1
), (
7.2
). The relations (
7.13
) are obvious. Further, by direct calculation we obtain

\displaystyle Q_{n}^{(12)}Q_{n}^{(23)}Q_{n}^{(12)}=Q_{n}^{(12)},\qquad P_{n}^{(12)}Q_{n}^{(23)}Q_{n}^{(12)}=P_{n}^{(23)}Q_{n}^{(12)},

(7.15)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(12)}\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(23)}\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(12)}=\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(12)},\qquad P_{n}^{(12)}\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(23)}\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(12)}=-P_{n}^{(23)}\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(12)}.

(7.16)

They give some of the relations (
7.14
). The rest of (
7.14
) is a consequence of the formulae (
7.15
), (
7.16
), (
4.1
),
Q_{n}^{(21)}=Q_{n}^{(12)}
and
\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(21)}=\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(12)}
. ∎

Define an augmentation
\varepsilon\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)\to{\mathbb{C}}
by

\varepsilon(\sigma_{a})=1,\qquad\varepsilon(\epsilon_{a})=0.

The subalgebras
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
of
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
generated by
B_{n}^{(a,a+1)}
and
\widetilde{B}_{n}^{(a,a+1)}
are contained in the images of
\rho
and
\widetilde{\rho}
respectively, but they do not coincide with these images, so we need to check the conditions (
5.78
)–(
5.81
) of Theorem
5.26
. They follow from Proposition
7.3
. Moreover, we have
\varepsilon(u_{a})=0
,
\rho(u_{a})=B_{n}^{(a,a+1)}
and
\widetilde{\rho}(\widetilde{u}_{a})=1-\widetilde{B}_{n}^{(a,a+1)}
for the elements
u_{a}=\frac{1-\sigma_{a}}{2}+\frac{\epsilon_{a}}{\omega}

\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)
, where
\omega=n
and
\omega=-n
for
\rho
and
\widetilde{\rho}
respectively. Hence, if we construct an element
s_{(k)}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)
such that

\displaystyle\sigma_{a}s_{(k)}=s_{(k)}\sigma_{a}=s_{(k)},\qquad\epsilon_{a}s_{(k)}=s_{(k)}\epsilon_{a}=0,\qquad\varepsilon(s_{(k)})=1

(7.17)

for any
a=1,\dots,k-1
we obtain the corresponding pairing operators, namely a
k
-th
S
-operator
S_{(k)}=\rho(s_{(k)})
for
A=B_{n}
and a
k
-th
A
-operator
A_{(k)}=\widetilde{\rho}(s_{(k)})
for
A=\widetilde{B}_{n}
.

Let
\tau_{ab}=\sigma_{a}\sigma_{a+1}\cdots\sigma_{b-1}
,
a<b
. This is the cycle
(a,a+1,\dots,b)\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
. Note that the transpositions have the form
\sigma_{ab}=\sigma_{ba}=\tau_{ab-1}\sigma_{b-1}\tau_{ab-1}^{-1}
. Define
\epsilon_{ab}=\epsilon_{ba}=\tau_{ab-1}\epsilon_{b-1}\tau_{ab-1}^{-1}
. We have
\sigma\sigma_{ab}\sigma^{-1}=\sigma_{\sigma(a)\sigma(b)}
and
\sigma\epsilon_{ab}\sigma^{-1}=\epsilon_{\sigma(a)\sigma(b)}
for any
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
.
These elements can be considered graphically in the following way
[
3
,
28
]
. Remind that an element
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
is presented by a diagram where each number
i
from the top row is connected with
\sigma(i)
from the bottom row. In particular, the diagram corresponding to the element
\sigma_{ab}
is

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{a-1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{b-1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{b+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{a-1}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{a+1}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{b-1}
\textstyle{b}
\textstyle{b+1}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{k}

The element
\epsilon_{ab}
is presented by the diagram

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{a-1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{b-1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{b}
\textstyle{b+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{a-1}
\textstyle{a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a+1}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{b-1}
\textstyle{b}
\textstyle{b+1}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{k}

To multiply two elements one needs to put the corresponding diagrams one over the other and substitute each arising loop by a factor
\omega
. This allows to simplify calculations in the Brauer algebra.

Consider the elements

y_{b}=\sum_{a=1}^{b-1}(\sigma_{ab}-\epsilon_{ab})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{b}(\omega).

These elements (up to a constant term) were introduced in
[
31
]
as analogues of Jucys–Murphy elements for the Brauer algebra. One can check that each element
y_{b}
commute with the subalgebra
{\mathcal{B}}_{b-1}(\omega)
. This implies, in particular, commutativity
[y_{a},y_{b}]=0
(see
[
31
]
for details). The images of the elements
y_{b}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(n)
and
y_{b}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-n)
under the homomorphisms
\rho
and
\widetilde{\rho}
are the matrices

Y_{b}=\rho(y_{b})=\sum_{a=1}^{b-1}\big(P_{n}^{(ab)}-Q_{n}^{(ab)}\big),\qquad\widetilde{Y}_{b}=\widetilde{\rho}(y_{b})=-\sum_{a=1}^{b-1}\big(P_{n}^{(ab)}-\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{(ab)}\big),

respectively.

The following analogue of the symmetrizer can be written in terms of the Jucys–Murphy elements (see
[
8
,
20
,
28
]
):

\displaystyle s_{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{b=2}^{k}\frac{(y_{b}+1)(y_{b}+\omega+b-3)}{2b+\omega-4}.

(7.18)

Proposition 7.10
.

The elements (
7.18
) satisfy the conditions (
7.17
). Hence the matrices

\displaystyle S_{(k)}=S_{(k)}^{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}=\rho(s_{(k)})=\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{b=2}^{k}\frac{(Y_{b}+1)(Y_{b}+n+b-3)}{2b+n-4}\qquad(2\leqslant k),

\displaystyle A_{(k)}=A_{(k)}^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}=\widetilde{\rho}(s_{(k)})=\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{b=2}^{k}\frac{(\widetilde{Y}_{b}+1)(\widetilde{Y}_{b}-n+b-3)}{2b-n-4}\qquad(2\leqslant k\leqslant r+1).

are the
k
-th
S
-operator for
B_{n}
and the
k
-th
A
-operator for
\widetilde{B}_{n}
respectively.

Proof.

By substituting
\varepsilon(y_{b})=b-1
we obtain
\varepsilon(s_{(k)})=1
. Suppose by induction that
\sigma_{a}s_{(k-1)}=s_{(k-1)}
and
\epsilon_{a}s_{(k-1)}=0
for all
a=1,\dots,k-2
. Let
I_{k-1,k}
be the left ideal of
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)
generated by the elements
u-\varepsilon(u)
,
u\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k-1}(\omega)
, then
us_{(k-1)}=0
for any
u\in I_{k-1,k}
. Since
y_{k}
commutes with elements of
{\mathcal{B}}_{k-1}(\omega)
and
s_{(k)}
is proportional to
(y_{k}+\omega+k-3)(y_{k}+1)s_{(k-1)}
we have
\sigma_{a}s_{(k)}=s_{(k)}
and
\epsilon_{a}s_{(k)}=0
for all
a=1,\dots,k-2
. Further, we obtain

\displaystyle\epsilon_{k-1}(y_{k}+\omega+k-3)=\epsilon_{k-1}\sigma_{k-1}-\epsilon_{k-1}^{2}+\epsilon_{k-1}\sum_{a=1}^{k-2}(\sigma_{ak}-\epsilon_{ak})+(\omega+k-3)\epsilon_{k-1}.

(7.19)

Note that
\epsilon_{k-1}\sigma_{k-1}=\epsilon_{k-1}
,
\epsilon_{k-1}^{2}=\omega\epsilon_{k-1}
,
\epsilon_{k-1}\sigma_{ak}=\sigma_{ak}\epsilon_{k-1,a}\in I_{k-1,k}
and
\epsilon_{k-1}\epsilon_{ak}=\epsilon_{k-1}\sigma_{a,k-1}\in\epsilon_{k-1}+I_{k-1,k}
for any
a\leqslant k-2
, so the element (
7.19
) belongs to
I_{k-1,k}
. Hence
\epsilon_{k-1}s_{(k)}
is proportional to the element
\epsilon_{k-1}(y_{k}+\omega+k-3)(y_{k}+1)s_{(k-1)}\in I_{k-1,k}(y_{k}+1)s_{(k-1)}=0
. Analogously, we derive

\displaystyle\sigma_{k-1}(1+y_{k})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k-1}+1-\epsilon_{k-1}+\sum_{a=1}^{k-2}(\sigma_{ak}\sigma_{k-1,a}-\sigma_{k-1}\epsilon_{ak})

\displaystyle\in 1+y_{k}+I_{k-1,k}\sum_{a=1}^{k-2}{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)\epsilon_{ak}.

By using the formulae
\epsilon_{ak}=\sigma_{a,k-1}\epsilon_{k-1}\sigma_{a,k-1}
,
(\sigma_{a,k-1}-1)y_{k}=y_{k}(\sigma_{a,k-1}-1)\in I_{k-1,k}
and the fact that (
7.19
) belongs to
I_{k-1,k}
one yields
\epsilon_{ak}(y_{k}+\omega+k-3)\in I_{k-1,k}
. This implies the inclusion
\sigma_{k-1}(1+y_{k})(y_{k}+\omega+k-3)\in(1+y_{k})(y_{k}+\omega+k-3)+I_{k-1,k}
, so that
\sigma_{k-1}s_{(k)}=s_{(k)}
. Thus
s_{(k)}
is left invariant with respect to
\varepsilon
. Due to Proposition
5.29
we obtain the right invariance of
s_{(k)}
.
The rest follows from Theorem
5.26
and Proposition
7.3
(see the reasoning above). ∎

The element (
7.18
) can be rewritten in the following form
[
18
,
19
]
:

s_{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{1\leqslant a<b\leqslant k}\bigg(1+\frac{\sigma_{ab}}{b-a}-\frac{\epsilon_{ab}}{\omega/2+b-a-1}\bigg),

where the product is calculated in the lexicographic order on the pairs
(a,b)
. By taking representations
\rho\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(n)\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
and
\widetilde{\rho}\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-n)\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)
we obtain the corresponding pairing operators:

S_{(k)}^{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}=\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{1\leqslant a<b\leqslant k}R_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(a-b),\qquad A_{(k)}^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}=\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{1\leqslant a<b\leqslant k}R_{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}(b-a).

In particular,
S_{(2)}^{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}=1-B_{n}
and
A_{(2)}^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}=\widetilde{B}_{n}
.

Remark 7.11
.

One can define another augmentation
\varepsilon^{\prime}\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\omega)\to{\mathbb{C}}
by
\varepsilon^{\prime}(\sigma_{a})=-1
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}(\epsilon_{a})=0
. The corresponding idempotent coincides with the usual anti-symmetrizer
a_{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(-1)^{\sigma}\sigma
since
\sigma_{b}a_{(k)}=-a_{(k)}
,
\epsilon_{b}a_{(k)}=\epsilon_{b}\sigma_{b}a_{(k)}=-\epsilon_{b}a_{(k)}
for all
b=1,\dots,k-1
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}(a_{(k)})=1
. Hence the operators
A^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}_{(k)}=\rho(a_{(k)})
and
S^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}_{(k)}=\widetilde{\rho}(a_{(k)})
define the pairings
\Xi^{*}_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})\times\Xi_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{B}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\mathfrak{X}}_{\widetilde{B}_{n}}^{*}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
respectively. But these pairings are degenerate since the conditions (
5.78
)–(
5.81
) are not valid for
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

Let us calculate the ranks of the pairing operators
S_{(k)}^{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}
and
A_{(k)}^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}
. They are equal to their traces. According to
[
28
, formulae (1.52), (1.72), (1.77)]
the (full) traces of these pairing operators are

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits S_{(k)}^{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}=\frac{n+2k-2}{n+k-2}\;{n+k-2\choose k}=\frac{n+2k-2}{k}\;{n+k-3\choose k-1}\qquad(2\leqslant k),

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits A_{(k)}^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}=(-1)^{k}\;\frac{-n+2k-2}{-n+k-2}\;{-n+k-2\choose k}=\frac{n-2k+2}{k}\;{n+1\choose k-1}\qquad(2\leqslant k\leqslant r+1).

In particular, by substituting
n=2r
and
k=r+1
to the expression for
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits A^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}_{(k)}
we derive
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}_{(k)}=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits A^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}_{(k)}=0
. Hence

A^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}_{(k)}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Xi_{\widetilde{B}_{2r}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=0\qquad\text{for all}\quad k\geqslant r+1.

This explains the inequality for
k
in the formula (
7.10
): for other
k
the operator
A_{(k)}^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}
vanishes (it vanishes even for the last value
k=r+1
). Let us conclude.

Proposition 7.12
.

The dimension of the component
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
is not zero for any
k\geqslant 0
. The dimension of
\Xi_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}
is not zero iff
0\leqslant k\leqslant r
. Namely, we have

\displaystyle\dim{\mathfrak{X}}_{B_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\frac{n+2k-2}{k}\;{n+k-3\choose k-1}\qquad(1\leqslant k),

\displaystyle\dim\Xi_{\widetilde{B}_{n}}({\mathbb{C}})_{k}=\frac{n-2k+2}{k}\;{n+1\choose k-1}\qquad(1\leqslant k\leqslant r).

The minor operators for Manin matrices
M
of the types
B
and
D
have the form

\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{S_{(k)}}(M)=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}S^{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}_{(k)},\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}(M)=A^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)},

\big(A^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}_{(k)}=0
for
k\geqslant n+1\big)
.
For Manin matrices
M
of the type
C
we have

\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{S_{(k)}}(M)=M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}S^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}_{(k)},\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits^{A_{(k)}}(M)=A^{\mathfrak{sp}_{n}}_{(k)}M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)},

(
A^{\mathfrak{sp}_{2r}}_{(k)}=0
for
k\geqslant r+1
)
.

Let us give an example. The bases of the spaces
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B_{2}}({\mathbb{C}})_{2}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B_{2}}^{*}({\mathbb{C}})_{2}
consist of the elements
x^{i}_{(2)}=(x^{i})^{2}
and
x_{i}^{(2)}=(x_{i})^{2}
,
i=1,2
, respectively. The operator
S^{\mathfrak{so}_{2}}_{(2)}=1-B_{2}
defines the pairing
\big\langle x^{j}_{(2)},x_{i}^{(2)}\big\rangle=\delta_{i}^{j}
. Let
y^{i}=\sum_{j=1,2}M^{i}_{j}x^{j}
, then
y^{i}_{(2)}=(y^{i})^{2}
. Since
x^{1}x^{2}=x^{2}x^{1}=0
we have
y^{i}_{(2)}=\sum_{j=1,2}\big(M^{i}_{j}\big)^{2}x^{j}_{(2)}
. Thus the second
S
-minor of a
(B_{2},A_{2})
-Manin matrix
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in the basis
\big(x^{1}_{(2)},x^{2}_{(2)}\big)
has the form

\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}\nolimits_{S_{(2)}}(M)=\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}&b^{2}\\
c^{2}&d^{2}\end{pmatrix}\!.

Remark 7.13
.

The existence of the pairing operators for the idempotents
A=B_{n}
and
A=\widetilde{B}_{n}
can be deduced from Theorem
5.12
(in the case, when
{\mathbb{C}}
is exactly the field of complex numbers). Since
A^{\top}=A
we have isomorphisms
V_{k}\cong\overline{V}_{k}
and
W_{k}\cong\overline{W}_{k}
given by restriction of the map
({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\to\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{n})^{\otimes k}\big)^{*}
,
e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\mapsto e^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
. Under these isomorphisms the natural pairings
V_{k}\times\overline{V}_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
W_{k}\times\overline{W}_{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}
are identified with restrictions of the standard bilinear form
\langle e_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}},e_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\rangle=\delta_{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots\delta_{i_{k}j_{k}}
to the spaces
V_{k}
and
W_{k}
respectively. Since the subspaces
V_{k}
and
W_{k}
are given by linear equations with real coefficients the restrictions of this bilinear form are non-degenerate, so the pairing operators
S_{(k)}
and
A_{(k)}
exist for any
k
. In particular, the pairing
A
-operators for
B_{n}
and the pairing
S
-operators for
\widetilde{B}_{n}
also exist.

Conclusion

It was demonstrated in the works
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
30
,
34
]
as well as in the current paper that the Manin matrices have a lot of applications to integrable systems, representation theory and other fields of mathematics and physics. These results show importance of non-commutative geometry developed by Manin in
[
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
for many questions of mathematics and mathematical physics.

By switching the consideration from
A
-Manin matrices to more general
(A,B)
-Manin matrices we obtain a larger class of useful examples such as the
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrices and the Manin matrices of types
B
,
C
and
D
. In particular, the theory of the
(\widehat{q},\widehat{p})
-Manin matrices gives more complete picture for the
q
-Manin matrices.

It was shown that the tensor notations and usage of idempotents gives a convenient approach to the Manin matrices. In particular, it allows to generalise the notion of minors to the general case. This general theory of minors relates Manin matrices with the representation theory of the symmetric groups and their generalisations such as Hecke and Brauer algebras. This alludes to a possible relation between the theory of Manin matrices and the Schur–Weyl duality.

The left equivalence of idempotents gives us relationship between different considerations of Manin matrices. For example, the minors of
q
-Manin matrices can be considered by means of the
q
-antisymmetrizer arisen from a representation of the symmetric group as well as by using higher idempotents of the Hecke algebra; the left equivalence implies a simple relation between the minor operators constructed in these two different ways. Moreover, left and right equivalences can be used to investigate the corresponding quadratic algebras as it was done in Section
6.3
.

It is also discovered that some Lax matrices with spectral parameter is a type of Manin matrices generalised to the infinite-dimensional case. Namely, we defined an idempotent
\widehat{A}_{n}
acting on a completed tensor power
\big({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\big[u,u^{-1}\big]\big)^{\otimes 2}
and proved that these Lax matrices are exactly Manin operators that acts on the tensor factor
{\mathbb{C}}\big[u,u^{-1}\big]
by a function multiplication.

Appendices

Appendix A
Reduced expression and set of inversions

Let
R
be a finite root system and
W
be the corresponding reflection group in the sense of
[
14
, Chapter 1]
. Let
R^{+}\subset R
be a subset of positive roots. Denote the corresponding simple roots by
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}
, where
r
is the rank of the root system
R
. The simple reflections
s_{i}=s_{\alpha_{i}}
generate the group
W
. The
length
of
w\in W
, denoted by
\ell(w)
, is the minimal
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
such that
w
can be presented as a product of
k
simple reflections. If
\ell=\ell(w)
, then any expression
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{l}}
is called a
reduced expression
for the element
w\in W
.

Note that
\ell(w^{-1})=\ell(w)
. Indeed, since
s_{i}^{2}=1
we have
(s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}})^{-1}=s_{i_{k}}\cdots s_{i_{1}}
, so the element
w
can be presented as a product of
k
simple reflections iff the element
w^{-1}
can be presented as a product of
k
simple reflections.

Let
R^{-}=-R^{+}
be the set of negative roots and let
R_{w}^{+}:=R^{+}\cap w^{-1}R^{-}
. The latter is the set of positive roots
\alpha\in R^{+}
such that
w\alpha\in R^{-}
. As it is proved in
[
14
, Section 1.7]
the number of such roots is equal to the length of
w
, that is
\ell(w)=|R_{w}^{+}|
. Note also that from
-wR_{w}^{+}=(-wR^{+})\cap(-R^{-})=R^{+}\cap wR^{-}
we obtain

\displaystyle R^{+}_{w^{-1}}=-wR^{+}_{w}.

(A.1)

The symmetric group
{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
is a reflection group with the root system
R=\{e_{i}-e_{j}\mid i\neq j\}
of rank
r=n-1
. In this case we have
R^{+}=\{e_{i}-e_{j}\mid i<j\}
,
R^{-}=\{e_{i}-e_{j}\mid i>j\}
,
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
,
s_{i}=\sigma_{i}
. For a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
the set
R^{+}_{\sigma}
is identified with the set of inversions of
\sigma
:

\displaystyle R^{+}_{\sigma}=\{e_{i}-e_{j}\mid i<j,\,\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)\}.

(A.2)

In particular, the number of inversions is equal to the length:
\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}\nolimits(\sigma)=\ell(\sigma)
. Note that the number of elements
\sigma\in{\mathbf{S}}_{k}
with a fixed length
\ell(\sigma)
can be calculated from the generating function (
6.36
).

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\ell(w)=\ell
and
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
be a reduced expression. Then

\displaystyle R^{+}_{w}=\{\alpha_{i_{\ell}},s_{i_{\ell}}\alpha_{i_{\ell-1}},\dots,s_{i_{\ell}}\cdots s_{i_{2}}\alpha_{i_{1}}\}=\{s_{i_{\ell}}\cdots s_{i_{k+1}}\alpha_{i_{k}}\mid k=1,\dots,\ell\},

(A.3)

\displaystyle R^{+}_{w^{-1}}=\{\alpha_{i_{1}},s_{i_{1}}\alpha_{i_{2}},\dots,s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell-1}}\alpha_{i_{\ell}}\}=\{s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}\alpha_{i_{k}}\mid k=1,\dots,\ell\}.

(A.4)

Proof.

First we prove that all the roots
\beta_{k}:=s_{i_{\ell}}\cdots s_{i_{k+1}}\alpha_{i_{k}}
belongs of
R^{+}_{w}
. Note that
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
is a reduced expression for any
k=1,\dots,\ell
, since otherwise
\ell(w)<\ell
. By virtue of
[
14
, Section 1.6]
the equality
\ell(s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}s_{i_{k}})=\ell(s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}})+1
implies

\displaystyle s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}\alpha_{i_{k}}\in R^{+},\qquad k=1,\dots,\ell.

In the same way for the reduced expression
w^{-1}=s_{i_{\ell}}\cdots s_{i_{1}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\beta_{k}=s_{i_{\ell}}\cdots s_{i_{k+1}}\alpha_{i_{k}}\in R^{+},\qquad k=1,\dots,\ell.

Since
w\beta_{k}=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}s_{i_{k}}\alpha_{i_{k}}=-s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}\alpha_{i_{k}}\in R^{-}
we derive
\beta_{k}\in R^{+}_{w}
. Due to the formula
\ell(w)=|R^{+}_{w}|
we only need to prove that the roots
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{\ell}
are pairwise different. We prove it by induction on
\ell
. For
\ell=0
and
\ell=1
there is nothing to check. Let
w^{\prime}=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell-1}}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{k}=s_{i_{\ell-1}}\cdots s_{i_{k+1}}\alpha_{i_{k}}\in R^{+}_{w^{\prime}}
. Due to the induction assumption the roots
\beta^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\beta^{\prime}_{\ell-1}
are pairwise different. Hence
\beta_{k}=s_{i_{\ell}}\beta^{\prime}_{k}
,
k=1,\dots,\ell-1
are also pairwise different. Now suppose that
\beta_{\ell}=\beta_{k}
for some
k=1,\dots,\ell-1
. By acting by
s_{i_{\ell}}
to the both hand sides we obtain
-\alpha_{i_{\ell}}=\beta^{\prime}_{k}
, but the roots
\beta^{\prime}_{k}\in R^{+}_{w^{\prime}}
are positive. This contradiction ends the proof of the formula (
A.3
). The formula (
A.4
) is obtained from (
A.1
) and (
A.3
). ∎

Appendix B
Lie algebras as quadratic algebras

Here we present a quadratic algebra closely related with a finite dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

Let
n=\dim\mathfrak{g}+1
and
\big(x^{1},\dots,x^{n-1}\big)
be a basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with the brackets
[x^{i},x^{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}C^{ij}_{k}x^{k}
,
C^{ij}_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Consider the quadratic algebra with generators
x^{1},\dots,x^{n-1},x^{n}
and relations

\displaystyle x^{i}x^{j}-x^{j}x^{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}C^{ij}_{k}x^{k}x^{n},\qquad i,j=1,\dots,n-1,

(B.1)

\displaystyle x^{i}x^{n}-x^{n}x^{i}=0,\qquad i=1,\dots,n-1.

(B.2)

Let
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
be a matrix with entries

\displaystyle(C_{\mathfrak{g}})^{ij}_{kl}=C^{ij}_{k}\delta_{ln}+C^{ij}_{l}\delta_{kn},\qquad i,j,k,l=1,\dots,n,

(B.3)

where we set
C^{ij}_{n}=C^{in}_{k}=C^{nj}_{k}=0
. Since (
B.3
) is antisymmetric in
i,j
and symmetric in
k,l
we have the formulae

C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{2}=0,\qquad C_{\mathfrak{g}}A_{n}=0\qquad A_{n}C_{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{\mathfrak{g}}.

They imply that the operator
A_{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{n}-\tfrac{1}{4}C_{\mathfrak{g}}
is an idempotent. The relations (
B.1
) and (
B.2
) define the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\mathfrak{g}}}({\mathbb{C}})
.

Remark B.1
.

By fixing a non-zero value of the central element
x^{n}
we obtain the universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
. In other words, the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{\mathfrak{g}}}({\mathbb{C}})
is the quantisation of the algebra
S\mathfrak{g}={\mathbb{C}}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]
with the Lie–Poisson brackets
\{x^{i},x^{j}\}=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}C^{ij}_{k}x^{k}
. The central element
x^{n}
plays the role of the quantisation parameter.
